 WE SING DIANA
 By Wanda Fraiken Neff

 To Nora Deming, growing up in a quiet country town, men, women,
 and life itself were teasing problems. Men were freer than women,
 she thought—less bound by custom, less enslaved by their own
 timidities. At Petersfield, the great women’s college, and in New
 York and London, she found others who like herself led
 half-lives, suppressing their emotions and concealing their
 disillusionment. What was it that women wanted?

 ‘We Sing Diana’ tells the tale of Nora’s search for
 fulfillment,—her career, her activities, her friends, and last
 and greatest of all, her passionate acceptance of love. To many
 women the story will show in a flash of revelation the problems
 and abysses in their own and their friends’ lives hitherto only
 dimly apprehended. To men it will come as a novel glimpse into a
 modern woman’s inner life in a world where men have little part.
 And all its readers, regardless of their sex, will appreciate its
 masculine candor, its feminine intuition, its convincing
 sincerity and above all its narrative power that permits no
 flagging of interest from the first page to the last.


 WANDA FRAIKEN NEFF

 author of ‘We Sing Diana’, began writing for a children’s paper
 connected with the Minneapolis _Journal_ at the age of nine and
 says that she has been covering paper with experimental writing
 ever since. She graduated from the University of Minnesota in
 1909, taught in a wide variety of schools and colleges, ranging
 from Colorado and Wisconsin to New York and Virginia, and
 acquired an M.A. degree at Columbia in 1917.

 She began collecting material for ‘We Sing Diana’ during the
 years 1921-1922, when the American Association of University
 Women sent her to England to study the history of Victorian
 working women. Instructorships at Barnard and at Vassar gave her
 contact with contemporary segregated education of women, and
 marriage in 1925, the necessary leisure, as well as detachment,
 to begin work on this brilliant and convincing story of a woman
 who attempts to find creative satisfaction in intellectual work.

 She is the wife of Professor Emery Neff of Columbia University
 and spends most of her time in New York City, which she knows
 from Harlem to Greenwich Village.


 WE SING DIANA


 WANDA FRAIKEN NEFF

 WE SING DIANA


 HOUGHTON MIFFLIN COMPANY

 CONTENTS


 Part I.  The Neophyte 1

 PART I.
 The Neophyte


 CHAPTER I


 I

Cordelia Tait was thirty-one years old when she read in ‘The Diocesan
News’ that the sisterhood of Saint Agnes had been established in
connection with the cathedral at Kewaukee. She had a thrill of spiritual
exaltation. When mamma died—and every day mamma seemed nearer to that
Place of Preparation which, as nearly as Cordelia could understand her
rector, was somewhat akin to Purgatory—Cordelia would join the
sisterhood. In cases of extreme piety, so she read, the usual age limit
of thirty for novices was stretched to forty. Mamma died six months
before Cordelia’s fortieth birthday. Less than two weeks after her
death, with the ironic clash of events which had characterized all of
Cordelia’s life, she received a letter from the friend of her
boarding-school days, the only real friend she had ever had, a widow
with one child, who had passed the seven years of her widowhood in a
pilgrimage from one health cure to another. Helen Deming, now that the
climate of Colorado could do nothing for her, begged Cordelia to take
care of her little girl. Cordelia read over the letter she had written
to the Bishop during her mother’s last illness, tore it into bits, and
took upon her maidenly shoulders the matronly responsibility of rearing
a child.

When the thirteen-year-old daughter of her old friend arrived, Cordelia
did not know what to do with her. Nora was a spindly, pale girl, with
hair and eyes of a brownish red. Just like a cow, Nora confided in one
of her rare bursts of talkativeness. She wasn’t a common-looking child,
and she didn’t appear difficult to manage.

On the first day, Nora was taken up to Cordelia’s bedroom to inspect her
bureau drawers and closet, which were to serve as models for her own.
Cordelia had decided that the foundation of her young charge’s training
must be neatness. Nora examined the folded bits of ribbon and lace, the
rows of handkerchief boxes, the glove boxes, with encouraging attention.
The perfection of detail, which with women like Cordelia so easily
becomes a passion, was amazing. Nora followed Cordelia to the closet. On
a shelf near the floor were assembled the battalions of Cordelia’s
shoes. Her beautiful feet were her only vanity. She wore number twos,
triple A, made to order by a Maronac bootmaker, who took great pride in
his work. Since they were expensive, they were patched and repatched,
with an infinity of patches, and the oldest dated back before Nora’s
birth.

‘Now,’ concluded Cordelia impressively, ‘if you arrange your belongings
in the same way, you can get up in the dead of night and find any
article you want.’

‘But I never want to find anything in the dead of night,’ Nora
commented, with the flicker of a smile lighting up her solemn young
face.

Cordelia, undaunted, went on delivering her homily on the orderly care
of places hidden from the common eye as a necessity of decent living.
She had great veneration for the unseen corners of the world. She
confessed that after she went to bed at night, if she remembered that
she had neglected to place her shoes neatly side by side, she couldn’t
go to sleep without getting up to arrange them in the proper order.

All the time she was talking, Nora reflected on the possibility of a
fairy prince’s finding one of Cordelia’s shoes on the streets of Maronac
and thinking Cinderella had come to life again. But after several rigid
days under Cordelia’s régime, she decided that her guardian would never
follow Cinderella’s careless fashion of dropping her slipper.

A few more days in Maronac made her doubt the existence of fairy princes
there—or even ordinary men. At least none came near Cordelia. Every one
who visited her was a woman, old like her, generally unmarried or long
widowed. Young wives walking arm in arm with their husbands, happy
mothers with rosy-cheeked little boys and girls skipping beside them,
passed by the brown dwelling standing close to the street, its row of
front windows neatly shuttered, the shades lowered to the nicety of an
inch below the upper sash, the white curtains looped back in a geometric
curve. And yet Cordelia seemed to love children. When Nora accompanied
her to the shops on Main Street, Cordelia always stopped to examine the
babies left outside in their carriages. Both Cordelia and Nora, as only
children, had been denied all intimate experience with that most
clamorously egoistic creation of our planet, the young of the human
species, so that they saw none of the prose characterizing the chubby
infants over whom both of them cooed rapturous endearments.

Everything at Cordelia’s house was strange. Cordelia herself was totally
unlike Nora’s mother, only three years younger, who had kept the bright
ways of her youth until the end, in spite of widowhood and shattered
health. Cordelia, dressed in deep mourning, which increased the
sallowness of her face and the deep circles under eyes which were a
greyish brown behind thick-rimmed spectacles, following an endless line
of what she called duty from one day to the next, correct, reserved,
made no appeal to the riotous emotions which leaped underneath Nora’s
shy and quiet exterior. Nora’s childhood home—of which she had a vivid
memory although it had been broken up when she was only six—with its
erratic ways, the uncertain comings and goings, the disorder,
processions of friends and family visitings which had belonged to a
happy, shadowy past, would have driven Cordelia to distraction.

But the girls at school soon became Nora’s friends, and the strangeness
of her transplanted existence passed away. With the adaptability of the
young, she settled down in her new home. From the first she had loved
Maronac, lying close to the blue stretches of Lake Michigan, with the
sprawling car-ferries and passenger boats plying in and out of the
river, and whistles, bells, foghorns, the cries of seagulls, the gentle
dashing of the waves or beating storms so intimate a part of the life of
the town. She especially enjoyed the walk to her school across the
river, reached by passing through Main Street and over the bridge. She
read the German signs which hung below those in English on the various
business houses. Maronac, like so many towns in Wisconsin, had a large
Teutonic population. When she heard the bell warning pedestrians that
the drawbridge would open for one of the lake boats, she ran and leaped
across before the third bell sounded. Then she stood on the other side
watching the big boat slide past her, the men waving their hats to the
crowds on the bridge. Often she stopped to talk to the fat old German
bridge-tender. After she informed him that she was beginning to study
his language, he greeted her, ‘Guten Morgen, schöne Mädchen’; to which
she responded, ‘Guten Morgen, mein Freund.’

The chief advantage of Cordelia’s house was that it was only a block
from the public library. Every Saturday, while Cordelia worked at Saint
Margaret’s, Nora read in the library, returning late in the afternoon
with volumes tucked under her arm to read that night and on Sunday
during Cordelia’s succession of devotions.

Cordelia, busy with her church work, left Nora much to herself. Although
her ambition to serve in Saint Agnes’ had been snatched from her, the
Bishop’s High-Church reforms in the Kewaukee diocese had given her
plenty to do in her own parish.

After a visit to England in the year 1900, Bishop Welling had returned
to his see in knee breeches, gaiters, and a tall silk hat with a broad
brim turned up elaborately at the sides and laced with silk cords. He
was, in Scriptural phrase, like a man born again. And before all his
innovations of genuflections, holy water, the reserved sacrament, and
the ceremonial of the true Holy Catholic Church had been established,
his locks, already touched with grey, spread over his shoulders in a
fashion resembling the later portraits of the poet Milton. He had now
advanced beyond even an English model. The Bishop was a good man, much
beloved by both the clergy and the lay members of his diocese. He was
young enough to retain the necessary physical vigor for conflict, and
old enough to realize that, if he kept his temper and steadily imported
a succession of earnest young English clergymen with the correct views,
he would live to see his hitherto barren Low Church pastures blossom
with the beauty of the true faith.

At Saint Margaret’s a sisterhood had developed, bound by no vows, but in
its informal and voluntary way humbly following the Bishop’s new light.
As senior member of the Altar Guild, Cordelia had the responsibility
every week-day of preparing the altar for early communion. For the
Sunday services she was assisted by women, all of them unmarried, who
were not kept sufficiently busy at home. Every Saturday they scoured the
brass candlesticks and the flower vases, preserved the spotless purity
of the white marble altar, spread out the linen cloths, arranged the
flowers, set the holy vessels in the proper array, laid out the
vestments. They stopped in their labors for a lunch in the Guild Room
downstairs, and sometimes did not finish until late in the afternoon. So
has religion provided, since the beginning, an occupation for its vestal
virgins, who might otherwise grow weary in a world which has steadily
pushed them aside.

Sometimes Nora stopped at the church on her way from the library. She
sat watching the queer observances of the Altar Guild until Cordelia was
ready to go home with her. The women performed their work with covered
heads, Cordelia wearing a lace cap which had belonged to her mother.
Every time they passed the Cross, they dropped down quickly on one knee
and crossed themselves. Nora learned that the water which had refreshed
the altar flowers and that which had cleansed the communion linen and
the priestly robes must be thrown upon the ground, to pass away
according to nature’s formula.

She often went to high mass with Cordelia. Brought up in the unadorned
Calvinistic faith, she was fascinated by the ceremonial of Saint
Margaret’s. When the procession of choir boys passed down the central
aisle, preceded by the Cross, she closed her eyes quickly to hide the
tears.


 II

Cordelia had invited the Altar Guild to have the refreshment of tea at
her house after the hard Saturday labor, and Nora, now a girl of
sixteen, was helping her pass the cups. All of Cordelia’s regular
co-workers were on hand: Della Schuster, a red-cheeked woman of thirty
from the south side of town; Amy Henderson, in high school with Nora,
but soon to relinquish her church duties for mundane employment in the
grocery department of Weyhausen’s store; and Edna Tillotson, tolerated
because of her real gift for arranging flowers, and in spite of her
unreliability and her coarse jokes.

Since the coming of the new rector, Father Stokesbury, another of the
Bishop’s young English importations, tall, handsome, and unmarried,
Cordelia had found the Guild burning with a new zeal. Saint Margaret’s,
at least the feminine portion of it, was once more seeking salvation.

To-day Edna’s taste in humor displayed itself in a rapid census of the
young worshippers at Saint Margaret’s who were beginning to come out in
large numbers to the early service.

‘Poor young man!’ she began with the mimicry which her enemies found so
distasteful. ‘It’s so sad him living all alone at the rectory.’ Then the
real Edna emerged. ‘I wonder which one o’ them’ll catch him.’

‘I hope he won’t marry in the parish,’ Cordelia said.

‘He might be doin’ charity—makin’ one less old maid in Saint
Margaret’s,’ Edna cackled. Edna always laughed at her own jokes so
lustily that she covered any possible awkward pauses.

Before the echoes of her mirth had subsided, the bell rang, a loud,
determined peal. Nora was dispatched to the door. When she opened it,
the new rector stood facing her.

‘Miss Tait—does she live here?’ He looked down at Nora a little
uncertainly.

Cordelia’s front hall was a place which few men had invaded. The corners
of the rug showed no traces of having been kicked up by a rude masculine
foot. There was an unsullied freshness to the stair carpet. The silver
tray for visitors’ cards on the mahogany table, with a fine old mirror
hanging above it, had a nicety that only a well-trained man would have
respected. And, final proof of all, the hall-tree in the corner bore no
confused burden of hats and coats which all men alike have an
unreasonable prejudice against hiding away in a dark closet.

Father Stokesbury took several steps forward, twisted the light rug in
front of the door, and covered the card-table with his broad-brimmed
black hat.

In the sitting-room there was a clatter of china as the women rose, like
a startled flock of pigeons, at the rector’s appearance.

‘I hoped I’d be in time for tea,’ he said, shaking hands with Cordelia.

‘I think you know every one here.’ Cordelia beamed on her little flock.

The rector looked in Nora’s direction. ‘Except this young lady.’

‘Nora Deming, who lives with me. Her father was a Presbyterian.’

‘A heretic in our midst.’

Nora blushed with the vivid memory of her apostasy these three years she
had lived with Cordelia and attended Saint Margaret’s much oftener than
the Presbyterian church across the street. Of late she had been a
frequent visitor. The flicker of the candles, the chanting of the choir
boys, the rhythm of the prayers, harmonized with the dreamy world in
which she found herself at sixteen. And what made this flush of shame
continue to burn in her pale face was the consciousness that she
followed Cordelia to Saint Margaret’s to watch Stanley Morton, who
carried the Cross in the procession of high mass. Something in his blue
eyes raised to the holy burden he carried stirred her—like the colored
reproductions of Raphael and Andrea del Sarto in the books she searched
for in the library. When she met Stanley in everyday garments on the
street, she lowered her eyes and passed quickly, so that she would not
lose her vision.

‘We may get her confirmed yet,’ the new rector was saying hopefully, as
he settled in the Sleepy Hollow chair Edna had vacated for him.

Nora, unaccustomed to the jocular fashion in men, responded with a slow
smile. She felt a warm drawing power in this stranger with the dancing
young eyes. When she passed him his cup of tea, she volunteered shyly,
‘That’s the Bishop’s seat you’re in.’

He looked under him with a great show of concern. ‘Does that mean I
shall be bishop some day?’ he asked her, spreading out his hands over
his shoulders, where a bishop’s locks might one day be resting.

‘Nora calls it the Bishop’s seat because Bishop Welling always picks out
that old chair when he comes here,’ Cordelia explained.

Father Stokesbury made a rapid survey of the room, his gaze passing from
the picture of Queen Victoria and all her grandchildren on to the view
of Warwick Castle and then to the corner cupboard with its precious
Lowestoft china of a lovely blue, which Nora was never trusted to dust.

‘This room makes me feel perfectly at home,’ he said.

‘My parents were born in England,’ Cordelia stated proudly. ‘Yes, near
Warwick. Both of them.’ Cordelia was now launched upon her favorite
topic. ‘I’ve always felt as if I came from there too. I use my mother’s
old English cook book. I’ve never had another.’

‘Some say the English are the most abominable cooks in the world,’ he
declared solemnly. Cordelia could only look incredulous surprise. ‘We
have a national prejudice in favor of cabbage and its more aristocratic
cousin. Candidly, I loathe the whole cabbage tribe. I came to America to
get fed properly. Now your ices—I anticipate a whole summer of ices. And
tomatoes—a great delicacy—I’m told they’re cheap here. And peaches all
summer. Cheaper than potatoes. Fancy it. In London I’ve seen hot-house
peaches sold for two shillings each.’

Nora leaned forward in her chair. Were all men—that part of the world so
mysterious, so compelling and strange to her—like Father Stokesbury? The
gleam of fun on the surface, so akin to the smothered amusement at life
she buried under layer after layer of shyness and fear—it was a rare joy
to find it in Maronac—to find it anywhere. Sitting in the room cluttered
with the tea-drinking Altar Guild, Nora saw only Father Stokesbury, her
brown eyes deep red, then black with this novel discovery of kinship,
like a timid young bird exulting in the first consciousness of wings
that flew. Father Stokesbury turned toward her, their eyes met, and she
looked quickly away.

Again the doorbell broke upon the tea-drinking; the alien Plant girls
were introduced into the sanctities of Saint Margaret’s. They were, like
Nora, Presbyterians. Julia Plant, a large, almost fierce-looking woman,
was so shy that she never visited any one but Cordelia. At the sight of
the new rector, she assumed an expression even more severe, to conceal
her terror of strangers.

Father Stokesbury examined the visitors critically. ‘I can’t understand
why the heretics gather at your house, Miss Tait.’

Once more Nora raised her frightened eyes. Yes, he belonged to her
world. There was no doubt of it.

The Plant girls looked mystified. But Esther, disciplined to face masses
of human beings as a teacher of music and director of the Presbyterian
choir, had no awe of even an Episcopal clergyman. She began to tell them
about the return to town of Willy Blodgett.

‘I remember how fat he was,’ Cordelia said. ‘Always eating during school
hours. He brought a huge lunch to eat at recess every day—liver sausage
sandwiches, pie, bananas, and I don’t know what all. He was sticking his
head in his desk to pick at it.’

‘He’s still fat. He came out to choir practice this week.’ Esther
directed her remarks to the minister, a properly adjusted company smile
on her face. ‘Do you know his voice never changed? It’s a beautiful
soprano—high and clear. Just like a woman.’

‘Really,’ commented Father Stokesbury.

‘There’s Mrs. Bates. She sings just like a man—an octave lower,’
Cordelia said.

‘He wants to sing in the chorus, and when he starts singing, the row of
sopranos just shakes.’

‘We need a good soprano at Saint Margaret’s,’ the rector said. ‘But
every useful person in Maronac seems to be Presbyterian.’

Amy Henderson, who was a militant Episcopalian, shook her head
disapprovingly. ‘He’s too big and fat for a choir boy,’ she asserted
with emphatic conviction. ‘And he’d sound queer with the men. It
wouldn’t do.’

‘Do you remember that concert we went to years ago?’ Cordelia asked
Julia. ‘Willy sang a solo. I got to laughing so——’ Cordelia pulled off
her glasses and began to wipe her eyes at the memory. ‘That big fat man
and then that high voice coming out—it was too much for me. And then I
looked over at you, Julia, and you were just shaking. When you saw me,
you went right off.’

‘Willy can’t even shave,’ Edna Tillotson whispered to Della so loud that
every one in the room heard her.

‘Poor man,’ Julia remarked. ‘He wouldn’t hurt a fly. Not like some men.
Those stabbers——’ Julia emerged from her silence with a trumpet blast.

‘Stabbers?’ All eyes were fastened upon poor, frightened Julia.

‘I was coming home from the concert the other night.’ Julia directed her
narrative exclusively to Cordelia, almost turning her back upon Father
Stokesbury. Julia feared men even more than women. ‘Over at Turner Hall.
Esther was staying afterward, so I went on alone. You know how dark it
is there on Sixth Street close to the bridge.’ In the excitement of her
tale, her threatening eyes included Father Stokesbury, who was polite
attention. ‘A man was behind me.’

Amy Henderson, who was very timid about going out alone at night, gave a
little shriek.

‘One of those stabbers,’ Julia pronounced in her most sepulchral tones.

‘What?’

‘Stabbers. It’s like a dagger, I guess. They stick it into people.’

Father Stokesbury, sunk into the comfortable folds of the Sleepy Hollow
chair, sat up suddenly.

‘They stab women,’ Julia explained. ‘This man—I heard him walking faster
and faster. Then he came right up. I felt I was going to scream. He had
one arm doubled up.’

‘What did he do?’ the Altar Guild demanded in a single voice.

‘I had my door key in my hand. I always carry it that way. I was all
ready to punch it in his eye. I swung out my fist——’

‘Julia Plant!’ breathed Cordelia in an outburst of admiration.

‘Just then two women turned sudden at the corner of Jefferson Street. He
saw them an’ just made off for the docks. An’ they said to me, “We
three’d better stick together. That man was one o’ those stabbers.”’

‘Have they stabbed any one?’ Father Stokesbury inquired politely.

At his question Nora was seized with hysterical giggles, but Julia’s
audience was too intent upon her reply to notice.

‘Not on this side o’ town. But over on the south side. Not far from you,
Della. An’ they’ll be here next. If that man hadn’t got scared off——’

‘Did you see his dagger?’ Father Stokesbury inquired again.

Nora, partially recovered from her paroxysm, looked toward him. His
mouth was very grave, but his eyes had a twitching uncertainty.

‘Of course not. They keep ’em hidden. The first thing you know it’s
sticking into you.’

The rector’s face disappeared behind a large handkerchief with the swift
eclipse of the moon by a stormy cloud. From behind it Nora detected
sounds suspiciously similar to a series of gurgling gasps.

‘They stab young girls, generally,’ Julia concluded.

‘Of course,’ Edna Tillotson sniffed. ‘Who wouldn’t know that?’ The
ignorance of her feminine circle at times was more than Edna could
stand. ‘You don’t think a stabber would bother an old bird like me, do
you, Julia? I don’t believe that man near the bridge was a stabber.’

Father Stokesbury rose abruptly and made a swift departure. Nora
observed that his face and his ears were very red. Cordelia followed him
to the front door, replaced the card tray, and bent down to straighten
the rug before she rejoined her visitors. A twinge of pain shot through
her knees. At forty-three she was lame and stiff after a day spent
working at the church.

‘He don’t seem like a clergyman,’ Edna was saying when she returned.
‘Not enough dignity. I saw him snickerin’ away when Julia was talking
about that stabber. Of course a man with his collar buttoned behind—he
ain’t any different from the rest o’ them.’ Edna, who had no particular
esteem for mankind, never lost an opportunity to state her opinion about
the latest erring representative.

An angry flush rose in Cordelia’s dark cheeks. ‘I hope you aren’t like
some folks, Edna, and begin criticizing a young man before he’s had a
fair trial.’

Nora, uncomfortably aware of the storm raging in Cordelia, began to
gather up the tea-cups. In a moment the Guild and the Plant girls
hurried away. Nora stood in the middle of the sitting-room, once more
seized with helpless laughter.

‘What does _ail_ you, Nora?’

‘They’re so funny. I——’

‘Well, I don’t see anything to laugh at. You wash the tea-things while
I’m getting supper, Nora. It’s late. Most six o’clock.’

Nora, in the little wash-room adjoining the kitchen, went through the
mechanical process of washing the tea-cups, her mind rioting over the
comedy of the last moments. Father Stokesbury had laughed too—laughed as
hard as she. He was probably laughing now. She could imagine him walking
home along the quiet street leading to the rectory in zigzag steps of
overpowering mirth. Nora bent over the dishpan laughing all over.

‘Supper,’ called Cordelia. ‘Come, Nora.’


 III

Old Mrs. Hill, the wife of a retired captain on one of the lake boats,
came to Cordelia whenever the consciousness of her burdens was too
intolerable to be borne at home.

Close upon supper she arrived, as usual. ‘I just had to get away,’ she
began; ‘Mr. Hill’s been under foot all day, and I got that nervous——’

Mrs. Hill had the sagged face and mournful expression of one who has no
doubts concerning her own oppression. ‘No one comes to see me,’ she
complained. ‘Even you, Cordelia. I wonder what your poor dead mother
would say if she knew how you were neglecting old friends.’

‘Sometimes I get tired of people,’ Cordelia admitted when she had closed
the front door upon Mrs. Hill’s grumblings.

‘I feel sorry for old Captain Hill,’ Nora retorted. ‘He’s lonesome too.
After being a boat captain—and now sitting at home. The next time she
comes, I’m going over to talk with him.’

The weary Cordelia was beginning to close the house for the night. In a
feminine household this is an elaborate ceremony of precaution. First,
all the windows downstairs were fastened. Then, Cordelia bolted the
cellar door which led into the back hall, next the front and the back
doors, and then in succession the kitchen, the sitting-room, and the
dining-room doors which opened into the back hall. At last she and Nora
started upstairs for bed.

Nora followed Cordelia into her room. She didn’t want to go to bed. She
wanted to talk to some one. She stood staring vacantly at the
photographs of Cordelia’s parents, the bearded man and the very
cross-looking woman, which had never changed their position on the
bureau. Then her glance passed to the crucifix of black and silver over
the table beside the bed and the photograph of Bishop Welling in his
silken robes and apron with the beautiful lace.

Cordelia, with the evening service to be read before she could close her
eyes, began to turn the pages of her prayer-book. ‘Don’t forget to bolt
your door, Nora,’ she said, looking up over her thick spectacles. ‘With
two lone women in the house, we can’t be too careful.’

‘Yes, I’ll remember,’ Nora promised, shaking hands according to a stiff
little ceremony she had inaugurated when she first came. Somehow it
wasn’t easy to kiss Cordelia.

She obediently locked herself into her room and slowly prepared for bed.
From across the hall came the murmur of Cordelia’s prayers. In bed at
last, she reached for the book which that morning she had hidden under
her pillow. It was ‘Tom Jones,’ and she had been repeating the trite
experience of bold maidens who have fallen in love with that sprightly
and adventurous hero.

She had excused her late vigils over the book—it was dreadfully long—by
the necessity of finding escape from the life she led at Cordelia’s. All
those women—their gossip and maliciousness—nothing happening. In ‘Tom
Jones’ something was always happening.

Cordelia had given her ‘Cranford’ to read, but a few chapters smothered
her. She got boys’ books from the library—‘The Count of Monte Cristo’
and ‘Sherlock Holmes’ and ‘Treasure Island.’ Scott, if you skipped a
lot, wasn’t bad. But ‘Cranford’—worse than Louisa Alcott. The open
fields of Fielding brought refreshment, the inns, the highwaymen, Tom’s
fights, the dens of wickedness in London. Nora, sitting propped up in
bed, forgot her smarting, sleep-laden eyes, her cramped arms and legs.
How unmoral and unchurchly and unfeminine! Few women. And those not the
women found in the parish of Saint Margaret’s.

But to-night Tom and his adventures seemed less satisfactory—a little
low. Her thoughts—Nora dropped her book.

When you were old like Cordelia, you thought about being good, you did
piles of church work, and maybe dreamed about the joys of heaven. But if
you were young—it was shameful, but young people—at least some of
them—how did she know? Did the other girls at school, Nora pondered,
think about—about love and about boys who never even looked at them?
Like Nora gazing wistfully after Stanley every Sunday. But it wasn’t
Stanley. What was it then? And boys at school—Tom Baldwin who played
football and wore a bright crimson sweater—Nora had prayed he would
speak to her some day—actually prayed. Then she had put herself in his
way. But he was always with a crowd of boys. He didn’t even see her. And
the boys she had danced with at the church parties Cordelia took her to
in the Guild Hall—they weren’t exactly—not what one dreamed of—but——

Now, suddenly, a tall blond man with a lilting voice which went up and
down displaced Stanley and Tom Baldwin and all the other clumsy boys.
Father Stokesbury had none of the uncouthness of Maronac Harbor—the way
he had helped her pass the cups—he was a gentleman. Somehow he revived
in her the faint memories of her father, about whom her mother had
talked ceaselessly until the end. Her mother had wanted her to marry a
man just like her father. The careless ease and assurance the new rector
had shown. Mere boys—how she despised them! Her dreams were concrete,
swift, charming the uneventfulness of Maronac Harbor with new colors and
a fresh breath. Nora fell asleep, with ‘Tom Jones’ lying spread out on
the floor beside her.


 CHAPTER II


 I

Nora always thought of Sunday as Cordelia’s day. She rose very early, on
dark, cold Sunday mornings, dewy dawns in midsummer, clicked about the
house in her neat Sunday shoes, and hurried over to the church to attend
to the last rite for the early communion service. At breakfast, with
Della Schuster from the other side of town a frequent guest, Cordelia
was generally abstracted. A few housekeeping duties held her until it
was time for the ten o’clock service. Then home once more. When she
covered her black taffeta dress with a large apron and began to get
dinner, Nora was conscious of the irritation Cordelia never quite
succeeded in concealing. But Nora had to be fed on Sundays as well as on
week days.

On the Sunday following the rector’s call, Cordelia stretched out on the
couch after dinner to read ‘The Churchman’ until she fell into a doze.

‘Now if Lizzie Guildford leaves me in peace,’ she sighed to Nora, with
forebodings concerning Lizzie, who had edged toward her after service.
Ever since Father Stokesbury’s arrival, she had been hinting that her
services in the Altar Guild were free for the asking.

Lizzie arrived just as ‘The Churchman’ dropped from Cordelia’s limp
fingers. She entered the room with a slight premonitory cough, glanced
at herself in the pier glass between the two south windows, and sat
down, smoothing out a light dress of summer silk, much too gay for a
woman of fifty in Cordelia’s opinion, with her ringed hands. She was the
richest woman in Maronac Harbor, living alone in the large house with
the mansard roof and the high tower which occupied a whole block on Main
Street, built by her father with some of the profits from cheating the
early settlers in the region.

‘Have you had Father Stokesbury to supper yet?’ Lizzie opened fire at
once in her subdued, ladylike tones.

‘No, I haven’t.’ Cordelia could be brutally direct with people she
disliked.

‘I think we’re going to like him. Don’t you?’ Lizzie coughed again.

‘I think so.’

‘He’s young, but really spiritual-minded. He’s so eager for all of us to
work hard for Saint Margaret’s. Of course I do what I can.’

 * * * * *

‘I won’t have her!’ Cordelia stormed afterward to Nora. ‘She can’t come
in Amy’s place. I’ll lie—I’ll tell her I’ve got some one else.’

‘Of course,’ Nora agreed easily. ‘I have to lie most every day.’

‘Why, Nora Deming!’

Nora, glancing at the clock, reminded Cordelia that it was time to start
for Della Schuster’s, where they had been invited to supper.

Della lived on the south side of town in the German settlement. Her
mother, scorning the generation of her countrymen who had spread over
the American settlement of the north side, taking their German
beer-gardens with them, clung to her old-country ways and her cheerful
orange cottage in a street of blue, orange, and green houses with their
gay gardens.

At the river, Cordelia and Nora were delayed by the opening of the
drawbridge for one of the early passenger boats that had begun making
regular trips across the lake. When the rush of traffic was resumed, two
comfortable German citizens barred the sidewalk in front of them. The
women took to the street, Cordelia much enraged.

‘They treat women like cows,’ she blustered.

‘Hausfrauen,’ Nora corrected her. When she was studying European
history, she had interviewed the baker from whom Cordelia bought
Kaffeekuchen on the subject of compulsory military training, from which
he had escaped. But he had added laments for the cheap opera of his
native land, the good music in the beer-gardens, the annual song-fests,
and the submissiveness of German women. Even a good German lady got
demoralized on American soil. And as for the natives themselves—Nora now
understood perfectly the attitude of the Teutonic male toward women.

Della’s mother had further enlightened her. ‘Big young lady,’ she always
began. ‘Sixteen now. You get a fella yet?’ Nora shook her head. ‘You
don’t want to be old maid like Miss Tait. A good-lookin’ girl like you.
You watch out for nice fella. My Della’—Mrs. Schuster made a discouraged
cluck in her throat—’she won’t go to German church. A German fella—she
don’t want none. She gets left, I tell her.’

‘Not all girls want to get married,’ Nora volunteered.

Mrs. Schuster’s face spread into a wide toothless smile. ‘Ah, now,’ she
laughed. ‘Not want to. My Gott!’

Della was waiting for them in front of the house, prepared to display
her garden. It was one of the prettiest Nora had ever seen, like a big
fragrant nosegay, all colors, all kinds of sweet scents in an orderly
profusion of bloom from early spring until frost. Most of the flowers
for the church came from Della’s garden.

Mrs. Schuster, a big, comfortable woman, waddled to the front door.
‘Vell, vell,’ she greeted Nora with her usual formula, ‘how the little
one has grown.’

The house, crowded with rocking-chairs and ornaments, the sofa, piled
with cushions heavily embroidered with flowers and appropriate
sentiments by Della, kept the two women busy all of the time. Della,
with her garden, the house-work, and the Altar Guild, had solved better
than Lizzie Guildford the problem of the disposal of time. But her
mother scolded steadily at an old-maid daughter in the house, and
complained that Della left nothing for her to do.

Cordelia and Della settled themselves among the embroidered daisies,
roses, and sunflowers on the sofa and were soon launched upon a lengthy
discussion of their church work. Nora followed Mrs. Schuster out to the
dining-room, were the parrot was. He was a clever bird who could not
only repeat a number of words, but could even sing ‘My Old Kentucky
Home.’ Mrs. Schuster was now teaching him a little German.

When Nora stood in front of his cage, the bird winked at her solemnly.
‘He talks more than any parrot in town,’ she declared, turning toward
Mrs. Schuster.

‘Old maid—old maid—Della old maid,’ shrieked the parrot.

Mrs. Schuster laughed until her little blue eyes disappeared, and her
nose, like one of her delicious raised doughnuts, shook up and down.
‘That’s my teachin’ him,’ she admitted. ‘Della—old maid with parrot, I
tell her. Yes, he talks pretty goot.’

After Mrs. Schuster had pressed the last of a succession of cold ham,
pickles, cake, jelly, and cookies relentlessly upon her guests, Cordelia
had to hurry away to the evening service.

‘It’s like you was married to that church,’ Mrs. Schuster said at
parting. ‘My Della the same way.’

‘Old maid—old maid—Della old maid,’ the parrot called after them.

Nora surprised Cordelia by offering to go to evensong with her. She
liked to watch Father Stokesbury in the dimness of the altar, his face
ruddy in the light from the lectern against the snowy whiteness of his
surplice. She felt a little wicked as she observed Cordelia’s
absorption, her fervent breathing of the prayers, her knees set firmly
on the hard, uncarpeted floor.


 II

With the passing of the summer Nora discovered one day that Cordelia was
beginning to seem less old. She had relaxed the perpetual activity
crowded into hours which might easily have been empty, and sat down
sometimes without even a piece of sewing to occupy her, rocking back and
forth, staring in front of her. One hot afternoon in July she came
downstairs wearing a thin white lawn dress with the short sleeves and
neck cut out a little, revealing shapely arms and a firm white throat.

‘Do I look silly, Nora? It’s so warm. Of course the dress is out of
style. I haven’t worn it since the summer before mamma died.’

The rector had formed the habit of stopping for a cup of tea at
Cordelia’s whenever he happened to be calling in the vicinity. He
confessed to hurrying through his calls on the south side of town to be
back in time to abuse Cordelia’s hospitality. He told Nora it was Miss
Tait’s English blood which made her so kind to him.

‘Now you, Miss Nora, you run upstairs to read a novel when a lonely man
wants to be amused.’

There was the exquisite torture of seeing Father Stokesbury, and then
the awful moment when Nora realized she was unable to say a word. She
just had to run upstairs and hide herself in her room. There,
undisturbed by the necessity of making conversation, she could revive in
her memory the delightful smile which shone all over his face, even
though the hum of voices below stabbed her like pain.

Once, when she had stumbled toward the door which offered escape, Father
Stokesbury had followed her and said in a tantalizingly pleading tone,
‘Come, Miss Nora, please stay and talk to me.’

She could hardly get upstairs. She was trembling all over when she
reached her little carpet rocking-chair near the window. Did he care?
Did he want her to stay? But if the sight of her stirred him as the
sound of his voice her——No, he couldn’t joke that way. He, too, would be
dumb. Why should he care? He never saw her as she really was, capable of
amusing talk, full of smiles, free and easy. He saw only an awkward
schoolgirl twisting her icy hands in front of her, with not a word to
say. He didn’t care. He was just being polite. If she were
prettier——Nora went over to the mirror, swinging it back and forth in
its heavy black-walnut framework to see if she were altogether plain.
The girls at school said if she only did things with her hair——But what?
It didn’t matter. She made a difference to no one.

Father Stokesbury played cribbage with Cordelia in the evening. It was
his favorite game. He had a shameless way of inviting himself to supper,
and then staying on, hour after hour, bent over a cribbage board. Nora
didn’t know how long, because she went to bed.

Sometimes when she had moments of active resentment at the way a certain
man had invaded her chaste maiden reveries, she tried to think as badly
as possible of him to see if such measures of revenge would bring
relief. A clergyman who enjoyed playing games with an old woman like
Cordelia and drinking tea with a roomful of women was no real man. Nora
had her ideals of the world to which real men belonged, adventurous,
swashbuckling, emancipated. At least she had once made such limits to a
manly sphere. But when reality smashes into dreams——Father Stokesbury
had none of the rough masculine virtues and yet—there was so much fun in
him. His sober garb had no power of extinguishing his gaiety. He never
seemed bored by the Plant girls, or even by old Mrs. Hill, when they
intruded upon the cribbage games. He brought out his best stories for
Cordelia, who tried to laugh hard at the right time.

The old house, which had been so subdued during the long invalidism of
Mrs. Tait with the consequent procession of croaking and eccentric women
or church visitors, and had never surrendered to Nora’s youth, collapsed
before Father Stokesbury. The best silver was no longer kept hidden in a
strong-box upstairs. Cordelia’s old-fashioned jewelry was worn once
more, the quaint brooches, the heavy gold bracelets, the pearl and
emerald ring.

Old Mrs. Cooper, who visited Cordelia for two weeks every summer as a
rest from the Old Ladies’ Home in Kewaukee, where she found herself
happier than in the homes of her married sons, at once saw the change in
Cordelia.

‘She’s beginning to get over her mother’s death,’ she told Nora. ‘It’s
three years now. Poor Cordelia. She was always so prim and proper. Never
running around like other young girls. Always so good to her mother.
Stayed right by her. But she gave up her own young life. She really
never had a chance. I don’t suppose she ever walked out with any young
man.’

Mrs. Cooper liked the new rector. She went every morning to the early
service, stopping to chat with Father Stokesbury afterward. She made
airy speculations about a possible wife for him in Saint Margaret’s,
calling upon Cordelia’s advice as she went through her list. But
Cordelia, a faithful follower of the Bishop in all his High-Church
reforms, favored the celibacy of the clergy, and at most gave her
approval only to a marriage outside the parish.


 III

Late in August Lizzie Guildford returned from a tour of the East. She
appeared at church in the elegant finery that no dressmaker in Maronac
could have achieved for her. She had also imported a lorgnette, which
she flourished with the proper air of easy detachment as she followed
the service in her prayer-book. She held Father Stokesbury in a long
conversation at the door, where he was attempting to exchange morning
greetings with all of his parishioners.

At once Lizzie detected the rector’s frequent informal visits to
Cordelia’s house, and she discovered, without delay, three or four
afternoons a week, the necessity of consulting Cordelia on some
important matter which she prolonged until Father Stokesbury appeared.
He, assuming Miss Guildford to be one of Cordelia’s most cherished
friends, treated her accordingly. Cordelia, helpless in her rage, was
forced to keep up some decent courtesy to her guest. Lizzie soon began
to take the whole parish to her heart. She implored the rector to use
her extensive grounds for the Boys’ Club, which he had organized and
made his special charge. She also placed her tennis court, the only one
in town and hitherto restricted to a small group in her favor, at the
disposal of the young people, without qualifications. Nora, dwelling in
a churchly household, began to play there every afternoon with Eleanor
Porter, whose father had recently come from New York to take charge of
the Dry Docks. Rumor reported that Lizzie Guildford was at last opening
her purse strings. She had sent for a beautiful silver chalice set with
amethysts, which she presented, not to Saint Margaret’s, but to its
rector. She was so eager to do more than give money to her church that
she called at the rectory to discuss with her priest the possibilities
of service. Father Stokesbury in an ill-advised moment suggested Miss
Guildford for the Altar Guild to Cordelia.

‘I won’t have her!’ Cordelia snapped, forgetting all allegiance to a
spiritual overlord. ‘She’s no good. She wouldn’t do any work—just show
off. If she comes on, I leave.’

Naturally Father Stokesbury had a moment of pained surprise. The matter
was hastily dropped, and Lizzie continued to sigh for greater spiritual
usefulness.

Nora could see that her visits had become intolerable to Cordelia. She
was a malicious gossip, bringing out her insinuating stories in a smooth
and restrained voice in a way which infuriated the directly honest
Cordelia.

‘I suppose you’ve heard of Amy Henderson’s romance,’ she began one day,
while she was watching for Father Stokesbury’s belated appearance.
Cordelia said nothing. ‘She’s been getting the most beautiful flowers
all through the summer—carnations, lilies, and the most gorgeous roses.
I saw them when I was calling on her mother.’

‘I didn’t know you called on Mrs. Henderson,’ Cordelia interrupted.

‘I’m doing a little parish visiting at Father Stokesbury’s suggestion.
Mrs. Henderson told me about her daughter’s admirer. They don’t even
know who he is. Isn’t it romantic?’ Lizzie emitted a laugh that imitated
spontaneous girlish interest. ‘They’re sent from Kewaukee without even a
card.’

Cordelia maintained a forbidding silence.

Soon Amy Henderson’s mysterious lover began to compete with Lizzie’s
indefatigable spiritual activity in the town mind. Amy at church looked
just the same, her light hair a trifle more elaborately curled and her
face, always a vivid pink, a somewhat more pronounced carmine.
Uncritical people like Cordelia considered Amy very pretty. Since she
had been working in Weyhausen’s, she had more money to spend on clothes.
Her dresses became much more heavily trimmed with lace from bargain
counters, and on Sundays she wore a white hat trimmed with two pale-blue
ostrich plumes. Amy, like many blondes, considered blue her best color.
It gave her the innocent expression of a healthily tinted wax doll with
flaxen curls, an excellent style in dolls.

Amy’s romance, in time, took definite shape. The mysterious lover from
Kewaukee was forgotten in the fresh interest of Henry Shumway. No one in
Maronac Harbor knew anything about him. He had come from foreign parts
to take charge of the men’s department at Weyhausen’s, and he had made
his work an immediate success. Young men, always weak before the latest
novelty in apparel, began to buy their suits, neckties, hats, and shoes
from Henry Shumway. He was the type of man so irresistible to an
inexperienced girl, tall, dark, his hair curling back from his
forehead—his envious rivals suspected art there—and with a dimple in his
chin. His clothes, the necessary advertising for his business, were
always of the most recent mode. Amy, the only unmarried daughter of a
widow whose other children had married far beneath Amy’s ideals for
them, might easily sigh before the dazzle of Henry’s charms. But that
Henry, with all the ready signs of a ‘light of love,’ should at once
settle for life upon Amy, was more than Lizzie Guildford could believe.

Lizzie was expert in affairs of the heart, for she had been much courted
in her youth. But each offer filled her with the fear that she was
sought in marriage, not for herself, but for her money. In the end, she
had refused all her lovers. During this period of extensive visits upon
Cordelia, she dwelt much upon this fervid youthful period. She thought
now that perhaps she had made a mistake. There was one man—he had seemed
such an honest person. If she had married him, she wouldn’t be so lonely
now.

‘I think it’s disgusting of Lizzie carrying on all the time about how
lonely she is,’ Cordelia exploded to Nora.

‘But she probably _is_ lonely, Cordelia.’

‘She isn’t the only one. Blabbing that way about yourself and your
feelings—it isn’t decent. Why doesn’t she ask poor old Mrs. Cooper to
stay a few days with her? Mr. Cooper clerked in Guildford’s store for
twenty years.’

But Lizzie continued to be lonely in her big house. By Easter time she
had aroused the antagonism of the whole parish by her undignified
expeditions to the rectory morning, noon, and night. If she had been
younger, they would have thought she was throwing herself at Father
Stokesbury’s head. But since she was a woman past fifty, she was merely
making a fool of herself. To the amazement of the Altar Guild, Father
Stokesbury remained blind to Lizzie’s folly. He continued polite through
her long calls, followed the usual parish routine, and indulged in the
relaxation of tea and cribbage with Cordelia.

Amy’s romance remained hidden from public scrutiny until Edna Tillotson
went to Mattie Stellwagen to have a grey silk dress made for Easter.
Mattie told her, as a great secret, that she was sewing on Amy
Henderson’s wedding outfit. After much persuasion she showed Edna the
wedding dress of white crepe bespattered with expensive lace medallions.
Edna at once communicated her discovery to the Altar Guild, in the face
of Cordelia’s disapproval.

May passed, and June, but still Amy’s wedding did not come off. Hints
that Edna had made a mistake sadly ruffled her temper.

‘I saw it with my own eyes,’ she insisted.

‘Perhaps it was just a nice dress,’ Della explained, ‘and Mattie
thought——’

‘She told Mattie with her own lips it was her wedding dress.’

Lizzie made a second parish call upon Mrs. Henderson, but no one came to
the door, even though Lizzie heard suspicious creakings inside. She went
next to buy some tea like Cordelia’s at Weyhausen’s, where she waited
for Amy to attend to her order.

‘What’s this I hear about your wedding?’ Lizzie giggled.

Amy blushed, but only slightly. ‘I’m not telling.’

Unfortunately Lizzie did not know Henry Shumway, and Amy’s plans had to
mystify her a little longer. Mr. Shumway continued to serve as a
masculine ideal for Maronac youths, irrespective of his matrimonial
intentions. Whether he was informed of the interest the richest woman in
Maronac took in the manager of the men’s furnishings at Weyhausen’s
depended upon Amy’s discretion. In the meantime Lizzie continued to
flaunt her friendship with the rector of Saint Margaret’s in the faces
of the whole parish.


 CHAPTER III


 I

Lizzie’s visits to Cordelia suddenly ceased. She avoided her at church,
sweeping out in the hushed elegance she affected, without a glance in
her direction. Nora observed her tricks with quiet amusement. When
Father Stokesbury went away for a month’s vacation in July, Lizzie
grasped a little respite from her ecclesiastical excesses, and absented
herself from Saint Margaret’s until his return.

Father Stokesbury had tea with Cordelia as soon as he resumed his parish
duties. Nora managed to blunder out that she was glad to see him again,
that they had missed him.

‘My dear, gracious Miss Nora. I’d almost be willing to go away again to
hear such words from you,’ the rector laughed.

But the following day he didn’t come to tea. Cordelia said he must be
very busy. Then the second day he didn’t come. Nora went to the front
window to look down the street again and again. The next morning when
she had finished cleaning her bedroom, stealing as much reading as she
dared out of the midst of her work, she came downstairs to find the
baking materials spread out on the kitchen table, but Cordelia nowhere
in sight. She called to her from the top of the cellar steps and looked
for her in the back yard. A few minutes later Cordelia returned, took
off the coat she had worn to cover her gingham dress, and finished her
interrupted baking. When she sat down to dinner, there were scarlet
spots over her cheek-bones.

She crumbled up some bread on her plate and gulped down a cup of tea.
Then she said, ‘I’ll leave you to wash the dishes, Nora. I’m going
upstairs to lie down a while. My head aches a little.’

When Nora carried a cup of tea to Cordelia’s room late in the afternoon,
she found the door locked. Cordelia’s voice sounded very queer and
strained. She didn’t want the tea. She didn’t want anything. Just to lie
quiet and not be disturbed. Nora could never remember Cordelia’s being
ill before. It seemed strange to have the quiet rooms downstairs all to
herself.

In the midst of her clumsy attempts to get supper, Mrs. Hill appeared at
the kitchen door.

‘Where’s Cordelia?’ she whispered cautiously.

‘Upstairs.’

‘Oh!’ Mrs. Hill seemed very much excited. Her old eyes had the feverish
glint of one entrusted with news too great to be borne. ‘Of course she
hasn’t told you. Lizzie Guildford tried to kill herself. With gas. They
say Cordelia was over there this morning.’

‘Was she out of her mind?’

‘It was your minister. She was set on marrying him. She asked him. Right
out, they say. Promised to deed over everything she had. And when he
wouldn’t——’ Mrs. Hill’s whisper grew into a lively hiss. ‘That ain’t the
worst either. She said it was Cordelia. Cordelia after him instead o’
her. She told it all over the parish. It’s spread all over town. I heard
it. And the Plant girls, and Edna. But this morning, when she thought
she was most gone, she sent for Cordelia. To be forgiven an’ die in
peace. That’s Lizzie for you. An’ they say Cordelia forgave her.’

Nora wanted to hold her fingers over her ears. Anything to shut out that
terrible story. Her lips curled in infinite scorn. ‘What fools women
are,’ she said.

Mrs. Hill shook her head. ‘You’re young. You don’t know. Just you wait.
The smartest ones often make the biggest fools of themselves.’ She rose
from her chair with a deep sigh. ‘Poor Cordelia. This’ll come hard on
her. That pride o’ hers. But no one’ll ever know. She’s upstairs, you
say?’

Nora went over and examined the steaming tea-kettle. ‘She took a little
nap after finishing up the baking,’ she said.

Upon Mrs. Hill’s departure, she carried Cordelia’s supper upstairs and
left it outside the door. Then she locked herself in her room. She
couldn’t eat a morsel. Maybe she was sick like Cordelia. Sick all over.
She would think of him no longer. Those mad fancies of hers—never again.
A man over whom women made fools of themselves. Lizzie Guildford—she and
Lizzie together. She dug her fingers into her burning cheeks. The shame
of it. Yes, and Cordelia too. She knew now—she saw—she understood why
Cordelia had changed so—become a different person. This was the end.
Never again would she think of him—conjure up before her late at night
in the darkness an image of blue eyes that crinkled with laughter and a
voice with endearing twists and turns.

The next day she stole off to go walking with Eleanor Porter before
tea-time. She could not trust herself to meet Father Stokesbury. If he
came——

Cordelia and Nora sat down to almost silent meals, Nora stifled by her
guilty consciousness of Cordelia’s failure to keep up a little
meaningless talk. She stayed away from the house as much as she could.

A week passed, and Nora saw the rector only at church. A weight of
constraint depressed the whole parish, while every one in low tones
discussed Lizzie Guildford. It was rumored about that Father Stokesbury
had stopped making calls, except where there was illness, and had shut
himself up in his study the rest of the time.

At last, Lizzie was well again, and departed for an indefinite stay with
friends. Nora wondered if Cordelia had seen her since that ghastly day.


 II

Amy Henderson’s mother was found dead in bed one morning. Amy sent at
once for Cordelia, who stayed with her most of the time until after the
funeral.

‘I didn’t see that Mr. Shumway to the service,’ Mrs. Hill reported to
Nora. ‘But he sent a huge wreath of white roses.’

The morning after the funeral, Nora, watching at the front window for
Eleanor Porter to come and play tennis, saw Amy Henderson starting for
the store.

‘There goes Amy as usual,’ she called to Cordelia, who was dusting the
parlor. Cordelia came and stood beside her, and together they watched
Amy out of sight. ‘I’m glad Amy’s got that young man,’ Nora observed
half to herself.

‘What young man?’ Cordelia demanded. ‘She hasn’t got him. She has
nothing.’ She resumed her dusting with a kind of rage.

‘I thought Miss Guildford said—and——’

‘Lizzie Guildford—what she says——’ Cordelia broke off suddenly, as if
she had been betrayed out of silence.

Eleanor’s shout sounded from the next corner, and Nora bounded out of
the house to escape from the knowledge of what Cordelia had tried
unsuccessfully to hide from her keen young eyes.

‘I’ll beat you a love set,’ boasted Eleanor, as they raced side by side
toward Lizzie Guildford’s tennis court.

By degrees rumors came to Nora which filled out what Cordelia had not
said. It seemed that Henry Shumway wasn’t going to marry Amy. He wasn’t
going to marry any one. At least not right away. He made love to all the
girls—even the waitresses at the hotel. An inexperienced girl like Amy
didn’t know any better than to take his empty gallantry seriously. And
those flowers that came from the mysterious donor in Kewaukee—Edna
Tillotson insinuated that Amy had sent them to herself.

As for Cordelia, age lay upon her once more, and her former chilling
reserve, when she resumed the mourning garments she had recently put
aside. Life was a steady course between home and church, back and forth,
as if nothing had ever disturbed the even monotony of its ways. The
house, too, settled back into its creaking joints as befits decrepit
age.


 III

Nora, returning from her daily walk to the end of the pier which
stretched out over the lake to the fog station, with its bells and
warning lights, saw a handsome collie pup bounding toward her. Roughly
friendly, he leaped up against her with joyful barks.

‘You beauty!’ she exclaimed in delight. ‘You dear young dog!’

Nora’s heart leaped at the sound of a familiar voice. ‘He is a fine
fellow, isn’t he?’ Father Stokesbury said. ‘The old Bishop gave him to
me. It was jolly nice of him, I thought. He said this chap would keep me
from being lonely at the rectory.’

Nora could not speak, but she lifted eloquent brown eyes to the young
man’s attempt at gaiety. There was something gone from him, the edge of
the fine buoyancy of his bright spirit dulled, the laughter less
infectious and bubbling.

If she could only have told him then, putting out her hand to this
lonely, discouraged young man, that she wasn’t like the others, that she
was his friend, and that she understood. She was desolate, too, very
often. His gaiety had helped her. He must smile in the same old way
again. Perhaps she could even have helped him to understand the poor
women who had complicated his position in the parish. They were very
unhappy, too. His brightness had uplifted them. They couldn’t help
liking him perhaps too well.

Yes, she understood. Knowledge of the emotional hunger of
women—especially women—came in a sudden glare of illumination. You could
be old like Lizzie Guildford or Cordelia, but there was something inside
you which kept young. It hungered for love—for the love of some man. Amy
Henderson and her pathetic dreams——Nora had once thought only the very
young of sixteen, seventeen, had hopes that life wouldn’t be dull and
sad. But she discerned now the truth that such hopes never left one.
Always you were tormented—always—by the wish to be happy—happy by loving
and being loved.

But Nora couldn’t put this into words. She felt a helpless choking at
her lips. She must hurry away. She couldn’t even tell him she was sorry.

Father Stokesbury summoned another gallant attempt at a smile before she
left him. Out of his hearing, the sobs escaped her. ‘The poor, poor
women!’ she wept aloud, her words lost in the boom of the lake as it
beat against the shore.

Beyond the sandy beach and on top of the hill close to Cordelia’s house,
Nora looked back. Father Stokesbury stood gazing out on the dancing blue
waters, the lively puppy close beside him, quite motionless.

 PART II.
 The Sacred Grove


 CHAPTER I


 I

When Nora saw Petersfield College hidden away in purple hills, she
forgot Cordelia and Maronac Harbor. For her the venerable brick
buildings draped in ivy, as exquisite as an old woman swathed in fine
lace, the graceful tower above the trees, the sweep of grass and
shrubbery, were little more than a setting for the girls, girls her own
age everywhere, on the tennis courts, the walks, leaning out of the
windows, girls destined to be her friends.

The older students in the residence hall to which she and Eleanor Porter
had been assigned took immediate possession of them. After dinner they
were carried off to a spread in ‘Fuzzy’ Leland’s room. Fuzzy, dressed in
a white sweater and white serge skirt, with hair as straight as wire and
the figure of a twelve-year-old boy, was a champion tennis player.

Her room was the typical student domain at Petersfield. Banners
representing most of the colleges of the country and chaotically varied
in color scheme adorned the walls. Photographs upheld in fish-net took
on dust from year to year. Tables, window-sills, the floor space under
chairs and bed held the books out of which Petersfield appeared to have
been feeding the previous moment. Wicker chairs in a collapsible state
and the couch heaped with cushions held all of the fifteen girls who
were not on the floor.

Several of the guests were strumming mandolins and singing ‘Dear
Petersfield, thy hills we love,’ to the accompanying gabble of the rest.
There were rapid introductions, queries as to where the freshmen had
come from, much giggling, and references to mysterious and confusing
college events—Greek games—step singing—freshman song-fest—Michaelmas
play. All their talk buzzed around Petersfield.

‘You better get Miss Perkins. She’s a sweet thing.’

‘Not that horrid Lambert woman.’

‘I’m passing off French orals, girls. He’s such a darling. Just the
cutest——’

‘She’s brought eight trunks—fifteen evening dresses and three fur coats.
And a maid came with her to help unpack.’

‘I’m going to take life easy this year. You watch.’

‘As if she ever cracked a book! Listen to Margie.’

‘My own true love! My turtle dove!’ warbled one of the mandolin artists.

The girls began to help Fuzzy pass the food. There was a roast chicken
one of the girls had brought from home, some messy jelly, a
miscellaneous assortment of cake, cookies, lukewarm cocoa, very sweet,
pickles, chocolates. In spite of the fact that an interval of only three
hours had elapsed since dinner, the food all disappeared.

Fuzzy asked Eleanor and Nora if they played tennis. Eleanor, who had
tennis ambitions, confessed afterward to Nora that she felt weak at her
stomach when she talked to Fuzzy.

‘If she’d only like me,’ she whispered. ‘Do you know she’s one of the
most popular girls at college?’

Nora, after the quiet life at Cordelia’s, felt very tired when she got
back to her room. But as she leaned out of her window and listened to
the procession of girls singing as they marched around the campus, she
already felt the charm of her college.

For the first week the older girls took charge of the freshmen, advised
them about furnishing their rooms, informed them about various college
activities, explained the course of study. Night after night the
devastating spreads continued. Then at last the freshmen were handed
over to the faculty.

All of Nora’s teachers were women, some old, some young. The older ones
seemed nicer, their voices mellow and low. The young instructors were
restless, strident, metallic, inclined to be humorous over the mistakes
of freshmen. College classes seemed very similar to high school. One
went on studying the same old things: English—no change; history—more
work in the library; French instead of German; more Latin if you wanted
it or classes in baby Greek; geology instead of botany.

But college life made all the difference. It was wonderful. You sat in
chapel, squeezed in a great throng, while Prexy in college gown talked
about the great opportunities for women with trained minds and their
responsibilities toward the rest of the world. You stood on the library
steps and sang ‘Dear Petersfield, thy hills we love.’ At meals girls
from San Francisco, from Denver, Boston, Mobile, and Santa Fe sat at the
same table. There was even a girl from Hawaii. In class meeting you
seconded the motion made by a charming young person in a rose dress whom
you longed to know. You studied your lessons with three or four girls
piled on your couch, you walked in a squadron out on the country road to
the cider mill or picnicked in the grove overlooking the lake. Never for
a waking moment were you left by yourself.

After a few days the freshman class broke up into its component parts.
The upperclassmen had long since returned to their Olympian heights.
Groups were formed, depending largely upon the halls in which freshmen
lived, and also upon a common interest in tennis, plays, study. The
athletic crowd to which Eleanor attached herself swept over the
geographical borders of halls. The dramatic crowd fiercely guarded its
entrances against all freshmen except those who manifested a slavish
desire to perform the jobs disagreeable to the real stars. The society
crowd, always weak among the freshmen, began to imitate its chosen
leaders, who were already talking about Prom Week. The grinds, an
ignominious crew at Petersfield as at most institutions supposedly
designed for the higher learning, dug into their work and remembered
that a poet had been produced from the studious upperclassmen and
another intellectual luminary had been sent abroad by the college for a
year of study. Then there were those like Nora, undecided where they
would leap.

The freshmen, however, were united by their common drudgery. They took
no joy in their labors. To the query of upperclassmen why these young
lambs did not frisk gaily about on the college green, they always
answered, ‘No time. Too much to do.’ What puzzled them in their stray
moments was why their elders studied so much less. They gathered in
groups of four, five, or six after dinner and initiated a noisy evening
of toil. Studying by one’s self was too depressingly impossible. They
formed frequent conspiracies to complain to their taskmistresses about
their burdens, but they never did. Every one was against them. Their
neighbors wanted to sleep and put them to flight. They had to learn to
choose places for congregation close to meek or indifferent girls—or
very sound sleepers.

Sometimes Nora revolted from this intellectual gregariousness and crept
into deserted corners of the library or into some abandoned classroom.
Where was all this joy that one got from intellectual achievement? she
asked herself. Was it all some romantic tale created by the old for the
deception of the young? Professor Steele, the grey-haired head of the
history department, who sometimes lectured to the freshman history
class, told them the satisfaction of her life had come from the
production of a modest shelf of volumes now used by her students. She
seemed like an honest woman, but one wondered.

As Nora and her classmates looked about them, they discovered that much
attention was given to friendship at Petersfield. It was different from
the careless comradeship of the group in which one happened to fall.
Sometimes a girl had an older member of the faculty for her friend. She
worshipped her from afar, studied to please her, sent her flowers before
Christmas vacation, called upon her for a few scared moments in Faculty
Hall. She was called ‘my dear’ as a final reward. If the faculty friend
were young, she made an ideal companion for picnics, expeditions to New
York—only an hour away by train, a confidante to whom one entrusted the
problems of a difficult home life.

Miss Goodwin, the most popular faculty friend, was so universally adored
by freshmen that she was humorously designated ‘The Freshman Disease.’
She was neither young nor old, tall nor short. She wasn’t exactly
handsome, but her magnificent eyes could easily slay a victim. She
affected the masculine in dress, wearing strictly tailored clothes, and
mannish felt hats, shoes, and ties; her voice was full of chesty tones.
Her province was the æsthetic. She wrote poetic dramas which the
students acted. She read poetry aloud to choice spirits who flocked to
her rooms. She was delightfully friendly and informal. In an atmosphere
in which a freshman felt lonely and often queer, Miss Goodwin restored
self-respect and confidence.

Nora, much to the envy of the less fortunate, was assigned to Miss
Goodwin’s English class. But for some reason not entirely plain to
herself, she remained a cold auditor of her poetic and dramatic
displays.

‘No, I don’t care for her much,’ she stated frankly to those who
demanded to know if she wasn’t ‘crazy’ about Miss Goodwin. ‘She tires
me, I guess. I didn’t come here to take a course in Miss Goodwin, and we
don’t learn much English.’

From such apostasy the girls shrank away. Before the month was over,
Nora was transferred to another section. She was a little worried. Had
the fire of Miss Goodwin’s eloquence grown cold before her critical
young eyes?

The weaker of Miss Goodwin’s disciples dropped down to the lower plane
of friendship with other girls, usually members of their own class.
Friendship was the great human experience. Nora, observing these sudden
infatuations, realized that she and Eleanor did not fit into the strict
college interpretation of the word. Eleanor, trailing after Fuzzy
Leland, was already on the freshman tennis team. Nora, too, desired a
friend.

Nora was studying history in her room the night her neighbors had
adjourned to the assembly hall to hear a famous woman lecture on the
question of woman suffrage. In a community life quiet moments must be
snatched when they could be.

A knock at her door struck the moment of Robespierre’s ascendancy. Emily
Moorhead, a tall dark girl from the opposite end of the corridor,
perched on Nora’s couch.

‘I hope I’m not disturbing you, dear,’ she said, stretching out like a
sleepy cat. ‘I saw the light under your door.’

There was something about Emily which brought back to Nora her earliest
childhood terror. Waking up from a nap, she had seen crawling toward
her, with silent, crouching tread, a striped creature which resembled
the tiger in her alphabet book. Her screams had brought her mother
running into the room. But all the assurances that the visitor was a
nice tiger-pussy from next door could not efface an experience which
repeated itself always at the sight of a cat. Yes, Emily, languorously
sleek and purring, was exactly like a cat.

‘I’m so glad to find you alone, Nora. I want to know you better. Don’t
you often feel there are people about you—something inside of you
reaches out to them? You long for them.’ Emily dropped her lazy feline
pose and sat erect. ‘I’m like that. There are few people here I care
about, but those few——’ Emily’s tone was significantly tender.

Nora had been instructed by Cordelia to distrust a diagnosis of the
heart in this open fashion. She secretly hoped she didn’t belong to
Emily’s few.

Emily began to save a seat for her at meals; she made lengthy visits
after dinner when Nora wanted to study. She asked her advice about
clothes. But Nora felt no devotion growing for this new and inconvenient
shadow. She tried to avoid Emily, but with no great success.

One evening Emily stayed longer than usual. Suddenly she threw her arms
about Nora. ‘You darling. You don’t know how I love you, Nora. I want to
be your only friend—the only one you trust your secrets to. I’m so
lonely. No one understands me. I yearn to be loved.’

Nora detached herself violently and avoided the sight of Emily’s darkly
flushed face. ‘I don’t like people that way,’ she said coldly. ‘And I’m
very sorry, but I’ve got some work to do to-night.’

Emily slammed the door after her. The next week silly little Sally
Winthrop was walking through the corridors with Emily’s arm over her
shoulder. They were inseparable. Nora looked the other way.


 II

With the spring, many of the freshmen, Nora among them, developed nerves
and bad tempers. They complained that they couldn’t sleep at night. Mary
Hopkins surreptitiously dragged her couch across the hall to her friend
Mabel Witherspoon’s room. The next morning a very chilly housemother
superintended its journey back. The college physician gave the verdict
that injudicious study and disregard of the rules about exercise were
responsible for the decline in freshman morale. Her patients said among
themselves that it was too much Petersfield.

The college, in spite of its proximity to New York, prided itself upon
its seclusion from the world. The village which supplied the material
needs was three miles away. The simplicity of life many of the girls
would never experience again. But after eight months of sylvan peace,
the freshmen sighed ungratefully for the world. They regarded the
juniors and seniors returning with their chaperons on the nine o’clock
train after a day in New York with the awed wonder of mediæval villagers
gazing upon a pilgrim with cockle shell and staff back from a holy
shrine.

Talk circled around teachers, studies, other girls, summer vacation.
Boys were mentioned only in vague generalities. Except during prom week
the only males who invaded the campus delivered groceries, meat, or ice.

‘I’m going to flirt with the baker’s boy,’ Gladys Spencer threatened.

Could one be sure that one’s fellow freshmen never thought about love?
Nora had abased recollections of the hours she had wasted
dreaming—longing—that some day——Even at Petersfield—almost like a
convent—a nunnery—she could not entirely keep burning desires out of her
mental labors. If those ridiculous women had let Father Stokesbury
alone——He was the finest man she had ever known. Heroes, great figures
in history, King Alfred, and Cromwell, and Garibaldi, and Louis XV (she
had a catholicity in her ideals as a result of the freshman survey
history course)—they dazzled one’s imagination, but one sighed for some
real person. Alone on walks—wanting to be alone—to think——She wouldn’t
go through life like Cordelia and Lizzie Guildford, and then when it was
too late——Her dreams dealt with unrealities. A hero—something to attach
the virtues one admired to—a kind of spiritual clotheshorse for the
imagination to play upon. He looked a little like Father Stokesbury. Not
a clergyman, but with his smile and enigmatic blue eyes.

Of course one had moments filled with flattering theories about maidenly
self-sufficiency. When lessons went well. Then again——


 III

At the beginning of her second year, Nora met Cabell Westley, a
classmate from Georgia, who occupied the room just above hers. Cabell
was frankly bored with Petersfield. She dragged her feet when she walked
and powdered her nose during the class hour. Her favorite evening
amusement was to dress up in party clothes, and spend endless time on a
cluster of curls which she draped over her left ear. When she was
properly arrayed, she descended the fire-escape which crossed Nora’s
window. She leaped down from the window-sill airily, and surveyed her
friend studying.

‘You’re killin’ yourself, child. It’s my duty to give you a minute o’
rest. How’d I look, darlin’? Fit to kill?’ She pirouetted in front of
Nora’s mirror, singing in a monotone:

 ‘I’m dyin’ for some one to love me,
 Some one to call my own.’

Then turning suddenly, she added in an attempt at desperation, ‘I mean
it, honey. I honest am.’

Writing letters to her boy friends in Georgia and reading their replies
to an available victim demanded much of her time. For Nora, these
passionate declarations of love had an uncanny fascination. Cabell had
been engaged numerous times. Her fiancés, past and present, in an
alternating row of silver and ivory frames on her dressing-table, she
arranged and rearranged according to their place in her favor at the
moment. From day to day they bobbed up and down the line like
contestants in an animated spelling-match.

She liked to review their qualifications. ‘Nice boy. Wonderful eyes,
don’t you think? But such a jealous disposition.’ And of another. ‘He’s
got heaps o’ money. But drink! Honey, he’d swallow the ocean if you’d
sell it to him over the bar. And gamblin’! He’s no mamma’s boy to settle
down with. And I love my peace an’ comfort. A nice easy pair o’ old
shoes the mornin’ after a dance; that’s my idea of a husband.’

She explained in detail how easy it was to be engaged to two or even
three boys simultaneously, if a girl used her head a little. ‘Keep ’em
apart. One at Sewaunee. The other at Athens. An’ one in Atlanta.’

Between classes and before and after meals Cabell sauntered up and down
the corridors in her shambling fashion, seeking excitement and singing
her usual plaint, ‘I’m dyin’ for some one to love me.’

Nora heard a whistle and smelled tobacco one night after she had gone to
bed. A window above her was opened. There were stealthy words exchanged
with a deep voice below, suppressed giggles, and then high heels
clattered on the fire-escape. In the brightness from a light below, Nora
saw Cabell distinctly as she passed her window. She at once closed her
eyes and pretended deep sleep.

When Cabell’s midnight flights into the unknown with a village youth of
no untarnished reputation finally were made known to the faculty, Cabell
was doomed to return to Georgia. She made farewell visits to all her
friends, her jaunty cheerfulness quite untouched by the disgrace which
ought to have weighted her down.

‘Good-bye, honey,’ she said to Nora. ‘Now don’t you think naughty things
about poor little Cabell. She just had to keep in practice.’

From the first letter, Nora gathered that Cabell’s technique had
suffered little. After a second, Nora never heard from her again.


 IV

Eleanor and Fuzzy were patient with Nora’s attempts to be athletic. They
showed her about strokes, chops, and their deadly effective service.

‘You must keep at it,’ Fuzzy admonished her. ‘Swing your racquet in your
room every evening. I keep up practice that way all winter.’

‘Don’t think of anything but the game while you’re playing,’ Eleanor
added.

‘But I can’t help thinking of other things.’

‘Then you’ll never make a player,’ they told her.

Nora tried basket-ball next, indoor baseball, skating, and skiing, but
she went through them all in the same undistinguished fashion. She was
greatly disappointed, for she wanted to belong to the athletic crowd, in
her opinion the sanest and the most vigorous in college. They were a
handsome, cheerful group, with none of the sick fancies of Gwendolen
Morris, writer of exotic verse for the college paper, whose brains Nora
was forced to admire. But it was hopeless to try to compete with others
in brawn and muscle.

Her days were crowded with ill-assorted studies. Chemistry ate up her
time as relentlessly as mathematics her freshman year. But she was still
in the stage of believing what she was told, and did not discredit the
