theory that the necessary mental discipline comes from the hated
subjects which are conquered with the greatest pains. She spent
beautiful spring afternoons in the nauseous atmosphere of the
laboratory, going over experiments again and again before they came out
correctly.

She left the laboratory late one afternoon and strolled up the hill on
the other side of the lake. When she heard voices, she dropped down
under a tree to escape company. She kept very still. The voices came
nearer and nearer. Nora closed her eyes. A low voice, a high piping
voice—Minna Horton and Gwendolen Morris in one of their interminable
debates. If they would only go on up the hill.

The low tones came from a spot not far away. At first she disregarded
them. Then a chance phrase interrupting her thoughts froze her into
immediate attention. The words of lovers—exchanges of devotion, undying
devotion. Nora opened her eyes. The two girls embraced, kissed each
other again and again. She was conscious of the drooping narrowness of
Gwendolen’s shoulders, the slenderness of her neck, as she threw herself
against Minna’s bulky frame.

Suddenly, the two girls fell apart. Whistles and catcalls echoed from
the hilltop. A young boy, who had been cutting grass with a scythe, was
shouting and jeering down upon them. The grin on his red face and the
cackling derision of his laughter were like the capers of a satyr
invading a nymph’s sacred bower. The girls fled to the protection of the
campus buildings.

Nora lay buried in the fresh spring grass, her hands pressed hard
against her eyes. Finally she roused herself to escape to her room. The
stream of girls passed her unobserved.

Minna Horton was an honor science student with ambitions to be a doctor.
Gwendolen was the literary genius of the campus. They had been intimate
friends since their freshman year, a little too exclusively interested
in each other, perhaps, but among the cleverest girls Nora knew. And all
the time possibly, this poison. Nora had a sick memory of the fungi she
had studied in botany, the rank growths, forms of life springing up in
unhealthy places, feeding on rot—slime molds found in damp earth and
decaying vegetable matter. Creation wasn’t all clean and pure. Nor human
relationships.

Nora had got her first shock in Maronac. A saloon-keeper there, a very
low man, had a meek little daughter who had gone to school with her. She
remembered the girl’s downcast brown eyes. Freda had suddenly
disappeared. The girls whispered. Nora heard them. Like Beatrice Cenci
and her father.

But girls—two girls. She couldn’t understand why she felt faint at the
thought. Emily Moorhead, her first year. Only instinct, like the swift
revulsion of a young animal sniffing at a poisonous weed, had held her
back.

It wasn’t their fault. They hungered for affection. Was it a choice
between this and pranks like Cabell Westley’s?

Petersfield was, after all, not very different from Maronac Harbor. It
duplicated the unequal proportion of sexes, the narrow conversational
boundaries, the emotional strain. The only difference she could discover
was that there were no old people at Petersfield—at least none of the
professors was very old—no babies, no dogs, and no cats except in the
college kitchens. Among the meagre sprinkling of men, not particularly
imposing, there was none equal to Father Stokesbury.

What were the girls to do? They wanted something which college had not
given them. Intellectual ambitions, the splendid women on the faculty,
the vigorous student body were not enough. But what—what else?

Nora herself was the same girl who had left Maronac Harbor. She had gone
on all this year perpetuating her freshman follies. Her arrogance still
isolated her. She had distrusted girlish intimacy because of Emily
Moorhead. She had scorned friendship with the women who could have given
her some of the lessons of age and wisdom because other girls fawned
upon their professors and used their favor for ulterior ends. The
intellectual hunger, the longing for companionship, which made her
suffer in Maronac were still unsatisfied.

And what Cabell had—what other girls wanted—the chance to be admired,
loved. Never in Maronac Harbor. No one had even glanced at her. Would
she finally, like Minna and Gwendolen—in her desperate need to be
loved——No, never. But what was she going to do?


 V

Nina Willoughby, a tall, slim girl who wore, with bored nonchalance, the
handsome clothes her family heaped upon her, had recently taken up woman
suffrage. She had marched down Fifth Avenue in a procession of
suffragettes, and her photograph had appeared in the ‘Times’ the
preceding Sunday. All Petersfield now worshipped her.

She was urged to organize college enthusiasm for political
enfranchisement. The place of the first meeting, to which Mary Howe
dragged Nora, was strangely enough a deserted graveyard. Nora, from a
section of the country fresh in its memories, had spent many an
afternoon reading the inscriptions of a preceding century upon the
lichen-covered brown stones lying flat in the tall weeds.

When Nora and Mary arrived, Nina and her subalterns already had a goodly
number of followers gathered around them. In the spring twilight the
birds, long ago retired to slumber after a busy day, were chattering a
lively protest at being disturbed.

A program of eloquent speeches resulted in the formation of the Woman
Suffrage League. At once the new reformers burned for action. Nora, on
the fringe of the crowd, listened in amazement to Nina’s proposal that
they prove the seriousness of their protest against the cruel exclusion
of woman from the public affairs of her community and country by
spending the night in the graveyard with no bed but a hard flat stone.
Shrill shouts of approval rent the still night.

A graveyard—sleeping there—votes for women—Nora tried to puzzle out the
connection. While she was still weighing this new college problem, one
of the officials of the Woman Suffrage League had separated her from
Mary, and she was stretched out on a tombstone, very hard and cold, with
the stars and a young moon shining down upon her. Her unaccustomed bed
got colder every moment. The weeds tickled her nose—made her sneeze. Her
back—her neck—it was as if some unseen monster were taking great icy
nips out of her raw flesh.

Next to her squirmed Frances Thompson, a freshman in Nora’s hall. ‘I
wish I could sneak home,’ she whispered. ‘I’d rather sleep than vote any
night.’

‘I won’t stay here,’ Nora whispered back. ‘I’m going to chuck it.’

‘Come on.’

The two bobbed up, and slowly, on hands and knees, began crawling toward
the stone wall near them. The birds began to scold afresh. A dog barked.
They stopped an instant, and then crept on. The tall weeds assisted
their escape, and the moon whisked under a cloud. A few more paces, and
Frances began to giggle. Nora gave her a frantic punch. They clambered
over the wall, their hands and legs cut by blackberry thorns. On the
other side, they halted, found out where they were, and then began to
run.

The chapel clock was striking. They flew over the rough ground. A moment
more, and they were inside the doors before they were locked for the
night.

Safely inside Nora’s room, they stood examining each other. Their faces
were scratched. Frances’s hand was bleeding. Nora’s pleated skirt was
torn by the last fence. Throwing themselves on the couch, they rocked
back and forth with wild laughter.

Nora began to rub her aching back. ‘It’s got—the—the print of Eliza
Cook’s obituary,’ she snorted between her seizures. ‘“Eliza Cook, died
in childbed 1801. Requiescat in peace.”’

During the next day, Nora observed her sleepy classmates under Professor
Steele’s all-seeing eye. They sneezed. There was a suspicious aroma of
camphor about them.

That night the New York papers came out with blazing headlines:

 PETERSFIELD GIRLS MAKE PROTEST
 NIGHT OF VIGIL IN GRAVEYARD
 VOTES FOR WOMEN

So this was Nina’s scheme. Publicity for woman suffrage. Women were a
queer crowd. In England they heckled members of Parliament and fought
the police. They seemed to enjoy being dragged off to prison and
starving themselves. What would they start doing in America if fools
like Nina had their way? As for Nora herself, she was done with woman
suffrage.

Prexy was furious. For several days it looked as if the seniors who had
started the affair might not receive their diplomas.

Miss Goodwin’s latest poetic drama, fortunately, diverted the college
authorities from Nina’s escapade. The play was to be presented in the
new open-air theatre. Practically every girl in college was dragged into
it. Nora hurried away from laboratory to drape herself in Greek robes.
The play was written in the classical spirit, with the girls
personifying various abstractions never quite clear during rehearsals,
and with dancing throngs weaving in and out among the maidens declaiming
Miss Goodwin’s resounding hexameters.

The evening of the performance, the sun set over the pines in front of
the audience with a melodramatic arc of crimson and orange as the
opening songs began. Never, all the oldest professors said, had there
been a more perfect spring night at Petersfield. The white-robed
Grecians in their choral rites did honor to the trees, the flowers, the
fountain, the sky, and last to their own maidenhood, represented by a
tutelary spirit, Miss Goodwin, a little too stout for classic draperies.
As they circled round her, they sang a concluding devotional before the
final dance of the maidens ended the play.

‘It’s the true spirit of our colleges,’ Nora’s Greek professor announced
afterward. ‘Our reverence for girlhood, for maidenhood. Miss Goodwin has
placed our modern attitude in Greek form with real genius. But the
intellectual world has always been Greek. It’s the spirit of the mind.’

The play had been great fun. During the excitement of the past days,
even Nora had got loose from her moorings. She watched Miss Goodwin
seated even more securely upon her throne, her laurel wreath fresh and
shining, with a quietly amused smile.

‘Isn’t she wonderful?’ breathed the freshmen.

Nora, in the last expiring moments of sophomore scorn, curled her lip in
a final ironic quiver. ‘Wonderful,’ she agreed.


 CHAPTER II


 I

Nora returned to college in the fall of 1914 dressed in a new suit
equipped with the latest embarrassment of fashion, a long skirt so tight
that the ingenuities of dressmaking had conceived a slit on one side
which permitted the wearer to take a mincing step. But over the gash in
the front of Nora’s skirt Cordelia had draped a pleated silk curtain
which covered Nora’s leg. On the campus, crowds of Petersfield girls,
all wearing crippling garments like Nora’s, some with the addition of
flounces at the top, which stuck out behind like the bustles of a
preceding era, learned for the first time that a war had begun in
Europe.

The adult world had been murmuring something about the assassination of
a certain European prince no one had ever heard of before, but a college
generation had not anticipated all this unnecessary fuss about it.
Petersfield in its leafy luxuriance of early September appeared
unchanged. But it developed that the war had already touched
Petersfield. Professor Steele and other members of the faculty had been
held in Europe. Some of the girls on holiday jaunts in France, Italy,
and Germany had not returned. When all of them finally trickled back,
they brought tales of lost baggage, terrible crossings in steerage
quarters resembling the coops built for travelling poultry, and rumors
of a stream of French and Belgian refugees already homeless.

Professor Steele at once hung a large violently colored map of Europe in
the hall outside of her classroom door, punctured with vari-colored
pins, which she moved from day to day. Strange names gradually took on
the familiar accents of friends. The Marne—not one of the girls had ever
heard of it before—turned out to be a river; Rheims—there was a picture
of the cathedral in the library; and of course one had a vague idea of
Antwerp.

A popular college sport developed from the crowds gathered around
Professor Steele’s map between classes. It was the ambition of the
student body to catch her at her daily pin-shifting. One would feel, a
jocular senior commented, almost like God, to be making pins jig up and
down in this apparently aimless fashion. The English and the French, red
and orange in Miss Steele’s scheme, developed a humorous habit of
jumping in one direction, then veering off in some mad chase, and
finally returning to their original position. It really was a huge joke.

The college, in a body, marched to the village church the first Sunday
in October to join in the prayers of Peace Sunday. It really was a
terrible bore, planned by Prexy, who thought the student body should try
to wake up the village. They marched back very hungry, with unbecoming
levity, to their college retirement, quite convinced the village had not
appreciated their valiant effort. The starving Belgians then obtruded
upon their customary peace. What would it be next?

During the dull November rains which drove the girls to the library,
Nora made her great college discovery. History had suddenly become the
most fascinating subject in the world. One day she had crept into a
seminar room, discovered empty in an idle scouting expedition at certain
hours of the day. Left alone with a pile of books, she experienced what
was akin to religious conversion. She left the library another person.
The war may have helped to lift the scales from her eyes; or it may have
been the transformation in Professor Steele, about which both faculty
and students were beginning to wonder.

A cold and detached scholar had suddenly caught fire. In the fall of
1914 she taught with a passionate earnestness which crowded her
classroom. Her big course for many years had been the political and
social study of modern Europe, upon which she had written three solid
volumes, highly respected in the world of scholarship. Every summer she
had wandered from one European city to another, reading in the libraries
of London, Paris, Berlin, tramping their streets, talking to the
professors in the international fashion which only the universities seem
to have learned. But in her weighing of evidence she had been so fearful
of rash judgments, of partiality, of her own conclusions upon subjects
never perfectly understood by an outsider, that her lectures had been
cautious and uninspiring. No one could tell what had made the change in
her. Her opinions about the war she kept to herself, preserving an
apparent rigid neutrality that the Executive of her country would have
approved. But Nora detected something which had never been there before.

Early and late Nora toiled in the library. The war had become a gigantic
spectacle, a new game for an initiate into historical mysteries. She
began to search for the source of this terrible hatred which was
destroying Europe. The raid of English seacoast towns, the threatened
German blockade, the Zeppelin raids on Paris, the sinking of ships
carrying American passengers, which a few sentimental professors at
Petersfield were using not only to prove the invidious cruelty of German
ingenuity, but also to develop an emotional reaction among the student
body, had different uses for her.

In Maronac she had lived among German people long enough to suspect
claims made for their diabolical supremacy. What surprised her was that
they had thought to do all these terrible things before their enemies.
Germans were slow-moving in Maronac, sentimental about Christmas trees
and their own music, and a little stubborn in holding to an old-world
attitude toward women. But they were good neighbors and hard-working
citizens.

She had a brief but vivid moment of emotion when she read in Cordelia’s
cramped and almost illegible handwriting that Father Stokesbury had
sailed for England to offer himself as an army chaplain. What relief!
The war had at least offered him escape from that pack of adoring women
who had drawn their tight cordons to imprison him in their female world.
But he had got back to the real world—the world of men. The soldiers in
the back lines—the hospitals—how they would be refreshed by his boyish
fun!

The war finally captured Petersfield undergraduates. Emily Moorhead
collected money for Belgian babies, and Gwendolen Morris wrote a poem
about bleeding France, Lafayette, and sister republics. In the library
Nora began slowly building up the impartial historical spirit. The dead
in Europe were still very far away.


 II

Since the collapse of the Woman Suffrage League, feminism had held
Petersfield only fitfully. When a famous Scandinavian woman came to
lecture the previous year, the intellectuals learned that the noblest
creature among women was the unmarried mother. But where in a place like
Petersfield does one find a glorious mate like the one described in the
lecture, and when he is found, how does one circumvent college
authorities?

‘Remember Hannah Dodsley,’ croaked one of the intellectuals not yet
converted to the new idea of having babies without husbands. Hannah, a
meek little freshman two years ago, had been sent home because she was
going to have a baby.

‘I don’t think many women will want them—that way,’ another suggested
delicately.

‘Do all women with husbands want babies?’

‘And if she has three or four already, does she want any more—with a
husband or without?’ queried one of seven daughters.

Evidently there were two sides to the question of maternal glory.

In the meantime the intellectuals sighed to make the world better. Since
the passing of the Home Rule Bill, Ireland promised to need them no
longer. They had always had socialistic dreams, but now for the first
time they called themselves the Socialist League. Ellen Standish, their
president, the daughter of a cotton manufacturer in New Jersey, planned
to take over the management of her father’s mill the following summer.
Other members formed equally ambitious programmes.

The new professor in sociology, Mr. Michael Powys, had some close
connection with their becoming pacifists. He was a phenomenon in
Petersfield, young, handsome, intense. His blue eyes burned with the
flames which reform the world.

Mr. Powys had been discovered in Europe early the preceding summer by
one of the more adventurous economists, who invited him to Petersfield.
Naturally the war held him on his native British shores. He was,
therefore, regarded as only another of the war casualties, and his
proposed courses of lectures struck from the calendar. But in January he
arrived unannounced, and with no explanations concerning his appearance.
It was rumored that the bribes and the prayers of a wealthy father had
got him out of England, where his views of non-resistance threatened to
make trouble. Although some of the faculty declared he was a deserter
from duty and a traitor to his country, it was difficult to pack off a
person of mature years who had been invited to Petersfield.

Not even for the first day did the new sociology professor show the
stealth usually associated with smuggled goods. He proclaimed his
philosophy with the compelling brilliance of a Broadway street sign. In
dealing with the problem of poverty, the subject of one course of
lectures, he offered socialism and the elimination of war as the only
salvation for a working population. His world, he explained, was not
Britain, but the toilers of every country, to whom he wished to give a
decent existence. His brotherhood idea was dazzling to the
intellectuals, who had long ago given up a religion which teaches the
same gospel. They rallied around Mr. Powys, Nora among them now, to
convert Petersfield.

In Nora’s dreams a very charming young man with flowing neckties
matching the deep blue of his eyes stood by her side in all her vigorous
plans to uplift her fellow men. During the class hour, too, she found
her attention wandering from Mr. Powys’s lecture to Mr. Powys himself.
His curly locks gleamed like sunshine in the bleak greyness of a winter
day. With his climaxes—and Mr. Powys made frequent use of climactic
effects—a ruddy glow coursed through his cheeks and made him as radiant
as a Greek god.

From Mr. Powys she learned rapidly the superior virtues of the poor,
their instinctive wisdom. For his pacifism she had a less immediate
reaction. The poor, being closer than fighting Europe, seemed enough to
begin on. Her intensive study of poverty and the stern precepts of the
twin science, economics, resulted in a chance to live during the month
of June in a New York settlement house situated in the most populous
section of the lower East Side.

Mr. Powys had made a similar experiment in surveying poverty at close
range.

‘While I was at Cambridge, I spent one long vac. in London. Down near
the docks, you know. An interesting region. For the depths of misery I
know no better place than Shadwell. Possibly Limehouse is a trifle
rougher. Of course there are no slums in New York equal to ours in
London.’


 III

Mary Howe, who believed in a varied human experience, convinced Nora
that it was her duty to attend Junior Prom. Mary provided one of her
superfluous party dresses and also Mr. Downing.

Importing men for Petersfield dances required elaborate manœuvres. Girls
with brothers were suddenly popular. The Prom crowd from the nearer
men’s colleges could be counted on annually for reënforcements. Any man
with fair skill in dancing, a dress suit, and the price of a railroad
ticket, could, if vouched for by a Petersfield girl or any remote social
connection of hers, be an acceptable guest.

Nora’s dancing partner was presented to her a few moments before the
grand march began. He danced well enough, did not immediately attempt
the persiflage that is common exchange at Proms, and had no conspicuous
deficiencies. Almost at once, however, he divined that Nora was one of
those strong-minded girls. Accordingly his first one-step was enlivened
by a running commentary on votes for women.

‘Of course they’ve got to serve in the army when they get the vote.’

‘But there aren’t going to be any more wars when women have the vote.’

Mr. Downing was much amused. ‘No more war. Of course women never have
any little wars among themselves. A woman’s tongue—why do we hear so
much about it?’

Nora looked stony. ‘You won’t hear any more from mine on this subject.’

Mr. Downing, being a polite youth, shifted the conversation to sport. He
let Nora know, without too much directness, that he was a long-distance
runner at his college, first stroke on the crew, and a player to be
reckoned with in tennis tournaments. Then he inquired about Nora’s
athletic prowess.

Nora wasn’t an entire success at the Prom. To men less obliging than Mr.
Downing she had little to say. The giggles of the successful girls
around her she had no marked ability to imitate. What was there to talk
about at a dance? And these college boys—they were like big clumsy
babies. Now if she had dared to ask Mr. Powys——Toward the end, the
muscles of her face ached in her efforts to keep up a battery of
cheerful smiles. She thought longingly of her bed as her feet rebelled
against the unaccustomed exercise.

So this was what girls called fun. They marked off the days on the
calendar until the supreme event of a college dance. They talked it over
for hours, days, weeks afterward.

The tiresome ordeal dragged to its conclusion. Nora gave Mr. Downing a
brisk good-night and hurried off to bed.

In the morning she had to hear the last sleepy trickles of reminiscence
about the dance. ‘Jack Downing said you were awfully good-looking,’ Mary
confided. ‘But I guess you were a little too high-brow for him. He
invited Sally Easton to his Prom after his first dance with her. Sally’s
an awful fool, but she’s pretty and she dances well. She thinks Jack is
just wonderful.’

The library was beautiful peace and security on a Saturday morning. Nora
hid herself away in the deserted seminar room and piled books all around
her.

Once more the monotonous college routine, as bad as the trench warfare
revealed by Professor Steele’s maps. The Eastern Front had become too
complicated even for undergraduate jokes.

Then May fête, delicious relief. A masque, reproducing in cheesecloth
and gilt paper the magnificence of the entertainments given in honor of
Queen Elizabeth, initiated a large proportion of the college into the
mysteries of dyeing and design. The fête, postponed for two days on
account of rain, finally came off the evening the news of the sinking of
the Lusitania reached Petersfield.

Final examinations mercifully obliterated the horror of the tragedy.


 IV

The glimpse of New York from the train window was all that Nora had yet
seen of the city, which was escape and adventure for Petersfield. The
tall apartment houses exposed to a traveller their shabby walls and the
dingy washings flapping on the roofs and in dark courts. The bedding was
spread out on the fire-escapes. Strange-looking people leaned out of the
windows, staring listlessly at the passing train. Children swarmed in
the streets, darted in and out of the traffic, as if they were playing a
grim game of life and death.

The settlement house, an old red-brick four-storeyed structure
indistinguishable from its neighbors, faced a street jammed with
pushcarts and the babble of foreign tongues. The children left off their
matching of wits with the vendor of chocolate and apples to crowd around
Nora and called out, ‘Teacher—Hello, Teacher.’

The residents of the settlement, gathered around the dinner-table,
presented strange contradictions to a newcomer. Mrs. Chambers, the
stately housekeeper, in a velvet dinner gown which revealed exquisite
shoulders and arms, was seated opposite the head resident, Miss Burton,
a weary-looking woman with a hard knob of grey hair perched close to her
forehead. Miss Saunders, a frivolous pink blonde elaborately dressed,
assisting Mr. Schwartz with the boys’ clubs, was an amusing contrast to
Miss Graham, in charge of the girls’ clubs, who came in after the soup
with her hair bearing the imprint of a hat worn all day.

While the others talked of their activities in a jargon full of terms
incomprehensible to Nora, Mrs. Chambers, in the fashion of a practiced
hostess, commented on the heat, the noise from the pushcarts, the
foreign quality of the East Side.

The street beyond Nora’s window was astir at one and two in the morning,
and children were still shrieking at their games. A queer odor like that
of old clothes stored in damp trunks filled her room, mixed with smells
of new food and seasonings. She longed for a single whiff of Maronac
Harbor or a stiff breeze from the Petersfield hills. Strange tongues
clattered in her ears. Then at last the neighborhood folded itself away
in its crowded dens, and Nora fell asleep.

The night classes, presided over by volunteer workers, met in the
schoolhouses of the district. Miss Graham assigned to Nora an animated
group of girls who seemed to be equipped with a knowledge of English
sufficient to talk about everything but the lesson. Having anticipated
the college generation in the generous use of rouge, they had the
appearance of buxom vitality, in spite of crowded living and working
quarters. Their main interest was to remind their teacher of the exact
time for the closing of the lesson, so that they could rush down to the
lower floor of the school building at the dramatic minute the boys were
being dismissed. An hour of dancing in the basement gymnasium followed
the classes. But the boys, in a neighborhood inclined to forensic
exercise, prolonged their debates long past the class hour. Often they
used the entire dancing period for a little extra oratory, and the girls
had to dance by themselves.

At the weekly settlement-house dances Miss Burton marched up and down
the two spacious rooms, probably once the drawing-rooms of a prosperous
Wall Street merchant, to keep the dancers from amorously remote corners.
Her lively interest in the courtship and marriage of the young people
made her eager to preserve a rigid system of etiquette which would
prevent any irregularity.

Even greater than her sympathy for young love was her enthusiasm about
babies. She was always capturing some clever young doctor and bringing
him to the clinic in the big bare room on the second floor of the house;
and she discovered nurses with the necessary variety of linguistic
equipment to pass on the doctor’s advice to the mothers. Miss Burton
herself presided over the weighing of the infants, as pleased over the
gains of the puniest ones as a juggler with some new feat. Women who had
never had children of their own seemed to love babies with the most
tender devotion. Cordelia had the same wistful interest as Miss Burton.
But the mothers of the numerous settlement progeny, while Miss Burton
was busy, displayed an alertness in collecting under their shawls the
free samples of babies’ food on display. They pushed and jostled each
other during the distribution of garments donated to the settlement, and
even grumbled at a huge share of the spoils.

Nora’s letters to Mr. Powys, written late at night, sometimes took on a
picturesque flippancy under her agile pen. Then she remembered the
ardent hopes which flashed from his beautiful eyes, and tore up what she
had written.

But contrary to his theories, Nora was sure that the poor had their
snatches of enjoyment. Only the settlement workers appeared to abstain
entirely from pleasure. The five older women, who during the summer
divided their time between the city house and a camp in the mountains,
were so tired that they were unable to respond to Mrs. Chambers’s
conventional ideas of dinner conversation. With all this propaganda
about the starving in Europe, their chief worry now was the possibility
that philanthropists would neglect the settlement. Mrs. Chambers
listened with a chilly aloofness.

After a tour of Fifth Avenue on her last day in New York, Nora, in the
home-going crowd of workers in the trolley, pondered once more over the
whole question of riches and poverty. Some of the poor she had lived
among were honorable and attractive. Others were not. It seemed to her
it would be easier to be good, if one had money. But here she knew she
was in unfamiliar country.

Nora experienced an unexpected pity when she said good-bye to her fellow
workers. Their courage in facing the problem of human misery was greater
than hers. Miss Burton and Miss Graham looked more exhausted every day.
Somehow they reminded her of Cordelia. The same unflagging devotion to
duty. The same sphere. She had expected that here in a great city, so
close to the beating of titanic forces, one became part of the bigness
of the world. But the borders of the settlement stretched no farther
than the parish of Saint Margaret’s or the peaceful groves of
Petersfield. Where did one find the strong winds of reality, the great
principles of a universal structure? One went on eagerly in youth, more
slowly in age, to find them, and they were always somewhere else.

Nora was only one of a long succession of college girls who had dreamed
of social service as her contribution to the regeneration of the world.
A few weeks brought disillusionment, and one had to try something else.


 CHAPTER III


 I

Nora began her last year at college by signing up for two of Professor
Steele’s stiffest history courses, her programme bolstered up further by
an advanced economics course. Mr. Powys still brought the elated glow of
poetry into a sordid world, but Nora suspected his lack of the solid
ballast of fact. She decided to drop sociology.

Ellen Standish had spent the summer trying to convert her father to her
theories concerning factory management. She had secured a rest-room for
the women workers, but before her other reforms he remained adamant. She
carried the record of her failure to Mr. Powys. He, too, had a cotton
manufacturer for a parent, with the same inability to see the light. A
comparison of fathers made delightful conversation. Ellen soon developed
so proprietary an attitude toward Mr. Powys that Nora could no longer
decently have a share of him. Ellen followed him about, called him
Michael in confidential asides, and was hopelessly in love, as Nora and
other members of the Socialist League could plainly see. All of her
former independent spirit was gone.

Mr. Powys was not immediately responsive. During these weeks of
unrestricted submarine warfare, he was making feverish trips to New York
almost daily, conferring with unseen powers, and giving Petersfield
meagre crumbs of his time and careless lectures, which alienated the
serious students. Through it all, Ellen boasted that no man was so
willing a martyr to ideas as Michael Powys.

He departed early in December on a ship dedicated to peace, its
passengers embarking for Europe over a mine-strewn sea with the
immediate intention of ending the war. Just how their project was to be
accomplished was not explained in detail. Ellen was sure that with
Michael Powys on board the peace ship would do something. Miss Steele
was reported to have remarked that she never expected to see the return
of her dove of peace. Mr. Powys had escaped the British army once, but
they would surely get him this time. She viewed the peace ship as
another Children’s Crusade, equally pitiful and ineffectual.

On the subject of Pacifism, the Socialist League finally split. Ellen
and her converts went their way. The others were now attacking the
college curriculum in stormy sessions in a dusty room in the library
tower.

The dissatisfaction with the present régime had started the previous
spring. Nora Deming and Mary Howe refused to take part in the annual May
fête. They would not even sit in the audience dressed as Elizabethan
ladies or gallants.

Mary Howe was once more repeating the objections she had expressed then.
‘I’m tired of all this play-stuff. I’ve done it for three years. I’ve
celebrated Christmas, Easter, and May Day quaintly until I’m bored
stiff. All the silly plays—every one of them silly—the elaborate
costumes, the rehearsals.’

‘You have said a mouthful,’ her audience applauded.

Mary went on. ‘All this college tradition——All freshman year I was
lugged out rainy mornings to sing songs. I stood in snow banks singing
songs, and in the boiling sun commencement week. I can’t sing. I don’t
want to try. And the tree-plantings, the flower-fêtes, the water-fêtes,
the snow-fêtes. What’s it all for?’

‘To keep little freshmen from getting homesick.’

‘To make us love dear old Petersfield.’

‘To make us come back as alumnæ and sing the same old songs fifty years
from now.’

‘I want to be let alone,’ concluded Mary. ‘I’m a woman. At least I ought
to be. My mother had me when she was my age.’

The remnant of the socialists proceeded, after the destruction of
college sentiment, to reform the entire institution. The regeneration of
the world was temporarily suspended.

‘Two years wasted on required algebra and science,’ one member began.

‘Why don’t they relate science to life? Botany—mostly deep-sea ferns.
And chemistry—I never saw the good of it. There isn’t a course in food
chemistry. Now biology—a good stiff course in physiology ought to be
required. I think I know where my stomach is. Midnight feeds taught me
that much. But I’m vague about the rest.’

A courageous member blinked a little, and then plunged into a problem
which she felt it her duty to lay before her friends. ‘Take marriage,
for example. I’ll swear half the girls in this college don’t know what
it means. I found out last year. And yet most of us——’

An eloquent pause emphasized the truth of her words. The socialists,
overcome by reticence and ignorance, tried to look blusteringly
experienced, and failed.

‘We ought to have lectures with charts,’ persisted the courageous
member. ‘They tried it at Wendover last year, and two girls fell into a
dead faint. It’s disgusting—humiliating.’

Nora shifted to safer ground. ‘We’re treated like children. The faculty
is afraid to speak out. You remember how Miss Norris discussed Byron’s
amorous affairs and Shelley’s. As if we were schoolgirls. Why mention
them at all if they’re scared? It was the same way with George Eliot in
the novel course. We don’t even study Swinburne.’

‘Or Nietzsche. In philosophy they’re so afraid we’ll lose our religion
they never get over apologizing for the Rationalists. The way we slide
over those gentlemen——’

‘In men’s colleges it isn’t that way,’ explained the sister of a Harvard
undergraduate.

‘Oh, men!’

‘Women’s colleges are all wrong.’

‘Coeducation is too distracting. I went to a mixed high school.’

‘If we only saw men oftener. I don’t mean at dances. To discuss
problems. If socialist leagues in all the Eastern colleges met three or
four times a year——’

‘You speak of the Millennium, my dear.’

The general conclusions of all such sessions were identical.
Petersfield, artificially detached from the currents and floods of real
existence, offered a false security which was no preparation for life.
Life—life—how that word echoed in all college talk. But how was a reform
to be reached? The trustees and the faculty, at least so far as they
could determine, venerated the unchanged institutions of almost fifty
years. Revolution was hopeless.


 II

For seniors the immediate problem was to begin thinking about earning a
living. With social service as a career dismissed, there seemed nothing
for Nora but teaching. If she could be paid to study history year after
year, not in Petersfield, but in a big stirring place like New York——But
such jobs did not seem to exist in the files of the college employment
bureau.

Nora woke up at night in a torment of perplexity. This question of a
future brought back all the ferment of her freshman year. The delights
of youth—she had long ago discovered the falsity of the common
sentimental attitude—she could never be as wretched as she was now.

Is a person waiting for a train happy? Youth is like that. No security.
Hopes of something better beyond. All the nervous tension of
anticipation is the daily portion of the young. They use their studies,
sports, dramatics, like narcotics, to dull these sharp pains. But it is
life they want, an undefinable experience, all the more tempting because
of its vagueness.

Nora had begun to sign application blanks for teaching jobs and to write
careful letters to prospective employers. But she could force no spirit
into these deadly preparations for the future.

She was listening carelessly one day to a lecture on Henry James, for
whom she had once experienced a temporary enthusiasm. Professor Whitson
had observed that his novels showed the writer’s tragic detachment from
life.

‘In studying his biography, one is reminded of a person outside looking
through a window at men and women in a brightly lighted room,’ the
lecturer continued.

In a flash Nora saw that at Petersfield there was for her not even a
view through a window. Her fellow socialists denounced their college for
failing to equip them for the world they now aspired to conquer. Their
airy arguments revolved in her troubled mind and stayed there. She had
spent almost four years in digging through subterranean passages which
in the end brought her no promise of light. The money her father had
left for her education was almost gone. What had all her labor brought
her?

In the meantime, there was the history essay contest which Nora had
entered. The prize of two hundred dollars was one of the few tangible
rewards of scholarship at Petersfield. Miss Steele had so organized the
requirements that the winner was generally recognized as the honor
student of her year. Nora’s subject, chosen after much trouble, was
‘British Empire Building.’ Those entering the contest had been assigned
a quiet alcove in the library, where they could read without
interruption. At one of the long tables Nora worked beside Amelia
Donaldson, driven into excessive study by a spinal curvature and
restless mental energy. Amelia was always there before Nora; her
short-sighted eyes close to her books never turned in her neighbor’s
direction. She permitted only the interruption of meals, wrinkled her
forehead at the scraping back of the chair next to hers. She was writing
an essay on the ‘African Slave Trade.’

Through the January blizzards, while the Verdun guns were crowding the
headlines of every daily paper, the two contestants went through one
document after another. By Easter vacation Nora had exhausted all the
material on her subject in the college library, as well as a disorderly
heap of Mr. Powys’s tracts left behind for the Socialist League. During
Easter vacation, to be spent visiting Mary Howe in New York, she planned
to use the public library.


 III

Mary lived in an ugly but comfortable brown-stone front off lower Fifth
Avenue. Her father, a rare specimen of American, had no definite
employment except that of enjoying himself. Her mother was kept occupied
in the nursery most of the day.

On the very first morning of her visit Nora started out in search of the
library. Mary was sleeping upstairs.

‘You’re beginning rather early,’ Mary’s father suggested, as he politely
accompanied his daughter’s guest to the front door.

At five o’clock Mary was waiting beside one of the lions at the entrance
to the library to conduct Nora to Greenwich Village.

‘Daddy and mother are dining out for a night or two,’ she said. ‘We must
make the best of this chance.’

The Dragon was Mary’s choice that evening. A green beast with an
elongated jaw decorated the outside door and the interior, and a green
color scheme emphasized the complexion of the presiding deity.
Waitresses in shepherdess costume, possibly the dragon’s victims, were
exchanging pleasantries with a tableful of young men when the two girls
entered.

‘Now you mustn’t act stiff, or you’ll spoil all the fun,’ warned Mary,
not explaining further what the fun was.

They sat down at the end of the room, with a full view of their
neighbors. Middle-aged women in outward appearance as sensible as the
Petersfield faculty formed a goodly proportion.

‘We’ve come too early,’ Mary sighed. ‘It ought to be more exciting than
this.’

Soon a dark young man accompanied by a woman not very young, with a
drooping hat, dangling earrings, and a large jade ring on her first
finger, sat down at their table. Mary nudged Nora. The young man,
simultaneously with a careful study of the menu, draped his arm over his
companion’s shoulders. Her hand reached out coyly to meet one of his.
They began to talk in unsuppressed tones, Mary keeping up a battery of
nudges at the most momentous stages in their conversation. The woman, an
eavesdropper gathered, was describing a condition resembling
intoxication which she had experienced at a Village dance the night
before. She had then performed courageous and unexpected feats, now
chronicled with some pride. When the young man’s soup arrived, he
dropped the hand he had been holding. He was very hungry. During the
wait for the next course, he became fond once more. Throughout the meal
he followed the same alternation of satisfying love and an appetite.
Nothing was allowed to interfere with his enjoyment of his dinner, which
his companion paid for.

‘You see,’ Mary announced proudly, like a Greek chorus. ‘That’s the way
it’s done.’

Soon the crowd shifted. At near-by tables young girls, like them dining
alone, were every moment being approached by Villagers in broad-brimmed
hats, flowing ties, and incipient beards. Mary sat in a feverish glow of
anticipation. At last one hovered near their table, measured the icy
glint in Nora’s eyes, and retired.

Mary turned upon her friend. ‘You’ll never get experience that way.’

‘Experience——Is this experience?’

‘Of course. You’re driving them off. Look this way.’ Mary assumed a
come-hither expression which sent Nora off into wild giggles. ‘Well—I——’
Mary sputtered helplessly. Then she opened her bag, brought out a box of
cigarettes, and passed them to Nora. Nora shook her head. Mary lit one.
It went out. She tried once more, coughed, and then succeeded in puffing
out a thin column of smoke. A table of boys next to them, not very
different from the Prom crowd at Petersfield, watched Mary, and then in
pantomime reproduced her struggles. Soon their table was in an uproar.
But Mary, occupied with an uneasy cigarette, held primly in the exact
centre of her mouth, smoked on, the wild young men keeping away, in
spite of her designedly shameless conduct.

For a second venture, Mary arranged to meet Eddie Gaston at his favorite
Italian restaurant south of the Square. She had made his acquaintance in
Maine the preceding summer, and to him she owed some of her most
inspiring ideas. A tall youth with a mop of curls, similar to the
ambrosial locks placed on most statues of Jove by enterprising
sculptors, leaned against the doorway of a stuffy little place opening
into a street full of explosive children, lean cats, and a strong smell
of garlic. He looked like a poet. There was a battered beauty in his
ashen face. His brown eyes had the soft melancholy usually associated
with an interest in rhythm. Best of all, he talked like a poet. At least
like Nora’s conception of one. His swift wonderful sentences were full
of words she had hitherto confronted only in her researches in Roget’s
Thesaurus and some contemporary æsthetic criticism lent her by Mary as a
preparation for meeting Eddie.

In the flow of these exquisite sentences Mary was giving a cibarious
concentration to the greasy menu card. ‘Yes, they’ve got ravioli. And
chicken and spumoni. Order the same, Nora. They’re all wonderful.’

Nora was following the elaborate processes of Mr. Gaston. He had first
extracted from his vest pocket a leather tube, which he snapped open,
revealing an amber cigarette holder reposing on a red satin pillow. He
then produced an elaborately designed cigarette case; and when his
cigarette was fitted neatly into its amber cap and lighted, he was free
to transfer his attention to the wine list. He politely consulted the
tastes of his companions. Nora’s ignorance drove her to prohibition.
Mary ordered _vin ordinaire_. A graceful sweep of Mr. Gaston’s arm
brought the waiter, a very tall and blond Italian, who belied the
popular conception of his race. ‘A bottle of _vin ordinaire_ and a
Martini cocktail.’ The cigarette in its holder Mr. Gaston gave an
impressive flourish.

At the conclusion of his cocktail, the poet began to question his guests
about poetry. He hoped they didn’t like Rupert Brooke.

‘We’re all reading him,’ Nora admitted. ‘We love him.’

‘War sentiment,’ Eddie pronounced. ‘Gibson is better. His realism—at
least there’s some not bad observation at times. But Brooke——Of course,
all the war poets!’ Again Eddie’s cigarette spun a reënforcing curvet.
‘My god is Dowson. Ernest Dowson.’ Eddie tipped up his glass. He was now
drinking some of Mary’s _vin ordinaire_.

‘I like Housman better,’ Nora said.

‘Oh!’ Eddie commented, his exclamation rising and falling in a variation
of polite disdain. Eddie, Nora suspected, always deliberately chose a
poet of his own. ‘But Davies is a rare spirit, a wilder singer.’

Mr. Gaston ordered a whiskey-and-soda. He next fingered a small glass
holding an emerald-green fluid. He held it up to the light, twisting and
turning it, until it shot out rays, and then drank it in lingering,
voluptuous sips.

‘“That I might drink, and leave the world unseen,”’ murmured Mr. Gaston.

This last teratological touch was too much for Nora, fed on historical
documents and crammed daily with the horrors of a contemporary sphere.

‘I’m afraid even drinking won’t do that,’ she said with schoolgirl
bluntness.

But Mr. Gaston had now ordered a large cigar, which he was trimming with
a very new shiny cigar-cutter.

‘Oh,’ protested Mary. ‘I wanted you to recite some of your own poetry,
Eddie.’

Eddie’s delicate features, before the columnar substantiality of the
cigar, had the wraith-like evanescence of the spirals of smoke
gambolling over his curls. He faded away into complete silence. Mary was
describing the horrors of her mother’s parties. Both girls were suddenly
conscious of Eddie’s unresponsiveness.

‘I’m afraid we’re boring you, Eddie,’ Mary said.

Eddie was very pale. Every moment he was paler. He became a sickly
green. He was staring in front of him, one hand clutching an empty wine
glass. He made a valiant effort to be polite. ‘No—I—I’m not bored. I’m
only drunk.’ He rubbed his hand over his head, then half rose. ‘If—if
you’ll excuse me,’ he said hurriedly.

Mary’s monologue had come to an abrupt stop. Her eyes had the round,
questioning gaze of wonder. Eddie got out of his chair and fled.

‘I’m going to leave this minute,’ Nora announced.

‘No, you can’t. He’s ill, poor boy. We’ll have to wait until he feels
able to go home. Maybe’—Mary’s face shone with excitement—’we’ll have to
go home with him.’

‘I won’t.’

‘He may be in such a state he can’t go alone.’ Mary suggested hopefully.

‘I won’t go.’

‘You wouldn’t desert a poor young man that drank a little too much. He
gets so intense writing poetry. He’s just like ashes. I saw him that way
in Maine. Then afterward——’

‘He gets drunk, I suppose.’

‘Poets have always led very irregular lives. You know that, Nora.’

‘He’s disgusting.’

‘You wait until he recites some of his verse. It’s wonderful. You’ll
forget everything.’

Mr. Gaston’s return cut off any further argument. He sat down heavily.
When his glance fell upon his half-smoked cigar, he turned green all
over again. Hurriedly he summoned the waiter for a whiskey-and-soda.
Nora flashed signals to Mary, who tried to keep her attention safely
directed elsewhere. Nora’s chair dug at her back. Her head ached. She
had been shut up in this vile place hours—hours with the tormenting
length of weeks, years. Some Italians at the next table had started a
shouting din of song. A man near the window was trying to quarrel with
the waiter. Eddie Gaston continued to stare in front of him. In another
moment, if he didn’t make a second hasty flight, Nora knew he was going
to be sick. A low groan. Nora now felt a dizzy swell overtaking her. The
remnants of Eddie’s cigar sent up sickening fumes. She couldn’t stand it
any longer. She lifted her limp body out of her chair. Mary pulled her
back with a jerk.

‘More whiskey,’ Eddie demanded.

‘He needs it to set him up,’ Mary whispered. ‘Nora, if you——’

Mary heroically started a cheerful monologue, Nora staring at Eddie
opposite her, surely now in the lowest depths of sickness. But the
whiskey gave him a sudden reviving spurt. He straightened up in his
chair, patted his curls, turned his head cautiously. Mary seized the
favorable moment to nod to Nora that Eddie was, for the time being,
restored.

‘Well,’ Mary remarked tactfully, ‘perhaps we might be going.’

Eddie’s attention was directed to a cigarette still clasped by its amber
holder. He reached for it, gathered together all his scattered powers,
and, after an uncertain fumble or two, abstracted the one from the
other, got the holder back into its case and into the correct pocket.

‘Well,’ sighed Eddie with the satisfaction of a real conquest.

The waiter cut short the beginning of a feeble argument as Mr. Gaston
was paying the bill. He got the young ladies and the poor young
gentleman into their coats and conducted them to the door. Mary hailed a
taxi-cab mercifully pausing at the corner and gave a strange address.
During the short ride, Mr. Gaston began to recite the sonnet which had
come to him only that morning. Yes, he was sure it was that morning.

 No murmur from beyond the iron gate
 That guards the world of deeds steals here, no rue
 For burning words or anger’s bitter brew:
 We are immune from sorrow, now, and hate.
 Let cool winds find my throat insatiate;
 Let glaucous hollows gleam as the retinue
 Of pale gold sunrise-clarions sound elate;
 Let keen blue swords of beauty cleave me through!

 So calm comes dropping slowly down, and sorrows,
 Desires unsated, melt like fevered dreams
 From days untroubled and unstirred tomorrows:
 In one dissolved wave of peace there streams
 Our mingled effluence; and, clinging girdless,
 We greet the dripping dawn with lips drowned wordless.

He went on and on. The search for beauty, the futility of wasted youth,
the bitter bitterness of love—one felt all this in the succession of
verses which poured from his lips.

When the taxi stopped at Mr. Gaston’s door, he turned to Nora. ‘I’m
going to begin life on a higher plane,’ he told her, ‘a higher plane.
I’ll begin to-morrow. A poet—his sacred duties—his——’

‘I think he’d better,’ Nora agreed, as she watched him climb the front
steps, clinging to the rail. ‘He can’t begin any too soon.’

‘I hope he gets upstairs to his room safely,’ said Mary. ‘He’s very
poor. He lives by writing—articles for the papers—book criticisms and a
poetry column. It’s killing for a poet—that kind of drudgery.’

‘He ought not drink up his money.’

‘He’s a real genius. The only one I’ve ever known. But mother won’t let
him come to the house. He came once when——’

‘I don’t believe I appreciate genius.’

‘No, you don’t, Nora. Here you had a great chance to—to——’

But Nora never saw Eddie Gaston again. And what is more significant,
never heard of him again. Mary Howe’s experiences went that way.


 IV

The morning after the May baskets had been hung on the doors of faculty
and class officers with the singing of appropriate Elizabethan melodies,
Nora was summoned to Professor Steele’s office.

Miss Steele placed a chair for Nora in front of her desk. ‘Miss Deming,
the committee has read your essay. It puzzled me, as well as the others,
that a young girl in the midst of this present emotional crisis should
be—so—so detached.’

Nora tried to evade Miss Steele’s direct glance. ‘I—I can’t help it,’
she faltered. ‘I’ve always been detached.’ She swallowed hard. ‘I—maybe
it’s because—after my father and mother died—I felt detached.
Cordelia—the friend who brought me up—she’s very good. But she’s
detached, too.’

Miss Steele looked down quickly toward the papers on her desk. ‘I wasn’t
criticizing you,’ she said. ‘I meant to give you high praise. The
committee awarded you the prize unanimously. It is excellent work.
Remarkable for an undergraduate.’ Miss Steele cleared her throat. ‘It
made me decide——We want you in our department next year as an
instructor. It will mean some drudgery at first, years of study, and
then——’

‘But I—I’m not good enough.’

Miss Steele rose and went over toward the window. ‘You are interested in
modern history, aren’t you? And in its foundations? I’ve followed your
work the last two years carefully. Except for a temporary aberration in
sociology’—Miss Steele’s face twisted into a reluctant smile—’you have
been the only student with no bias—no hasty conclusions. Of course it
means you are an historian.’

‘I—I don’t know.’

‘But you mustn’t go into it—into historical study—with the idea that—it
is hard to tell you.’ Miss Steele seemed to be tortured with the duty of
a shamed confession. ‘I must warn you—before you decide—you mustn’t make
my fatal error. Since the war began—they—my colleagues saw the
change—that I——But I couldn’t explain. For years I had been embracing a
fatal delusion.’

‘You thought there wouldn’t be any more wars.’ Nora leaped to the truth
without waiting for Miss Steele.

‘Exactly.’ The older woman’s voice was dry and hard. ‘It has been the
folly of my generation to take evolution too seriously. Unconsciously I
applied it to history. As I studied the past, I saw the world passing
through various evolutionary stages—that slowly—then—perhaps in my time
war would go—and then——’

‘But Greece and Rome—and Japan.’

‘Yes, I forgot them. I was a student of modern Europe. I didn’t go far
enough back. And I limited myself more and more to that patch of western
Europe which for me had become the world. I forgot the Balkans and
Russia and Turkey. Of course it was all childishness.’

Yes, Nora could understand now why they thought her essay was detached.
She hadn’t forgotten certain things. But it was remembering—not having
delusions—which made one feel so alone sometimes.

‘So you must have no hopes, my child. It’s my duty to warn you. The
world will go on making the same errors. Not even different errors. We
record the same stupid folly. Our function is to record—to interpret.
But if you start out—not a dupe—the sweep of the past, the glimpse of
the present—watching it—like a spectator at a play——’

‘But I——’ From these chilly heights Nora in the warm ardor of her youth
shrank back afraid.

‘Will you join us? I’m beginning to—please don’t think me foolish—the
war—I’m not young any more. I feel so tired. I want some one young to
start where I left off. You’—Miss Steele reached for Nora’s hand and
gave it a stiff, embarrassed jerk—’Nora, I want you to go on with my
work. There is no one else here I trust. I——’

Nora’s eyes, from whose burning directness her contemporaries looked
away sometimes, were lifted to Miss Steele’s face, a hard, furrowed face
which had attempted to build up fortifications for a too sensitive and
timid spirit. Miss Steele, so aloof on her lecturer’s platform, so
self-sufficient in her study, producing scholarly works, Nora had
admired. But this weary old woman reaching out for the sturdy support of
some one young, she loved.

‘I—I’ll do what I can,’ Nora heard herself saying in a clear, ringing
voice. ‘If you want me, I’ll come.’

Hurrying across the campus, with the spring breeze spreading out her
muslin dress like a cloud, she had the swift upliftment of a young
immortal entrusted with an urgent message for a stupid world. She passed
Faculty House, where three deliberate, stiff-gaited professors were
walking back and forth involved in a discussion of some infinitesimal
college business evidently carried away from a committee meeting. Their
academic tones, which years of the process of delivering authoritative
information produce, swept Nora down to the baffling indecisions of the
mortal plane. In an instant of rash folly she had dedicated herself to a
future represented by the women she saw before her.

She turned and fled from the campus. To the east a path led to open
country, with fruit trees in their pale green fringing the hillside. Up
toward the hilltop she ran, breathless and terrified. What had she done?
Why? She didn’t want any more of Petersfield. She must escape—go far
away. As she looked behind her, the regular ranks of red-brick buildings
were like prison walls, waiting to enclose her, to keep the fresh air
away from her, rob her of youth and hope. No, she wouldn’t. She would
tell Miss Steele she had changed her mind. She wanted to do something
else—something big and free. But what? Against this wall Nora’s wild
spirit beat. She reached the hilltop and threw herself down in the grass
under a sprightly apple tree flaunting the luxuriance of its prime.

In the college paper the winner of the history essay was announced as
Nora Deming, class of ’16, of Maronac Harbor, Wisconsin, who had been
appointed to an instructorship in history. This official bulletin of
campus news, every year close to its last gasp of an existence bounded
by college seasons, was crowded with interesting items. Below Nora’s
honor was spread out the array of college engagements. The class of ’16
displayed a roster of unusual length. Among the names was that of
Eleanor Porter, engaged to Fuzzy’s brother, athletic like Fuzzy, and so
solidly established in his father’s automobile business that they could
be married at once. Petersfield had also disposed of Eleanor’s future in
less formal fashion than Nora’s, but with equal sureness.

On the evening that Nora’s honors were published, Mary Howe beat against
Nora’s door. ‘Come to Amelia’s room quick. She’s carrying on fearfully
about not getting the prize. Almost hysterical, I guess. She wants to
see you.’

Amelia sat huddled over her study table, her eyes red and swollen. ‘Why
did you take it away from me?’ she accused Nora. ‘You didn’t try so
hard. And I—I didn’t have anything else. You—you with everything.’

Nora stood rigid in front of Amelia, quite untouched by pity. ‘How do
you know I had anything else?’ she demanded. Then, turning away, she
closed Amelia’s door between them.

Nora marched in the long procession of academically gowned seniors to
receive her diploma. She was a B.A. from Petersfield, who shook hands
with Prexy and received the congratulations of the faculty. Her next act
was to superintend the moving of her battered furniture to the wing of
Faculty House occupied by the young instructors, known among themselves
as ‘scrub faculty.’ She stood in the barren room for a moment. This,
then, was life, of which her Socialist League had so feelingly spoken.
So extravagant a waste of words over so little.


 V

In Maronac, Nora found that the war had already laid its insidious touch
upon the long-established peace of the American and German settlement.
Once more the river formed an impassable boundary. The Germans on the
south side of town, who sent money to their starving relatives abroad,
were bewildered by the strange tales circulated against their
birthplace. Their sympathies soon became the subject of much bitter talk
on the north side. They were traitors, aliens, ungrateful for all the
United States had given them. Father Stokesbury had been in a training
camp the last time he wrote to Cordelia. Britain needed more soldiers.
He might be in the trenches by this time. To Cordelia the war was now a
holy cause.

But Cordelia still continued her Sunday visits to Della. She and Mrs.
Schuster had domestic battles to settle. Della’s mother was pushing her
old-maid daughter into a marriage with a widower, the father of three
children.

‘It’s all Della hears,’ scolded Cordelia. ‘He’s a good enough man, but
he’s twenty years older. I know he is.’

‘My Della’s bein’ courted by a goot German man,’ Mrs. Schuster announced
to Nora on her first visit. ‘A fine fella.’ She wore the cheerful
expression of triumph which confirmed Cordelia’s worst fears.

‘But who will take care of the garden if Della leaves you?’ teased Nora.

‘I’ll have some goot grass. All them flowers—they’re no use to me.’

‘But living all alone——’

Mrs. Schuster only grinned. ‘I’ll keep the parrot. He’s a goot boy.’

The parrot shrieked back, ‘Della old maid—old maid.’

‘Silly boy,’ Mrs. Schuster said. ‘Silly boy.’

By the end of the summer Della was married in Saint Margaret’s, her
three stolid step-children in the front pew with Mrs. Schuster staring
solemnly at the bride in a long veil and all the conventional
habiliments of a wedding ceremony. In the pew with Cordelia and Nora sat
Amy Henderson, taking an hour off from Weyhausen’s to see her old fellow
worker on the Altar Guild married. If Amy had bitter memories as she
looked at Della’s bridal array, they were hidden by the elegance of a
blue crêpe dress which gave her the vivid pink freshness of her
girlhood. A salary of twenty-five dollars a week, an unprecedented sum
for a woman to earn in Maronac, may have helped her to forget. Old Mr.
Weyhausen recognized real capability. Lizzie Guildford occupied her
usual place with the same elegant superiority, never looking in the
direction of her neighbor. Except for her studied hostility, one would
think she had entirely forgotten the tragedy which joined her to
Cordelia. Father Stokesbury’s successor, a timid little Cockney
hurriedly produced by the Bishop, mumbled the service at galloping
speed, and soon the wedding guests were leaving the church.

At the next wedding in Saint Margaret’s Nora was one of Eleanor’s
bridesmaids. Mrs. Porter after much trouble had persuaded the Bishop to
read the service. He was very feeble, and his voice broke several times.

Nora found Cordelia stretched out in the Bishop’s chair when she
returned from the wedding breakfast. ‘I hope I won’t be going to another
wedding for a long time.’

Cordelia looked up. ‘Yes—two this summer. But Mrs. Porter is so
relieved. There was no one for Eleanor here.’ She turned away from Nora.
‘There’s no young man that you——’

‘No,’ Nora answered sternly.

‘You think you’ll like to—to—stay on there where it’s mostly women.’

‘I’ve got my work to do,’ Nora reminded her.

‘Yes, work—it helps. But——’ Cordelia’s fingers fluttered over a letter
on the library table. Nora saw her gather it up, and conceal it under
her white apron. ‘I heard this morning—from Father Stokesbury. He’s in
France close to the front—any time——’

Nora shrank from the revelation in Cordelia’s face. ‘It can’t touch
him,’ she said. The memory of her girlish dreams made a swift tug at her
heart. ‘Any one so—so alive. He can’t die.’

Cordelia had stumbled toward the hall door, and was gone.


 CHAPTER IV


 I

In late September a mob of freshman girls surged into the history
amphitheatre where Professor Steele every year delivered the two general
lectures which introduced first-year students to a prescribed diet of
‘history survey.’ Nora, sitting in the front row with the other members
of the department, experienced a fresh stab of panic as each new group
passed through the swinging doors. Out of this mass she was to build her
future.

The first lecture was a dramatic and brilliant description of Rome
before the barbarian invasions. In rapid summary Miss Steele presented
the elements of its power, its political organization (which embraced a
territory curiously similar, Nora observed, to the present possessions
of the Allied armies, now that Russia was falling away), the amenities
of life, the heights of culture reached by its citizens with their
triumphs in architecture, sculpture, and literature. She then descended
to the weakness of this apparently invincible empire; held together by
no actual unity, dependent upon mercenary armies, forbidding political
discussion, enjoying luxuries bought with slavery and the misery of the
common people, sinking into a general intellectual decadence,
indifferent to science, indulging in the vain shows of gladiatorial
combats. A comparison of this ancient decay, concerning which none of
the freshmen, with the bloom of health and home still upon them, seemed
to be greatly excited, might have been made with Rome’s modern
successor. But Miss Steele’s audience, not yet inured to the fatigue of
hour-lectures, were beginning to look toward the clock.

On the following day Professor Steele devoted her time to a description
of the modern states of Europe as they were before the barbarian
invasions. West of the Rhine dwelt ‘Chaos and old Night.’ Italy, France,
Spain, and England, even beyond Hadrian’s Wall, all possessed some of
the culture emanating from Rome. Then the barbarians came. At the name
of the Huns, so recently revived in popular memory, even the most
indifferent of the note-takers lifted an inquiring head. Greece had
fallen before Rome, Rome before the Vandals and the Huns. Miss Steele
went no farther. If the painful building of a new civilization were to
go the same way, the autumn of 1916 had not yet made clear.

The bell rang, the freshmen scrambled toward the door, breaking the
silence with the scraping of chairs and the clatter of released tongues.
The Roman schoolboy, informed of the approach of wild tribes from the
North, was probably equally indifferent to the great catastrophe.
Petersfield freshmen, involved in the intricacies of a crowded
book-store and new subjects, and suffering general geographical
bewilderment in strange scenes, had no time to contemplate the future of
Europe.

After the two lectures, Nora was assigned her portion of the history
flock. Professor Steele, who had no theories about coddling the
apprentices in her department, stated that freshman history, making a
general survey of the foundations of the modern world, was a liberal
education for her young instructors, and she gave them four sections.

Twenty-five or thirty untamed girls tumbling into a classroom appeared
to Nora not as human beings differing little from herself only four
years ago, but as fillers of chairs, with feet that always moved, as
rows of staring, sneering eyes in faces fixed in a succession of
grimaces at the slightest possible excuse for amusement. Nora heard a
high, unnatural voice going on and on by means of some kind of internal
machinery she was not conscious of directing. To her surprise these
meaningless and jumbled sentences were being taken seriously by the
restless throng before her and transcribed into note-books. By the end
of the first day she went to bed with pains shooting all over. By
morning she collected her jaded powers and crawled through another day.

Across the hall Mr. Powys had gathered a fresh crop of sociological
enthusiasts about him and was injecting into them his brand of hope for
the world. When Nora reached a painful pause, she heard his voice
ringing out with the old vigor she remembered.

Mr. Powys’s return was a surprise to all Petersfield. He was a drooping
peace dove. His blond hair had a kind of limp dejection which reminded
Nora of a young chick rescued from a hearty spring rain. From Mr. Powys
she got no succinct information of the peace ship or its manœuvres. He
had done his duty, and now he must have a moment or two to recover
before he decided on the next desperate venture. He brightened only when
Nora mentioned Ellen Standish.

‘Miss Standish has been wonderful—wonderful. So sympathetic. She made me
feel that my coming back mattered—that I was really needed here. I
couldn’t have returned without some such hope.’

Nora finished at last the agony of her first week of teaching, with the
blessed interval of Saturday and Sunday for recovery. In the meantime
fellow instructors took the burden of their jobs with a nonchalant
toleration, conversing light-heartedly at meals, which they consumed
with a normal quantity of enjoyment. They at least represented the
promise of a brighter future.

In her room all of Saturday Nora sat bent over a table heaped with books
dealing with the barbarian invasions. The volumes of Gibbon lay in a
pile on the floor beside her. Each fresh tome brought new confusion to
the task of reducing these spreading hordes within the confines of two
recitations. It was like arranging an animated antheap in some kind of
law and order.

Miss Ewing across the hall, a small dark woman from Georgia, with a
sallow face which suggested perpetual malaria, frequently interrupted
Nora’s labors.

‘Don’t work too hard, my dear,’ she warned her daily. ‘Take it all
comfortable and easy.’

Miss Ewing herself viewed life as a race. She had a bounding step close
to a run which carried her across the campus in a loping trot, precious
to undergraduate humor. An intellectual eagerness brought a flush to her
dark face, a sparkle to eyes of an otherwise commonplace grey, and drove
her to accomplish a phenomenal amount of labor. She was a
bacteriologist, with a love for science which gave her the solace of
religion, even though it had cut her off from a sheltered life and the
conventional marriage sacred to Georgian traditions.

She left her germs one Saturday afternoon to take Nora on a long country
walk. She swung off with her characteristic speed, not halting in the
ascent of the steepest hills. She darted into blackberry hedges to
follow a strange bird and chased after the moths which fluttered in
front of her.

When darkness settled upon the two, they stood on the brow of a hill
many miles from Petersfield.

‘Good evening, Vega, my dear,’ Miss Ewing said, looking up toward a
blue-white star above them. Then with a fine disregard of the sacredness
of the dinner hour in Faculty Hall, she pointed out, as they trudged
along, the serpents, dragons, dogs, kitchen utensils, and adventurous
human beings in which stars conveniently arrange themselves. Nora went
to sleep that night thinking of the Great Square of Pegasus in the
northeastern sky.

But on the next morning began a fresh contest with the swift march of
events in a history survey course. While the freshmen still followed the
last straggling barbarian invader, their teacher was a lap ahead in the
tangles of Christianity. In the distance of two or possibly three days
loomed the great figure of Charlemagne and a powerful Papacy.

Nora, in a dash from her classroom to the library to anticipate other
cub history instructors using the same books, collided one day with
Professor Steele, who was writing another book and therefore gathering
material with the greed of a squirrel carrying away nuts to his hole.
The head of the department looked very cross at her newest instructor.
The undergraduates had learned to give Professor Steele plenty of space
in her blind plunges from her study to the library, and her classroom.
She counted on their indulgence.

‘Well, Miss Deming,’ she snorted, straightening her hat.

Since her interview with Nora the preceding spring, she had given her
neither advice nor assistance. In choosing Nora Deming, she had paid her
the compliment of standing above the need of either.

But if Miss Steele had measured the despairing uncertainty of the shy
young person who hurried away from her, she might have spared a minute
from her documents. After the sureness of her powers a few months
earlier, Nora had descended to a pitiful and feverish questioning of her
ability to transcribe what she knew on the slippery surface of the
freshman mind. After a glowing account of the salvation of learning by
the Christian Church—and Nora was always glowing when she presented her
latest mental conquest—a shaking hand had risen before her
half-conscious gaze. ‘But isn’t this the Dark Ages we’re studying?’

It was Molly Abbott, with curly hair and a very charming smile, who had
read history virtuously in the library the night before instead of
attending freshman sing-song practice, for which act she was fined
twenty-five cents. The freshman has a love for anti-climax, for a
question which makes her appear to her instructor not as one of the
herd, but as an individual.

How is that great chasm between the teacher and the learner to be
bridged, especially where the one in authority is new in power and
undisciplined in gauging the mental stretches of her victims? What young
pedagogue has not started out with great intellectual ambitions,
determined to admit no compromise, to make all who should learn burn
with a zeal for learning? How does the spark strike fire?

Nora’s freshmen shook their heads and looked silly when she asked them
searching questions. They complained that she worked them too hard,
expected too much outside reading. Some of them cultivated for classroom
use a sullen, rebellious expression. And before them, Nora knew, lay the
peaks of the Renaissance and the Reformation. Nora was now grappling
with the Crusades.

As her infant historians tumbled out of the classroom, she pondered over
the state of their minds. Were they looking down upon the world with the
detachment of an aviation photographer whose lens reproduced the exact
proportions of hills and valleys? Or were they stuck in some morass of
detail from which they could not escape without her help? What
philosophy of history were they constructing for themselves? And what
philosophy had she, herself, harassed by the thought of keeping a lesson
or two ahead?

Did she want to go on? Did she like to teach? Sometimes at night, when
she was very tired, so tired that she felt sure a few hours of sleep
could never bring back the necessary vigor for a new day, she dreamed of
an existence better than this fretful haste. Cool, deliberate joy—where
did one find it? Reflection in sunny places?

Then she was very lonely. She still had the herd instincts of her
undergraduate days, and her instructorship so elevated her above the
student plane that even her old associates in the Socialist League
examined her with an appraising eye. In her classes she had squeezed the
humanity from the frigid young person the freshmen saw. They passed her
over in their search for a deity, and found Mr. Powys and Miss Goodwin.
All of her faculty contemporaries were as busy as she. Miss Ewing’s
bacteria made her their slave. There was no companion for a chance
interval.

Miss Abington, a next-door neighbor, attracted Nora. She was a shy woman
who nodded to the new history instructor when she passed her in the
hall, but for weeks went no farther. She looked very unhappy, unlike
Miss Ewing, whom science completely satisfied. Miss Abington, who taught
art, wore a green smock, moccasins of dull green suede, and her coils of
blond hair were confined by green velvet bands. She was generally
followed by her two cats, a huge Maltese and a tortoiseshell, against
which the older members of the faculty muttered dangerous threats. But
according to rumor Miss Abington possessed a special dispensation signed
by the President to keep her pets as a preventive against the nervous
depression she suffered from constant association with human beings.

Miss Abington finally invited Nora to tea one rainy Sunday afternoon. In
her own rooms her shyness suddenly left her, and she was a delightful
hostess. Her sitting-room wall was covered with her own work; portraits
of cats principally, but also paintings of flowers, and of gardens full
of sunshine, green spaces, flowers seen through tall grasses, with the
suggestion of a cat lurking in the shadows.

‘Yes,’ Miss Abington admitted, as Nora walked soberly around the room,
‘my two passions are flowers and cats. First, cats.’ To reënforce her
words the Maltese stalked out of Miss Abington’s bedroom toward Nora.

Nora backed toward the door, an uncomfortable chill passing down her
spinal column. ‘But I don’t like cats,’ she had to confess. ‘I’d rather
be in the room with a tiger.’

‘And this is Francis Bacon—a perfect gentleman.’ Miss Abington grabbed
her pet. ‘There. He weighs twenty pounds. Tremendous. And here is my
other child.’ The tortoise cat twisted itself over the rug. ‘Martin
Luther. They have terrific battles. Science and religion. Eternal
conflict. I named them first, and the pugnacity developed later.’

Nora now stood against the door, ready to spring out into the hall if
either feline made friendly advances. Miss Abington gathered science and
religion into the folds of her smock and carried them into her bedroom.

As they drank tea, Miss Abington confessed that the tragedy of life lay
for her in the fact that she spent her time talking about painting
instead of painting.

‘But you have painted a lot.’

‘Summers. And very badly. In three months one can do very little. And in
this academic atmosphere one sacrifices the emotions to the intellect.’

‘But your intelligence doesn’t help you to paint?’

‘Oh, no. I know too much to be a good painter. So I chatter about art to
crowds of girls who will clutter up museums and repeat my twaddle.’

The paintings of cats were beginning to have a delicate bloom of fame in
New York galleries. Miss Abington confessed that she kept nineteen cats
in her summer studio, none of them handsome, but all ‘so delightfully
cat-like.’ She studied every changing expression with the ceaseless
patience of a portrait painter until each animal had an individuality of
feature and soul transferred to the canvas. But being a tactful and very
kind woman, she kept cats out of her conversations with Nora. She
herself had a similar aversion to moths, marked by an almost morbid
sensitiveness to their destruction by frost as the autumn came on. As
she passed a street lamp at night with the ghostly creatures still
fluttering in steadily decreasing numbers about the light, she whispered
to Nora, ‘No, I can’t stand it. I can’t watch them suffer.’ Rebellious
