against the slow methods of hard weather and starvation, she made wild
plans of her own for their quick death; but it was difficult to
circumvent Nature. ‘Even man goes the same slow way,’ she admitted. ‘But
I shall take swift means to end myself. I don’t consider it a sin.’

The destruction of mankind in Europe was daily being accomplished by
means other than famine and hard weather. Nora was now reading books on
the Industrial Revolution. In England greedy squires had encouraged
their laborers to have large families for the benefit of the estate.
Mill workers viewed a numerous progeny as a means for larger aggregate
earnings. High food and low wages fought the old battles. A surplus
population spreading over a great empire had not afforded enough relief
for the little island groaning under its burden of people. Was Malthus,
whom Victorian sentimentalists had resented so violently, right after
all, and must the world have these periodic checks of famine,
pestilence, and war to keep down the swarming millions?

In the midst of Nora’s speculations came the news that the United States
had declared war against Germany. The students, who since Nora’s time
had vacillated between indifference and spasmodic sentiment, became
martial overnight. The Socialist League gave up its last feeble flicker
of pacifism. Nora’s freshmen grew restless and indifferent. In their
schoolgirl circle was the prospect now of brothers and friends in
officers’ training camps. The boys they had danced with at West Point a
week ago were now glorified, heroic men. Among the faculty, enthusiasm
for the war was somewhat tempered by sober considerations of the
thousands of dead in Europe.

Amelia Donaldson was arrested in New York for making anti-draft speeches
on the street. The professor of English literature, Miss Hays, had
settled Amelia as a teacher in the New York Labor College which she had
sponsored from its start. She told every one that Amelia was at last
very happy. But with the declaration of war, Amelia saw the students she
was giving economics and general culture snatched from her for bayonet
practice. There was nothing for her, then, but soapbox oratory.

Petersfield got first-page headlines in the New York papers. Amelia in
jail gave defiant interviews to all the reporters. Mr. Powys made a
flying trip to New York to furnish bail. He rescued her from martyrdom.
Amelia must be reserved for greater struggles.

His action divided campus opinion. The most excitable faculty members
wanted a ‘dangerous alien’ removed. Several of the trustees got stirred
up. But Miss Steele put aside her manuscripts for a few days to make a
fight to save him. In action she was a forceful figure, with unexpected
strategies in managing the trustees and a barrage of sarcasm to crumple
her faculty foes. Miss Hays carried Amelia off to a labor colony for the
summer with the hope that the problems of education would keep her from
street-corner agitation.


 II

By fall Petersfield had stepped in line to help win the war. Both
faculty and students in a large room in the science building scraped
bandages and prepared other hospital supplies. Red Cross drives consumed
most of the undergraduate energy. Miss Goodwin was coaching a play, the
proceeds to go to war relief. Every evening the faculty met in the
parlor of Faculty House to learn to knit. Women who had previously spent
all of their evenings in the laboratory or the library now bent over
wooden needles held awkwardly, making clumsy stitches which generally
had to come out. Mufflers in a variety of ripples and waves, sweaters
too large or too small for the average soldier, socks that remained in
the same indefinite stage of completion tumbled from under stiff
professorial fingers. At the same time silly girls of eighteen or
nineteen were achieving remarkable results in purling and fancy stitches
with little effort. Intellectual and manual skill, sadly enough, seldom
travel together.

Gradually the custom developed for one of the women to read while the
others worked for their country. To convert the American public to a
state of mind demanded by conflicts involving both hemispheres, amazing
chronicles were appearing, combining the realism of wholesale
destruction with a sentimental patriotic appeal. Soldier-authors, who
described in detail the putrefaction of death on the battlefield, the
torture of victims in barbed-wire entanglements, the wrecks of men in
the hospital beds, also recorded the spiritual elation of war, the hopes
of a new world, the glorious philosophy of death. War humor developed a
little more slowly. Untainted realism was still far from advisable.

From these readings Miss Steele was notably absent. She gathered all war
books into her phrase ‘garbage,’ declared she did not know how to knit,
and was too busy to learn. Miss Abington stole quietly away from the
first knitting meeting enlivened by war literature.

‘I can’t bear it,’ she whispered to Nora with a twisted, tortured smile.
‘I must try to think of other things.’

As the winter went on and the faculty staged grim contests in knitting
the greatest number of sweaters and socks, needles clicked steadily
through faculty meetings, Miss Steele looking on with scornful
amusement. Unlike the other professors she did not permit students to
knit in her classes. They quoted her as saying that a little information
about the past was not amiss in these troubled times, which she refused
to complicate further by the mental disease of divided attention.
Clearly enough she was not a patriotic woman.

Petersfield girls were giving a large share of their time to being
patriotic. They threw themselves whole-souled into each new drive and
fad, were photographed in Red Cross uniform, cracked jests over the
novelty of meatless and wheatless days, and made picturesque use of the
latest mob developments. When the march of events dragged somewhat, they
read war verse. Rupert Brooke had fallen before unworthy rivals
truckling in cheap and hysterical emotion. There was much
letter-writing, much pride in the possession of the photographs of
potential heroes. The trains were crowded with girls going to New York
to say good-bye to fathers, brothers, cousins, lovers, leaving for
distant camps. Handsome uniformed boys on leave appeared on the campus.

The old life could not go on in the same way. The faculty looked on with
a romantic sympathy for young love making holiday, blind to the
consequences of lavish permissions granted the undergraduates to spend
the day in New York. Not all went there to deliver tearful farewells to
lovers embarking for France. Some consoled themselves for want of lovers
by a day of change and adventure. They prowled about in strange places,
investigated Amelia’s laboring classes, and ate at Greenwich Village
restaurants, where they cultivated cigarette-smoking. They brought back
to the campus an assertiveness which came out in meetings agitating
greater undergraduate powers in college government. The bolder spirits
broke rules, and their followers in a body supported them and clashed
with faculty authority. Miss Steele refused to serve on a committee
appointed to investigate the cause of this new spirit. With her book in
full swing she had no time to waste on a committee searching for
something under their very noses.

Nora, who had given up her struggles in knitting after the completion of
a muffler rejected by the Red Cross, was also watching the rapid
appearance of a new order. During her second year of teaching she was
less driven by her subject. Her students ceased to be mere receivers of
painfully acquired lecture material. They had become individuals, caught
up in all the difficulties she had once suffered.

A freshman from the South confessed to Nora, as an excuse for inadequate
information about the causes of the French Revolution, that she had
stayed up all of the preceding night to argue with her room-mate from
Boston.

‘There’s two things we’ve got to get settled right away,’ she
explained—‘God and the Civil War.’

At the moment American soldiers were being killed in the front-line
trenches. Miss Ewing was expecting to hear every day that her brother
had landed in France.

‘The war doesn’t touch you,’ she reminded Nora. ‘You are very
fortunate.’

In Miss Ewing’s words Nora detected an unconscious superiority. Elevated
above the rest of Petersfield were those chosen ones who had relatives
on the way to the front. In the parlor of Faculty House women like Miss
Ewing of the inner circle of supreme devotion to their country drifted
together. Nora, overhearing their talk of commissions, ports in England
and France, the expectation of cablegrams, envied their intimate
participation. She had not written to Father Stokesbury, who sent
Cordelia letters full of humor from a French village not far from the
front. When her students consulted her as an historian about the war,
they were surprised to discover her bewilderment.

‘But I thought every one knew we were fighting to save Europe,’ one
young thing commented.

‘Save from what?’

But such a question was too much for a mere freshman.

The President in his message to Congress had presented his Fourteen
Points. They were read aloud, one by one, in the faculty parlor, during
the knitting, each decorated with comment and debate. Miss Ewing, a good
Southern Democrat, stood behind her President with a fervor which soon
made him in her eyes a saint, the savior of a war-torn world.

‘He’s the great man of our age,’ she affirmed to Nora, ‘the great man.’

But the New England Republicans did not agree with Miss Ewing. They were
violent in shouting a shrill warning to persons in danger of being duped
by sickly idealism.


 III

Ellen’s engagement to Michael Powys was precipitated into matrimony by
Amelia Donaldson’s importunate passion for Michael. From the day he had
rescued her from prison, Amelia had sent fervent epistles to her
deliverer, beginning on the high plane of sociology, but soon declaring
deathless love. Then she got ill. At last she threatened suicide. In
this crisis Nora was hastily summoned to New York to see Ellen and
Michael safely married.

When Nora returned to Petersfield, Cordelia’s letter was awaiting her.
Father Stokesbury was dead.

Never for a moment had she thought he could be killed—that death could
take any one—young, smiling, with a jest for everything. No, it couldn’t
be.

Father Stokesbury was the only splendid man she had ever known. She
could have loved him if——Why had she let those women keep her away? If
he and she——Racking sobs tore at her throat. He was a stranger. She had
never known him. No one in Maronac had pierced the enigmatic aloofness
underneath his sparkling exterior. Now it was too late.

If she could have seen him before he left Maronac——A memory of his face
in repose—the strained intensity—the troubled depths——What bitterness
had taught him that persistent raillery—that humor edged with sadness?
If she had talked to him—found out if he was sure he was embarking upon
a holy mission——Or was he like her—full of doubt? If a man fought to
save the world and his soul, the last moment would bring upliftment. But
if he had gone reluctantly—urged on by a duty which society forced upon
the young and the strong——There was the torment. She would never know.

The damning memory of the war books read in the faculty parlor—the
loathsome pictures——She mustn’t—one would go mad thinking of it. If he,
like some of them—slow death—not the swift proud end. No, she couldn’t
think of it.

Why should this man bring to her what her mother—her wasted mother, with
a sigh and a smile for her over the last struggling breath—had not
brought? The relief to her, that strong childish memory. And this
bitterness now.

At the end, if he had known some one loved him—some woman. If one is
greatly loved, death is not complete annihilation. The memory in the
other heart—a flitting, tenuous visitant in the midst of all the boring
duties in the world—even that——If she had loved him—even if he hadn’t
known—given him her best——But she had thrown away her chance. And for
what? Even Cordelia had something precious left her.

In the next days Nora tried to bury herself in her historical studies.
She found herself again and again staring at her book—not reading a
word. She detected Miss Abington and Miss Ewing examining her with
furtive, pitying glances. She knew what they were thinking. If it had
only been that——

On the library steps, she met Professor Steele with an armful of books.
‘Have you settled on any particular line of study?’ the older woman
inquired with her characteristic abruptness.

Nora informed her that she had almost decided to take eighteenth and
nineteenth century English history for her province.

‘Very good, very good,’ murmured Miss Steele, her eyes fixed upon the
library door. ‘Very inauspicious time. You’ll have to wait until after
the war to go to England.’

A few days later, Nora received a formal announcement from the committee
on scholarships that she had been awarded one for a year of graduate
study. She went at once to Miss Steele’s office.

‘I don’t see what I’ve done to deserve it,’ she exclaimed breathlessly.

Miss Steele looked up calmly from a mass of papers she had been
examining. ‘Good brains are scarce, Miss Deming. You really had very
little to compete against.’ Then, when she had completed a crisply
directed oration about Nora’s scholarly plans, she added: ‘Very bad time
to study. Our universities are demoralized. But I was afraid if you
stayed here much longer, you’d begin to think Petersfield was the world.
It isn’t. Last year your black despair gave me no concern. But this year
I’d begun to detect a tendency to be satisfied here—to settle down. A
grave symptom in the young.’

Nora saw the mocking smile lurking beneath Miss Steele’s solemnity, and
smiled too.

When she visited Knickerbocker University in New York to make plans for
her studies there in the fall, she had a moment of terror. Petersfield
had not prepared her for the crowded corridors and impersonal machinery
of a university. But it was this intellectual stronghold she must try to
conquer. She was like a nun, who, after running away from her cloister,
looks back with longing to the security there. And yet in the tumult of
the streets, with the confusion of traffic and the bright colors of the
foreign uniforms which now made New York like a European capital on
parade, she experienced what had never come to her in Petersfield.
Excitement, novelty, change. Her eyes were blinded with tears as she
made her uncertain way to the subway, nearly upsetting the box of a
flower-vendor selling clusters of pansies on the sidewalk.

‘Flowas—nice flowas,’ he invited customers in a soft foreign voice.

She selected a bunch and fastened them on her coat, like a banner to
give her courage as she made her first timid excursion out of that
sacred grove for maidenhood which Petersfield had been. In the
bewildering glare of sunshine, she blinked, hesitated an instant, and
then plunged forward with a firm step, her flowers nodding before her.

 PART III.
 Actæon


 CHAPTER I


 I

Nora, following the sidewalk which pierced Knickerbocker University,
passed between rows of buildings uniformly red brick, rising storey
above storey, all close duplicates, as if designed from the original
plan of an architect inspired to construct the largest number of
classrooms and the maximum of windows, sunshine, cross-ventilation, with
the smallest consumption of New York ground space. It came down to a
genius of economy in utilizing the very precious and rocky fragment of
Manhattan Island which the University had gobbled up just in time.
Accordingly, Knickerbocker University had anticipated the modern factory
in its construction, proving that culture does not always lag behind
industry.

But to one fresh from the groves of Petersfield, Knickerbocker was
dispiriting. Nora must wait to penetrate its inner life. Hidden springs
gushed, overflowed from a source having no connection with this barren
exterior. She must be patient.

In the September sunshine the people hurrying past her made her feel
cheerful, uplifted. It was they who mattered. All of them, one guessed
from their brisk, business-like air, had something important to do. They
were vital people whose existence mattered. The young men—were they
students—would they walk daily the same pavements, read in the
library—like her? Only the youngest ones. This was war-time: two
officers walking together, with the nervous, alert expression she was
sure they had cultivated, reminded her. And the last building before she
crossed the street, the windows open, boys in olive-drab uniforms sat
looking out. The S.A.T.C.; she had forgotten. If the war lasted long
enough, they would be soldiers. Without them the University would be
deserted—only professors and a few men physically unfit. These
youngsters—doing something to them—hasty plans—but universities must be
used some way. A crowd of boys in a window high above the street were
throwing down wads of paper to make the girls hurrying for the subway
look up at them.

Nora crossed the street. A broad business thoroughfare and a tangle of
trolley lines separated the dormitory for graduate women and those in
the professional schools from the rest of the University. The same old
sex division. Men’s dormitories on one side, a long row of them, the one
for women on the other, obeying the scriptural order, ‘male and female
created he them.’ The women’s hall, being an architect’s latest dream,
was even more gaunt and towering than its prototypes. Its windows in
unvarying rows going up and up—one of them hers—all representing some
woman huddled behind each glass pane——No, she wouldn’t live there. One
decision, at least, was easily made.

In her bag was a list of neighborhood apartments with rooms rented to
students. She would try these. She had a long block of the busy street
before she made the initial plunge of the first address. A shoe-shining
place with rows of hats in the window, its open door, men coming in and
out. All New York men looked smart and well-groomed. Nowhere else was
there the same correctness of detail. Here men made an art of middle
age, the rough tweeds suggesting the brown of early fall, with the
sunshine bringing out bright threads interwoven like autumn leaves, the
glossy hats in soft tones, the gleam of freshly polished shoes, the
socks in a harmony of dull tones. How stupid the clothes of men were
elsewhere! How alive here!

The women passed her unobserved. It was the men she saw. Women—she had
watched too many women. She was relieved that she had decided not to
live in that stuffy women’s hall. Of course it would be stuffy. Places
filled with women—only women—always were. All the vitality squeezed out.
Respectability and lack of adventure did it.

With her trunk and bags still at the station, Nora had a whole day to
look around. No haste—the luxury of it.

In the first apartment house an ebony elevator man, in a new uniform,
the collar very stiff and shiny with braid, immediately guessed her
errand.

‘Room, Miss? Miss Johnson—I guess she’s filled up. But Miss
Walters—eighth floor—she’s got a nice room. Pleasant lady. Take ye up,
Miss?’

Miss Walters, dressed in a street costume, opened the door. ‘A room. Oh,
yes. Just for yourself?’

She swung back a near-by door, presenting a view of a very small dark
room, opening upon a narrow court. She went ahead to turn on a
rose-shaded lamp. The lamp, with the yellow walls and white wicker
furniture, helped to give an impression of light, but at ten o’clock in
the morning the room was naturally full of gloom.

‘The last roomer stayed four years,’ Miss Walters began. ‘She loved this
room. So girlish, she said. The light furniture and the cretonne——’

‘But I couldn’t study in such a room.’ Miss Walters was politely
non-committal. ‘I thought I’d like a place with morning sun.’

‘Sun is very hard to get in New York and very expensive.’

Sunshine and fresh air a luxury. A new idea. Then she remembered the
settlement neighborhood.

In the next apartment the ominous smell of some disinfectant assailed
her. Across the court a woman was drying her hair with a fan. It seemed
indecent to live so close to people.

The trained nurse, generally away all day, with a room to rent,
conducted Nora into the presence of a young man without a collar, who
was pounding a typewriter. ‘Don’t mind him,’ Miss Jones said, observing
Nora’s hesitation. ‘He’s leaving to-day.’ The floor was strewn with
paper, sheets of it thrown loosely everywhere.

‘No. I don’t mind being exhibited,’ he said, pausing. ‘I don’t pay my
rent promptly, so I have to leave.’ He gave Miss Jones a meaning glance.

‘He’s an author,’ Miss Jones murmured confidentially as Nora backed out
of the room. ‘You didn’t let me show you the closets—two of them.’

The rent was ten dollars a week. Of course it was impossible. If the
author had been staying and there had been some kind of hole for her to
creep into—living in the same apartment with a man who wrote might give
her something.

All the decent rooms she saw were beyond what she could pay. ‘Haven’t
you a girl friend who would share the room with you?’ chirped
prospective landladies. Twilight was coming on, and she had to find a
place for the night. There was nothing but the women’s dormitory. Nora
tottered back on aching feet.

The matron assigned her a room whose unrelieved walls, wash-basin, and
concrete floor brought weary tears. Once more she was baffled. Herded
with hundreds of women. The same old thing all over again. Would there
ever be escape from such a life?

In the cafeteria downstairs the clatter of dishes and the voices
spinning in circles and dizzy waves around her choked her as she tried
to eat some corned-beef hash and tough buttered beets. Women, tired like
her, sat at bare tables, their hair falling in limp wisps over their
faces. Most of them were not young. The sagged resignation in the sallow
faces belonged with airless schoolrooms, high-pitched, nervous voices,
blackboards, clouds of chalk dust. Why had they come here?

The trolleys, the trucks, the fire-engines clanging up and down as if
bent on mad exercise, the milk carts, kept Nora awake most of the night.
Toward dawn she slept. When she opened her eyes, the breeze was blowing
out the sash curtain, and from her couch she could look down upon miles
and miles of streets, full of golden light, all astir with the activity
of a new morning.

Nora learned that the professor Miss Steele had advised her to study
with was on some war committee in Washington. Two of the others were not
to be found in their offices the first time she called. One very bored
young man talked to three or four women waiting in the hall, disposing
of them efficiently together to save valuable time.

‘It’s just our money they want,’ said one. ‘I wonder why I came.’

The professors Nora saw were very polite in a formal, almost scared way,
but they did not urge her to take their courses or advise her about the
offering of their colleagues. Evidently a graduate school was like the
cafeteria. The food was spread out, and you could take what looked good
to you. But a cafeteria meal was too often an unbalanced array of dishes
snatched at in a crowded line. No proper proportion of protein,
carbohydrates, and starches one heard about. Not at all what one wanted.
The history courses she registered for were probably not much better.

The lectures the first days were discouraging—all facts. Some were a
repetition of Petersfield. No new point of view. Nora took down notes in
a mad race with the lecturer; then her pen lagged, and she stared around
the room. Most of the students were women. The men were the discards of
the draft board, or wore wedding rings, sometimes ostentatiously. As
Miss Steele had said, this was an inauspicious time to begin graduate
work. The University was apologetic, uneasy. The war might last another
year. Professors were dispirited. Those who had tried to get over to
France champed fretfully over the drudgery of going on with the old
sing-song lectures. Those who felt civilization must be kept going raged
in secret over the rows of meek female faces before them.

From many of the classroom windows Nora could watch men drilling on the
athletic grounds. The papers were full of alarming news about an
epidemic of influenza brought from Europe which was spreading in the
training camps. In a few days men from the dormitory across the street
were being carried out by attendants with handkerchiefs over nose and
mouth. The epidemic had come. Women in Nora’s hall were ill. Many were
frightened. They were advised to keep outdoors and away from the subways
and theatres.

Nora in the airless library began thinking about germs. The library
encouraged low prospects. It was huge, unmanageable, slow. One waited
hours for books which, at last, were announced as not in.

At last she found a quiet reading-room; and by degrees she adjusted
herself to the University. In the basement of the library was a place
where tea was served every afternoon. Women, snatching a little
refreshment before they returned to their books, talked to Nora. At a
corner table every day sat two young men with the curse of feeble health
upon them. At least a kind of transparent unsubstantiality characterized
their tense frames. They appeared to be engaged in an argument prolonged
from day to day. Nora drank two cups of tea in lingering sips and bought
a second piece of currant cake. Tea on a dark, rainy day, the moist,
comforting warmth of it going down a throat parched by the dust of the
library books. The throat muscles relax. The soreness is gone. The pain
behind burning eyeballs pounds less, disappears. One sits in a cosy glow
enjoying the third cup.

The cell of a room in the dormitory was slowly taking on familiarity.
She liked to wake up in the morning with the sunshine on her face. It
was a peaceful place to study. Over the tall buildings rosy clouds came
at sunset. She could see Jupiter in the twilight from her window.
Jupiter—his ‘mild and magnificent eye’—where had she read it? Some poet.
Browning. Mars had risen by the time she came up after dinner. By
leaning out, she could just see its candle-flame gleam in the sky. Miss
Ewing had taught her the stars and the planets. What splendid women
there were at Petersfield!

Her neighbors dropped into her room to talk after dinner, determined to
keep the conversation away from influenza. They were less cheerless upon
acquaintance, often with lovely smiles and unexpected humor. There was
beauty in a few faces, so delicate that it was easily overlooked at
first. Mental restlessness was commoner than intelligence, but one found
both. They had such amusing reasons for coming to the University. Miss
Andrews, from Trenton, New Jersey, was threatened with a nervous
breakdown and had to give up teaching for a year. She drank hot milk
before going to bed at eight o’clock every night. Most of the others
wanted a change. Year after year in the same place. In New York
everything happened. They had heard of a graduate student who married
one of the professors; in anthropology, they thought. The theatre, the
opera, the orchestras—everything in New York. Then one had professional
ambitions—a better job. Nora was considered a remarkable person to have
taught in a college so young. How was it done? And Petersfield. A
college job was more than they could expect. Learning for itself—the
pleasure of grappling with tough intellectual problems—they did not talk
about that. Wasn’t Professor White hard to follow? He was scholarly,
but——


 II

Armistice Night. Nora was standing near Times Square in a mob of
revellers. Horns tooted. Confetti fell everywhere. It was a wild time.
Men and women joined hands and danced in the street. Noisy boys,
spreading out in a long line, swept little groups of bystanders into a
circle with deafening shouts when their prisoners tried to get away. The
police on proud-stepping horses curvetted in and out of the crowd. The
war was over. More noise. Clacking toys, whistles. Children got lost and
howled. Women fainted in the street. Hawkers sold pancakes, coffee, hot
dogs. Peace had come.

‘Those with dead—are not here,’ Nora’s companion said.

In the tumult Nora was thrown against her old Petersfield friend Helen
Spring.

‘Well, it’s over at last,’ Helen began. ‘Isn’t this wonderful? I heard
you were in New York. So you’re living near the University. You don’t
like the dormitory? If I’d got hold of you, I’d have begged you to take
half of my apartment in the Village. Amelia Donaldson just rented it.
Yes, she’s still in the Labor College, and awfully excited about it.’

Helen herself was taking up psycho-analysis with a remarkable man. She
intended to do startling things to people, mental cases, when she got
going. Helen, plump and easy-going, was not the popular conception of a
worker in abnormal psychology. She had been one of the milder members of
the Socialist League.

‘Amelia’s as crazy as ever,’ Helen chattered on. ‘She’s a case to begin
on. You must come down soon. We’ll both want to see you.’

Upon Nora’s visit she found Helen’s rooms looking out upon a little
garden, very charming with their long, low proportions and a
wood-burning fireplace. An old-fashioned four-storey house had been
converted into a coöperative apartment by some enterprising women who
sold each floor to members of their sex engaged in some unusual but
approved employment.

‘It’s a fascinating place to be,’ Helen said. ‘The most interesting
people.’

‘Maybe Amelia won’t care to see me,’ Nora ventured when Helen suggested
rapping on the door connecting with Amelia’s rooms in front.

‘She wants to see you. She said to be sure and call her.’ A man’s voice
could be heard. ‘It doesn’t matter,’ Helen explained. ‘He’s always
there.’

Amelia put on a bubbling show of delight at seeing an old classmate. She
was more animated and full of talk than in her college days. Behind her
stood a tall, ungainly young man, rather handsome in a swarthy way.

‘Only Tony,’ Amelia laughed by way of introduction.

Tony took a chair in a remote corner of Amelia’s apartment. Its black
furniture and deep blue hangings had an intense look like their owner.
Nora asked Amelia about the Labor College.

‘And you’re still studying, Helen tells me,’ Amelia remarked next. ‘I’ve
given all that up. There isn’t time in my life—at least not now.’

The young man kept his eyes fixed upon Amelia. They had the expression
of a faithful dog not always well used.

‘Who is Tony?’ Nora asked when they were back in Helen’s room, seated
before a blazing wood fire.

‘Amelia will tell you the next time she sees you. She’s reclaiming him.
He’s just out of some school of correction. His gang—they’re all in
gangs—those Italian boys south of the Square.’

‘He doesn’t look over eighteen. Is he in love with Amelia?’

‘She lets him hang around. He’s here by eight o’clock some mornings. He
cooks her meals—runs errands—polishes her shoes——’

‘So that’s the way she reclaims him?’

‘Well, he was stealing automobiles before. At least his friends were.
Poor Tony seems too stupid to get away with much himself. Perhaps having
a mistress——’

‘You don’t mean——’

‘My dear, psycho-analysis makes one suspect the purity of human
emotions. I trust no one and nothing. And with an over-sexed woman like
Amelia, I’d believe anything.’

‘Some one ought to warn him.’

Helen laughed. ‘Amelia’s up to something. She’s no fool, you know. And
the poor boy is flattered that an American above him socially——’

The next time Nora visited Helen there was the man’s voice on the other
side of the door. Then a terrible sound, like the howling of a dog. A
rap on the door, and Amelia asking them to go to a strike meeting with
her. It was near by. One of the men from the Labor College was the
speaker. Tony did not appear, and the three women walked away together.

The dingy hall rocked with the noise of the crowd. Children ran all
over. Babies crawled over the filthy floor or ate sticky messes which
got smeared over their faces, even in their hair. The smells. No
bathrooms. Nora knew that from the settlement. And the toilets in the
dark halls. The oratorical man who took charge of the meeting reminded
Nora of the boys who debated in the night-school classes at the
settlement. The same glib tongue shooting off eloquence without logic or
common sense. The crowd were a flock of sheep taking in everything he
said. When he got through, the gabbling tongues threatened a riot. Nora
was afraid.

‘Is the speaker a garment-worker?’ Nora asked Amelia. The strike seemed
to be involving garment workers.

‘No. He was trained at our school to be a labor-union leader. We need
young men who can talk well.’

‘I should think they’d prefer one of their own workers to direct them.
If I were an electrician or a plumber——’

‘A man from the ranks has no social sense. He doesn’t understand
industrial organization or capitalistic exploitation. He’s too close to
the economic struggle. He can’t forget the week’s pay check. We must get
Vicky Hamilton, organizer of the lady needleworkers, to explain things
to you. She belongs to the old Philadelphia Hamilton family. It’s very
useful to have such a name to use in labor ranks.’

When Amelia went back to her quarters, Nora and Helen heard Tony’s voice
again.

‘You see,’ laughed Helen.

‘Can’t you do something?’ Nora’s voice was desperate.

‘What can I do? It’s not my affair. I blame Petersfield for people like
Amelia. If she hadn’t been bottled up there—brooding for four
years—overstimulated mentally—all that feminine atmosphere. I don’t
believe in college communities. It’s the soil where Sapphism
flourishes.’

‘There wasn’t any at Petersfield. I never saw it.’ She hesitated. The
satyr laughter of the gardener’s assistant—a cat twisting its back and
purring—‘Well—maybe two cases,’ she admitted.

‘You were too innocent to suspect.’

‘So you question all friendships between women. It’s a dangerous point
of view.’

‘I didn’t say all. But too often.’

Helen was looking for mental sickness everywhere. It was the way of
psycho-analysts. Freud—exaggerating one theory. Nora had read enough of
him to know.

Greenwich Villagers went to meetings, lectures. Their thinking they
apparently left to others. Nora was carried off to listen to some new
kind of theory nearly every time she came down to sit in front of
Helen’s fire. Opportunities in New York—they mustn’t be neglected.
Sticking off at a university with the dead ideas in books—Nora must be
rescued from all that.

An Irish poet was addressing the Twentieth Century Club to which Helen
belonged. This was better than the thought-control crank last week.

Zoe Ryder was going with them. She was a gentle little woman brought up
on a large plantation in Virginia. Her childhood had been very lonely.
Her people were land-poor. All her education had come from an ignorant
governess and an old-fashioned library. She got up North and took
nurse’s training to make money in the quickest way. She had great
success with mental cases. Helen had met her at one of the clinics.

Zoe had just come off a very bad case. They were trying to cure a young
man—it was drink—his mind gone—all kinds of money. She had been shut up
alone with him on a country estate with only the servants to call upon.
The night nurses had set him off raving crazy, and they had to leave.
Only Zoe could manage him. She had a kind of hypnotic influence. But she
was so tired. One night he crept out of bed and got away. She followed
him in the darkness with a flashlight. She didn’t dare ask for help. It
was a hilly country, with marshes not far away. She wasn’t afraid of him
or the black night—but if he got into the marshes. That was the terror
for her. She got him back. He was too weak now to walk far. But the next
morning she saw that she must have a rest. Her patient was turned over
to a strong man and put into a strait-jacket.

The Irish speaker had beautiful, beseeching brown eyes. He wanted his
countrymen to be reasonable. He reviewed Irish history. There had been
mistakes on both sides. He wanted to go back to dear beautiful Ireland
and find peace. Then the fairies would return, and he could write his
verse.

Zoe sat next to Nora. During the talk Nora had felt a hand pulling at
her arm, but she wanted to hear what the poet was saying. She was
charmed by his mixture of fancy and shrewd common sense.

When he was done, Zoe whispered to her, ‘You see—that man over there—the
tall dark one.’ Nora examined a dignified man filling the opposite
corner. ‘He’s been staring at me the whole evening. I’ve been so
uncomfortable.’

‘I didn’t notice. He’s been looking at the speaker.’

‘No, no,’ Zoe insisted. ‘Let’s hurry away. He’ll try to speak to me
afterward.’

‘I suppose Zoe told you about the man staring at her,’ Helen remarked
when the two were alone. ‘She’s got persecution mania. Try to start her
on something else. Men are always following her on the street. Of course
she wants them to. She’s such a quiet little mouse I’m sure no one
really notices her. She’s tired out.’

‘Nursing’s too hard for her.’

‘And she won’t take anything but mental cases. It’s the excitement, I
guess. She writes charming verse for children. I want you to see it.
Full of humor—a kind of twinkle—and the sadness so close to the surface
of a child’s life. But this other thing—it gets stronger all the time.
Maybe if she had a little money and time to write—she might get over
it.’

Zoe appeared like a little brown bird chirping on a sunny winter
morning. This hallucination was terrible. If Helen with her new ideas
could only do something. But Helen insisted that not every one could be
saved by psycho-analysis. She was progressing far enough to see that.

Amelia introduced Nora to Vicky Hamilton, organizer of the lady
needleworkers, at the Dragon, with its memories of Mary Howe, now in
France dancing at the Red Cross parties with young officers. Miss
Hamilton had her hair cut short like a boy’s, and her handshake was
painfully gripping. They continued their discussion of industrial
problems in Amelia’s room. Tony for once wasn’t there.

Vicky crossed one leg over the other, lighted a cigarette, which
curvetted around one corner of her mouth as she talked. ‘You people up
at the University in the dissertation belt,’ she began anew, ‘you’re all
theory. There’s theoretical sympathy—emotional sometimes. But that’s not
enough. You’ve got to get down here with the crowd and start the
machinery going.’

‘That’s what I’ve told Nora,’ Amelia said.

Vicky, it developed, had domestic problems dragging at her industrial
efficiency. ‘Mother and brother have come up to New York for the winter.
I won’t live with them. I told them so. I refuse to have my comings and
goings questioned. I must be free—live my own life.’

‘Of course,’ agreed Amelia.

‘You’ve heard about Bess Leland’s baby?’ Vicky inquired of Amelia.

‘Yes, it’s come. A boy. Bess stayed at the office until six. And the
baby was born before nine. Easy—no pain. What motherhood should be.’

Bess was secretary for the Birth Control Association. It was wonderful
having a courageous woman like Bess down there—helping the cause by
showing that women, even with all the knowledge for preventing
conception, had babies. But only when they wanted them. Bess was
maternal. She needed children. This was her second child. His father was
a free-lance writer for a radical weekly. The father of three-year-old
Gloria was an actor, once considered by Bess the great luminary of the
theatre. Drink had dimmed his light, and he was now in the movies,
supported between jobs by his fifth wife.

Bess, with a photograph of Mary Wollstonecraft on her desk, had long
been a foe to marriage. She loved freely, without the constraint of
municipal bureau registrations or the fettering mediæval sanctities of
the Church. The degeneration of one glorious mate did not vanquish her.
For there could always be another. One was never disappointed exactly in
the same way a second time.

Both Vicky and Amelia agreed that men were satisfactory only as lovers.
As husbands and fathers they sneaked away from what society considered
their duty. Only women were parents. They clung to their young, needed
them.

This was the Socialist League of Petersfield in its early stages. But
these people acted, whereas Petersfield only talked.

Vicky in her unrestrained voice was deep in some pathological aspect of
pregnancy when Tony entered the room. He scarcely knocked. Nora hurried
away.


 III

The women at the dormitory reminded Nora of Cordelia and the Plant
girls. The ignorance—especially of the older ones—was almost
unbelievable. Petersfield, shut away as it was, had at least heard
echoes from a cosmopolitan centre. They trooped in timid little parties
of three or four down to Greenwich Village. Every one at home would ask
them about it. They tried the Barnyard, the Dragon, and all their
facsimiles with an endless variety of names, hoping each time that
something would happen. The wild air of Bohemia might at least blow over
them. But always the same disappointment. Bad air, dirt, men who looked
like automobile salesmen and flirted in a loud, clumsy way with the
waitresses, crowded dance floors, with never a chance to dance. One sat
almost smothered against the wall, waiting and waiting, and then at last
went home. Cheated—cheated.

A crowd from the dormitory went to the Night Court, another New York
novelty. Nora went with them. At midnight they sat in a stuffy, dimly
lighted room, sickly expectant. Why had they wanted to come? An old
woman stood facing the judge. She was a whimpering creature in a rusty
black bonnet and cape, a hard-working widow who took lodgers. This girl
couldn’t pay her rent. Out of a job and every week the same tale about
waiting for the money her husband was going to send her. Yes, she
admitted she had threatened to turn the girl out. She had her living to
make. And if the young woman started behaving bad with one of the
lodgers—this young man who turned out to be a detective and got her into
all this trouble—how could she help it?

The detective took the stand. He’d been sent to the house. The old
lady’d been carrying on suspicious. He got the goods on her. The young
woman was handed over to him by this old customer, who took charge of
the whole affair. His language had none of the polysyllabic evasiveness
still used by even such modernists as Helen and Amelia. For the
primitive relations of human beings he used plain language. ‘I told her
to take off her clothes,’ the hard voice said. ‘She had on only——’ The
words cut Nora like a knife.

The young woman told her story. It was tragic enough. There was a
lingering prettiness in her face. She spoke in a whisper. An abandoned
wife, her last cent gone, making a choice between the street on a
January night or the bold hints of her landlady. The old woman made the
bargain. She was in the clutches of a crone who knew how to work her
trade.

The judge made a caustic speech. The accused, who employed the
sentimentality of widowhood, children who would do nothing for their
poor mother, her own white hair, to plead for her, deserved no pity. A
helpless young woman, in her ignorance coming to a cheap lodging-house
she thought was respectable, trusting some one who pretended to be her
friend, had been used to carry on a profitable business. The accused was
sentenced to hard labor in the work-house.

Nora wanted to go. She couldn’t stand any more. But the others shook
their heads, and leaned forward in their seats. Two women dressed in fur
coats and simple black hats, an air of restrained elegance about them,
spoke in quiet tones. But they, too, were guilty of running disorderly
houses.

‘They weren’t even painted,’ one of the group commented as they stood
huddled together on the snowy street.

Past them reeled a woman very drunk shouting lines from ‘Paradise Lost.’

Returning from the opera a few nights ago several of them had seen with
their own eyes women stopping men on the street, choosing dark places
away from policemen. Women in flashy clothes with flowers in their hats.
That kind always seemed to wear flowers.

On the subway to-night after two o’clock a woman opposite Nora and Mary
Whitson attracted their attention. Her hair was too yellow, her brows
heavily pencilled, her cheeks and chin flabby and old in spite of the
paint.

‘She’s out of luck,’ Mary confided. ‘Getting old. It’s trade on the
streets for her now. The others—“kept” women—I meet them in the park.
Fattening like geese in a pen. They’re always alone dragging a little
white dog behind them.’

‘How do you know?’ Nora insisted.

‘I can tell. It’s in their eyes.’

The party collected in Nora’s room afterward. There was so much they
must talk over. Women talking together late at night, sitting about in
faded old dressing-gowns, brushing their hair, suddenly become very
frank. In the morning they remember that nice women never think about
sex. It wasn’t that they wanted to put on any superior airs. A girl who
was good because she’d never had a chance to be anything else—what
virtue that way? But had the old standards disappeared? Were women like
men taking experience, good and bad, as it came?

The plays they went to turned all their ideas upside down. They were
preoccupied with sex and the new attitude. The critics, commenting on
the downfall of the Puritanic censorship of the stage, prophesied that
at last something would happen to the American drama. But the graduate
women, so innocent and inexperienced, were bewildered. They went over
the whole question once more. All this sentimentality about harlots and
the daring irregularity of the new woman—to what was it leading?

‘It’s out of date, then—the old-fashioned virgin purity business?’
inquired an uncompromising little creature from Vermont. ‘What they’ve
held over us all these years—it’s a fraud, then?’

They agreed that their lack of experience had not been exciting. But
what else was there?

Mary Whitson lingered in Nora’s room after the rest had gone. ‘I’ve
never even been kissed,’ she stated boldly. ‘And I’m twenty-nine.
Sometimes I think—just to know what it’s like—even if I didn’t care for
the man.’

At the beginning of the second term new men suddenly appeared in the
history classes, dismissed from the training camps in this country. They
wore the desperate earnestness of those who must make up for lost time.
Nora wondered what they thought of the droning professors with their
lecture notes, yellow sometimes with age. She speculated often about the
date of the last new thought. Miss Steele had warned her of the drudgery
of graduate study until she began to steer her own boat and commanded
the attention of the big men. This must be what she was to expect. But
how dead, how dead it was! She remembered the words of Ezekiel which
Newman had written on the view of Oxford hung in the Oratory at
Birmingham: ‘Son of Man, can these dry bones live?’

For while Nora’s professors were fluttering old lectures, their country
was churning with party hatreds, and the American people were running
this way and that like a panic-stricken crowd caught in fire. The
President had sailed for Europe, and the Peace Conference was in full
swing. Nora found that she had to give up reading the morning papers.
They upset all her work for the day. Instead she gathered several of the
better weeklies representing a divergence of opinion and pondered over
them Sunday mornings in her room. The discussions she had started with
the women in the dormitory she had been wise enough to discontinue.
Those from the South had Miss Ewing’s blind devotion to the Democratic
Party which closed their eyes to all but one point of view. New England
Republicanism was becoming more and more bitter. And Nora had no
illusions about the idealism of her own section of the country, sitting
snug in the fertility of its cornfields, wheat lands, and green
pastures. All it wanted was to be let alone and continue prosperous. She
made secret plans to talk to the men seated near her in the history
lectures. But they were too aloof and shy. The professors, on their
elevated platforms, were even more remote.

Nothing but the contradictions of the weeklies. The President
bewildered, baffled. One couldn’t tell. A chance word caught on a street
corner, on subway platforms, the top of a bus. But how futile are the
words of the man in the street those know best who dream of real
democracy. At last a kind of makeshift Covenant, the League of Nations,
which radicals like Amelia and Vicky said was better than nothing. The
left wing of the weeklies made no such concessions. The President
returned, rode through the streets. It wasn’t a triumphal procession.
Nora stood in a vast throng and cheered. One hoped that there would be
no more war. Hope was something.


 IV

Amelia was beginning to neglect Tony. He wept like a whipped little boy,
throwing himself on the floor in Helen’s room, even when Nora was there,
and cursing Amelia between his sobs. One day he took a stiletto out of
his pocket and threatened to kill himself.

‘Tony, you silly boy,’ Helen said.

Amelia had no time for Tony now. She sent him away—told him not to
bother her. Garrison Smith, a lecturer at the Labor College, spent every
evening in Amelia’s room talking very late. Now that the war was over,
he was free to agitate a scheme for uniting the union-labor forces of
England and the United States. He was sailing for Europe soon. He also
planned to study schools for working-men abroad.

‘Garrison certainly works his personality overtime,’ Helen said of him.
‘But I admire the way he manages everything. During the war he made
mince-meat out of the Draft Board. Amelia and I almost die over his
stories of what he told them. But I wonder if she’s a match for him.’

Amelia, however, seemed very happy. She was the kind, Helen repeated to
Nora, who needed a man to keep her going. With women she was pale,
moaning over her aching back, hysterical in argument. But men brought
out a playfulness in her, a lightness close to humor; her cheeks
flushed, her eyes sparkled.

Garrison Smith was frequently summoned to different cities to confer
with those concerned in his British schemes. He wrote lengthy letters to
Amelia asking her advice about knotty questions, telling her again and
again how much he needed her, how he had grown to depend upon her. These
letters she read to Helen, to Nora, even to Tony at the times he slunk
back to her for comfort.

One Saturday afternoon Helen, Nora, and Amelia were picking violets on
the aqueduct. They sprang out of the green grass in great luxurious
patches. The flower-picking was Helen’s idea. Every spring she made a
pilgrimage to this violet haunt, and picked and picked. Only darkness
put an end to her greedy desire to have every blossom she saw. Amelia
had scruples about touching wild flowers. To her it was a kind of
murder. Then, too, her back wasn’t equal to the strain of stooping.
Nora, after gathering several modest clusters, joined Amelia stretched
out full length on a sunny hillside, close to a bank of violets.
Amelia’s eyes were half shut. A smile flitted over her face. Then she
sighed, a lazy, contented sigh. Nora wondered what made Amelia so happy.

Amelia stirred, but the eyes which looked up at Nora sitting near her
had the emptiness of a sleep-walker’s gaze.

‘It was so—so wonderful—our love——’ breathed Amelia, still smiling her
lazy, happy smile.

A queer, throaty sound escaped Nora’s lips. Amelia looked around, like a
child roused suddenly from peaceful dreams.

‘Well.’ Amelia’s voice lost its sleepy softness. ‘I mean it. You—you
don’t know—you don’t know anything about it.’ Again the happy look.
‘Perhaps I shouldn’t have shocked you. But I’m so—so full of it. He went
away early this morning. I may never see him again. That doesn’t trouble
me. We had last night together.’

‘And you can tell me this?’

Amelia’s lips curled into something close to a smile. ‘It may do you
good.’ She met Nora’s direct, scornful look without lowering her eyes.
‘And those like you. Your stiff little world. I was there once.
Tight—tight like a drum—and hard. I wouldn’t go back to it——’

‘And what changed you—got you out of my stiff little world?’

‘Michael Powys. He was the love of my life.’ Amelia’s voice softened.
‘At least that first quickening of love in a girl——But how he made me
suffer!’

‘The way you make Tony.’

‘Tony—that baby. He doesn’t suffer. All show. But if he did—is it only
women who should suffer?’

‘You’re using Tony for revenge then—revenge for what you——’

‘Perhaps. Poor Tony.’

‘I can’t believe you.’

‘Well—a woman finds it convenient to have some kind of a sighing lover
trailing after her.’ This was the old, bitter Amelia. ‘Men are that way.
The sight of another man pursuing the woman they’re after—it adds zest
to the chase. Garrison saw—and other men will—that I——I’m disgusting
you, Nora. But I don’t care.’ Amelia sat upright and fumbled with a knot
of hair falling over her neck. ‘If I had your charm, Nora Deming, and
your beauty—there is beauty hidden away in you somewhere. You’re a fool
wasting it all the way you do. Going on and on—using your brains. As if
they mattered in a woman. You’ll put on spectacles in a few more
years—that red hair all on fire here in the sun—it’ll be dull—grey. I
can see you that way. And you—like ice. You don’t understand. You turn
up your nose because I—I with no beauty—no charm—a disgusting weak
body—snatch what I can. I have. I will again. No one can make me believe
I do wrong. Right and wrong—they don’t exist for me.’

Helen was approaching them. ‘Such richness!’ she shouted. ‘I’ve got
flowers to take back to everybody.’

Nora got Helen between her and Amelia on the walk to the subway. She was
sickened by the torrent of words Amelia had poured over her. Something
burned in her, like a hot coal placed on raw flesh. So this was the
defiant new woman insisting upon her place in the world.

Tony was waiting in Amelia’s room. He bounded out of the door when he
heard their steps on the stairs, like a lonely child who had waited long
for its mother.

Helen in her sitting-room bent over her flowers. She spread out in a row
on her couch the little nosegays she had fastened together with tough
grass, and got down on her knees before them.

‘The darlings—the darlings. How cool and sweet. Violet time in spring—I
live for it, Nora. It’s my Easter every year. We women without
religion—we must have something. Woodsy—woodsy sweetlings,’ she crooned
to herself.

Nora, stretched out in Helen’s big chair, watched Helen getting bowls
for her treasures. At last they were taken care of for the night. In the
morning she would distribute her flowers among the friends looking out
on bare city streets. She began to murmur the chosen names.

Nora interrupted her in the midst of them. ‘Helen—tell me. Do you think
Amelia is—just right?’

‘She told you about her farewells with Garrison, then?’ Helen laughed.
‘I thought maybe she wouldn’t. She thinks you’re so easily shocked,
Nora.’

‘Yes, she told me.’

‘Poor girl. She saw him off this morning. That’s why I asked her to go
violet-picking with us.’

‘She came right out with it.’

‘Yes, Amelia is very frank. And honest, too.’

‘I don’t see how you can stand having her here—close to you this way.’

‘She doesn’t bother me. And just because I happen to be a born old
maid—maybe I’m fortunate. I’m not sure, though. But how can I judge what
works best for a girl like Amelia?’

‘I don’t believe she even loves that man.’

‘Garrison? Well, maybe it’s better if she doesn’t. You know what I think
about him. He’ll love many times. A generous lover.’

‘How could he? I feel sick all over.’

‘Yes, you would. I’m sorry, Nora. Here, take some of my violets back
with you. For those graduate women of yours. They need flowers more than
Amelia.’ Helen’s queer little chuckle was over some joke she kept to
herself.

In the adjoining room Tony was laughing, a boyish laugh full of joy.
Nora went back to the dormitory with a generous portion of Helen’s
violets. Helen made her take them, gave her a cool little peck on the
cheek at parting, and laughed once more.


 CHAPTER II


 I

By September the United States had welcomed most of its heroes home.
Nora, sitting in her history lectures, observed a new group of men,
shabby some of them, with army shoes and often the army flannel shirt
completing a civilian costume of a shiny coat strained at the shoulders
and trousers threatening collapse in the seat. There was sturdiness and
hardness about most of these men, as if, having conquered fear, hunger,
disease, and vice, they asserted an animal vigor which set them apart
from other men. But there were cripples too; legs and arms missing.
Crutches and canes tapped in the hallways and lecture rooms. In the
faces of some there was strain, suffering, a transparent pallor. They
collected in little groups in the rear of Nora’s history classes,
talking together in low tones, deliberately closing the exchange of
their experiences not only to the women, but equally to the married men
and the fledglings fresh from college.

But from the first day, Nora detected a new spirit in the University.
Even the professors were changed. The old droning tones had disappeared.
They may have been frightened last year or only disheartened. Or were
they, like actors roused to play their best before a good audience,
conscious of the men sitting in the back rows?

Never before had places of learning been crowded with so serious a
student body as in the fall of 1919. Perhaps they never will be again.
At last there seemed to be earnest concentration upon education, a
hunger for new ideas and enlightenment. Every man returning from France
seemed sure of one thing—that for the complications of life, stripped
now of its former vapid American idealism, he must know more. In the
tensity of the classroom Nora built up these speculations.

Among the history lecturers was Professor Nicholson, with grey hair
straggling over a face that had a curious, unconquerable youth. In what
the other professors said one found the same new note of radicalism
creeping in, subtle generally and evasive, but there for the discerning.
They, too, made frequent comparisons with modern events, clarifying and
interpreting both old and new. But, in spite of these resemblances, he
stood out above them. The first day she could not quite catch the
difference. Was it a closer reality he reached, a more intense
experience?

The women around Nora whispered that Professor Nicholson had been
studying in England when war was declared. At once he offered himself as
an ambulance driver. Later he enlisted as a private. He had seen hard
fighting in the Argonne drive, been wounded. Nora wondered how much
these women knew. He was a small man, slight, almost delicate, and the
eyes and sensitive mouth did not suggest ambulances or shell fire. But
the nose, large, aggressive, riding over the fine lines of the rest of
the face, was not that of a dreamer. One of Nora’s neighbors repeated
what a man who had studied under him before the war had told her. He was
just the usual scholar then, a good lecturer, but all brilliant theory
like the others. The war had changed him.

The opening lectures in a course dealing with nineteenth-century England
pictured manufacturing and agricultural England before the Industrial
Revolution. The vividness of phrase, the pictorial and dramatic effect,
were unforgettable. Miss Steele’s conception of the historian, a kind of
recording angel, looking back, seeing in the present both the past
and the future, but not a cruel recording angel—pitying,
understanding—Professor Nicholson seemed like that.

Behind Nora sat a unique specimen of graduate student, handsome,
fashionably dressed, his well-set-up figure and lean muscular frame
suggesting a steady programme of games and exercise rather than hard
work. From his conversations with his companion Nora pieced together
those first days a fairly complete biography. He had been studying at
Oxford when the war broke out, offered himself for British service at
once, and was later transferred to an American regiment. But he
described a machine-gun attack in the style of a tennis match, a polo
game, or a drag-hunt. Fighting was the duty of a gentleman, disagreeable
sometimes, but a game, an adventure.

In the basement room of the library Nora, snatching a cup of tea,
watched a man at the same table turning over the pages of a green paper
pamphlet as he sipped coffee. On the chair beside him were crutches. She
remembered that he was in one of her classes. As he read here and there,
he scowled and muttered to himself, unconscious of her watching him.
Something about the peace conference, Nora gathered from a chapter
heading.

‘Have you found something good about the war?’ she asked him at last.

He looked up, still scowling. ‘What every one will be thinking five
years from now.’

She looked at the author, the most famous of British dramatists. ‘I’d
like to get the book,’ she said.

‘I brought it back with me. You can’t get it here.’

‘I’d like to read it,’ she persisted.

‘You wouldn’t like it. No woman would.’

‘Do you think all women have the same opinions?’

‘Well—they’re idealists. They wouldn’t care for this straight stuff.
I’ve been home six months.’ He laughed.

‘I don’t pretend to know anything about the war. I’ve heard nothing from
any man who was actually there. I watch all of you talking together——’

‘If you’d like to read this—I’m through with it.’

She thanked him, and he hobbled away.

The book gave Nora a new boldness. She came to her classes late, the
seats in front all filled by the women who strove daily for those
closest to the lecturer. A vacant chair was to be found only in the rear
of the room. The men were talking. She caught stray words,
unintelligible slang, foreign phrases.

‘I can’t—I can’t believe it was for nothing,’ a strained voice said
behind her. ‘Being wounded—suffering the way some of us have—all for
nothing.’ The other men were silent. ‘I won’t believe it,’ he concluded
in the same grey tones.

How could a woman know what these men thought except by the crime of
eavesdropping? If she could only talk to them. Between them and her was
an invisible wall, stronger than stone and mortar.

Another time she heard; ‘But it all ends the same way. It was hell, but
not one of us would have missed it. Nothing in the end. Months wasted.
Everything here gone to the bow-wows. But those who didn’t get into the
scrap are outside. They know it. They don’t belong to our generation.’

Professor Nicholson had finished his introductory lectures. He was now
plunged into the intricacies of a new age, with its machinery in
factories, its locomotives and steamships. The classes were set to
drawing steam engines and spinning-jennies. When they had recovered from
the first bewilderment, they visited a cotton mill in New Jersey,
located in a gaunt town where smelly canals, a river stagnant with
refuse, and tenement houses built of a gloomy native stone swarmed with
the children whose parents were employed in the mill. It was not a
cheerful introduction. The mill itself was almost overpowering to those
who had never seen the textile industry at first hand. The smells, the
terrible heat in some of the rooms, the thundering noises were a
revelation of the heights to which human endurance could be stretched.
The preparing rooms were the most revolting. American cotton, the
dirtiest and the poorest on the market, was a tough proposition, some of
the men said. They got together and talked with Professor Nicholson, in
spite of the noise, when the foreman walked ahead. Nora tried to push
herself near them.

She caught a reference to the steel strike, which now made newspaper
headlines. The noise shut out the rest. Men talking together about vital
things, the facts without sentimentality, what happened from day to day,
and women on the outside exchanging the same twaddle. It was hopeless,
unfair, but what could she do about it?

A woman sticking close to her kept repeating: ‘I think it’s
dreadful—dreadful. I never knew it was like this. The only mill I’ve
ever been in was a flour mill—such a nice clean place.’

In the carding-room cotton fluff drifted like snow over the hair of the
girls, their faces stolid, almost inhuman. The atmosphere in which they
toiled eight hours daily had a stench like burned salad oil. The air
vibrated with the continuous screeching roar of the machinery. But when
the foreman’s back was turned, the girls’ lips moved.

Professor Nicholson afterward described the Apex Mill as above the
average, with good lighting, a fairly modern ventilating system, and
excellent vacuum apparatus for clearing the cotton fluff out of the air.
Although it wasn’t ideal, the general management was decent. The
problems of shifting foreign labor and competition with the Southern
cotton mills, with lax State laws and child labor, complicated the
policy of many employers. Nora remembered the reforming zeal of Ellen
Standish and her obdurate father. Perhaps Ellen had not been entirely
fair with him. The professor seemed to be straining himself for an
almost superhuman fairness, a little dark man near Nora remarked.

The lectures on industrial England were soon crowded with men. Extra
chairs had to be brought in. Professor Nicholson, his clear voice racing
over swift sentences, presented his material by running comments on
contemporary problems. He quoted novels Nora had never read, eloquent
with the misery of adjustment, the exploitation of the helpless, the
greed of capitalists whom the new era had given sudden power. He read
lines from a poet Nora thought she knew. This was the stuff men wanted
in a university. Some of the women were bewildered by the mechanical
terms and scientific conceptions.

‘Is this history?’ one of them inquired of Nora.

At the close of the hour the men in the back seats crowded around the
lecture platform. They got very much excited over the discussion of
issues raised, their voices loud, their eyes and arms enforcing what
they said. From chance phrases one guessed that they were fighting the
war all over again. These men transformed all they heard into their own
experience. As all roads lead to Rome, so did all history for them speak
in what they themselves had seen. And Nora on the outside could only
gnash her teeth in silent rage.

Nora sought out Professor Nicholson in his office. She was not satisfied
with her courses, she explained. They were too narrow, not giving her
the sweep of vision she must have. He listened while she reviewed what
she had studied, what she had tried to do the year before.

‘You’ve got a big job——nineteenth-century England,’ he commented when
she was through.

‘I know—I know very well. But I want to really understand—why England
was what she was—the attitude of other countries to her—all of it. I’ve
started——’

He advised her to go to literature. English—she knew fairly well. But
German and French——

‘Of course I’m outlining a job for life,’ he smiled, the baffling
freshness in his face. Freshness—youth—it was ideas, fresh ideas, caring
for them more than for life, which made a man look like Professor
Nicholson.

‘That’s what I want,’ she gasped eagerly, ‘a real job.’

Nora met the crippled soldier again in the basement refreshment room and
returned his book.

‘I agree with you,’ she began.

‘Really?’ His tone was almost rude.

As she was trying to frame a reply, another man sat down beside him.
‘Well, Perkins. How goes it?’

Mr. Perkins attempted an awkward introduction. ‘My friend John Harper.’

‘Nora Deming,’ she helped him. ‘Mr. Perkins and I have been talking
about the war.’

‘That’s what he and I talk about, too,’ the stranger said. He had a way
of looking straight at one when he talked that was a little
disconcerting at first. He was a graduate student in economics like
Perkins, he informed her. He had decided when he got out of the army
that he didn’t want to go back into business.

‘I was one of these efficiency experts,’ he said. ‘Office manager for
the Zenith Rubber Company. I enlisted because I got bored stiff making
patriotic speeches.’

He and Perkins laughed, and Nora, after a moment, joined them. Mr.
Harper was the first young business man she had ever known. One of the
new school strange to her. The business men in Maronac, even Eleanor’s
father at the Dry Docks, were leisurely in speech and action. The young
man evidently rushed things through. He spoke rapidly, examining his
listener with his restless eyes, never entirely serious, however.

Nora came upon the two men in the same place the following day. Mr.
Harper was starting a story.

‘I was telling about a German prisoner brought in by some men in our
company. He got down on his knees to the captain. A butcher with ten
children, he said. Fat as a sausage—middle-aged. We let him go. He
started off—looking back at us like a—a rat. Then he began to run. The
lines were so close together we could watch him almost up to his own
gang. All safe. He turned. Waved to us. His deliverers.’ The story was
interrupted by laughter. ‘Then he—he went right up in the air. Blown to
bits—pieces of him going straight up. We’d stuck a bomb with a time-fuse
in Fritz’s pocket. It went off just the right minute—inside his own
lines.’

Both men were laughing.

‘Do you think that’s a funny story?’ Nora inquired in amazement, looking
first at one and then at the other.

They were now roaring with laughter. ‘Killing,’ Mr. Harper explained.
‘Let me tell you the game we played with Hun prisoners that jumped on
the running-board of our staff car. We stepped on the gas and shot them
off like bottles in a row.’

Nora rose. ‘I’m afraid I haven’t learned war humor yet,’ she said.

Evidently a new kind had developed. A man limping across the campus
remarked to his companion, ‘They say the main advantage of a wooden leg
is that you can hold up your sock with a carpet tack. But even better—I
don’t have to change the sock on my game foot. It can be regarded as
more or less permanent.’


 II

On Sunday afternoon Nora was invited to Professor Nicholson’s to tea. A
stuffy, overheated room filled with people, too much furniture, and an
overdressed, gushing woman fussing about among her guests. In the
confusion Nora discerned two or three graduate students grouped around
Professor Nicholson. Mrs. Nicholson at once began describing to Nora her
terrible sufferings while her husband was in France.

‘I never thought I’d live through it. The days—the nights worse. Of
course he didn’t have to go. But I never complained.’ She finished with
a grimace, intended for a smile, which made Nora inwardly shudder. ‘I
suppose you’re crazy about my husband. All his students are. It’s all I
hear—how wonderful he is.’

She then turned to the woman on the other side, evidently an old friend,
and began an exchange of gossip, mentioning some of Nora’s professors,
in a high-pitched voice.

Before Nora left, she had a few moments with Professor Nicholson. He
expressed regret that he had been unable to talk with her, hoped to see
her again soon to hear how she was getting on. Then she left, dismissed
with another grimace from her hostess, and a hard throaty sound
caricaturing laughter.

On her way back to the dormitory Nora met Mr. Harper, who asked if he
might join her. After a few preliminary remarks, he began to talk of
himself. In the midst of his economic studies he was trying to get
caught up with the new books. His friend Perkins was a great reader. But
both he and Perkins floundered in places. In the realm of ideas, the
discussion of contemporary problems, they felt safe enough. But all this
art stuff——

Nora in a spirit of mischief advised him to read ‘The Tragic Muse.’
‘James, you know,’ she explained.

‘Is that he-man stuff?’

‘I don’t know. It depends on the man, you see——’

‘All this diagnosis of the soul since the war. I’m not much for that.
Wells—in those first books. He started me going—that man.’

‘But did he carry you very far? I like my ideas dished out straight. Not
lost in all his verbiage.’

‘Of course Shaw,’ went on Mr. Harper; ‘there’s the giant of the crowd.
And I’m reading Tolstoy.’

He hovered at the door of the women’s hall until Nora had to ask him in.
He looked around with his appraising glance. She could see that he was
amused. He settled himself on the nearest sofa, not quite at ease, but
making a pretense of boldness, as the graduate women filed past slowly
enough to make an examination of him and Nora sitting there.

‘It was the army that got me out of business,’ he was finally
explaining. ‘Queer how it was, too. I was on to all the tricks. They’re
the same ones. The only difference is that business is efficient. Things
move along. In the army it was an unholy mess. I never wasted time
hating the Germans. They were a devilish smart crowd—a lot o’ them. But
the colonel of our outfit—when he wasn’t drunk——’

‘I understand,’ Nora said.

‘Well—lugging him out of one scrape after another——Orders one day. All
off the next. What my poor men had to stand—I was swearing blue devils
most o’ the time. Old Jack we called him—old Jack loved power. To make
us hop around just to see us hop—that was the man. I got some of my old
stuff handed back to me. Business organization—it means driving men
under you. I was good at it. But the army got under my skin. I couldn’t
come back and see men scared to death—white-collar slaves—jumping around
when I snapped my fingers. If I go into business again—I won’t be a
slave-driver. I haven’t got it thought out yet. But in a year here——You
ought to hear Doctor Lewis lecture. Industrial Problems. It’s great
stuff. The man knows what he’s talking about. He can’t fool me.’

The dinner bell rang, and Mr. Harper rose. ‘D’you ever get out of this
place?’

‘Oh, yes,’ Nora assured him.

‘Could you—would you give me the pleasure—a show or something——’


 III

Everywhere Nora went she met John Harper, always correctly dressed and
with something new to talk over. In the dingy basement room, up in the
history stacks where she foraged around for books, and in the
reading-room used by economics as well as by history students. His
energy carried him everywhere. When she left the library before dinner,
he, too, was ready to leave for the day. At first she regarded these
encounters as accidental. Then something in the way he looked at her, a
new softness in his sharply darting eyes, made her self-conscious and
shy.

‘Can’t we have dinner somewhere?’ he suggested, standing on the steps of
the dormitory. Inside Nora knew a business meeting was awaiting her.

‘Thanks very much. Yes, I should like to. But I must hurry to my
meeting now.’ The closing of the door gave her a sense of relief, of
escape.

Three solemn young men on a sofa balancing tea-cups were facing two
women in the parlor. Nora, the vice-president of the Women’s History
Club, arriving late, was being introduced by Miss Stark, the president,
a very energetic woman, to what she learned was the executive committee
of the Men’s History Club.

‘I feel so sorry for all these young girls coming to New York and never
meeting any of you young men here at Knickerbocker,’ Miss Stark was
saying.

One of the young men looked interested, a second cautious, the third,
also the president, alarmed.

‘I didn’t come to Knickerbocker to meet young women,’ he declared, with
a candor Nora could not help admiring.

As the discussion developed, she had a confused recollection of Miss
Stark’s theory that the two societies devoted to historical studies
should become one.

‘It doesn’t seem fair for us women to miss the lively discussions you
men have,’ the secretary, Miss Fleet, said, with a smile which swept
over the trio on the sofa. Miss Fleet in Missouri, Nora suspected, had a
way with men.

‘Our meetings are very dull,’ the interested young man observed.

‘Perhaps the young women would liven them up for you,’ Miss Stark
ventured.

‘Scholarly discussions are never lively,’ the president remarked, with
finality, looking around him as if he wanted to escape.

‘Don’t you think it would be a good idea for the two clubs to join, Miss
Deming?’

Nora started. Miss Stark was looking to her support.

‘I—I hadn’t thought of it before. I don’t know. But the men don’t seem
to want it.’ Her glance was directed toward the president. He appeared a
little flustered.

Miss Stark drew herself up proudly in her high-backed chair. ‘I never
knew before there was sex in history.’

‘There’s sex in everything,’ the president said firmly. Then, reaching
for a battered hat, he rose and shook hands solemnly with Miss Stark,
concluding with a stiff jerk of a bow. The cautious young man was beside
him. The enthusiastic young man was still talking to Miss Fleet about
the book he was writing, but, when they were close to the door, he, too,
scuttled away.

‘Why didn’t you help me out?’ Miss Stark turned upon Nora.

The women’s club was stupid enough. But whether the addition of men
would help it any was uncertain. An unwieldy organization, trying
clumsily to be wise, or, better still, according to Miss Stark, to be
sociable, did not attract Nora.

It was clear enough the men didn’t want the women. Men serious about
their work usually preferred to keep to themselves. They could be
violent with their own sex, if they had to be. But with women—that
outworn chivalry. It would always keep men and women apart. Chivalry may
have gone along well enough when fair ladies kept to their bowers behind
castle walls. But now, when women were getting into everything, a man
had to look out. If he could fight her like another man——But he could
only despise her and wish she’d stay in her place.

And what women studying history at Knickerbocker had anything to
contribute to the men’s discussions? Was there anything that they really
yearned to have dragged out into the fierce light of real mental combat?
Around Nora daily buzzed girls from every State in the Union, many of
them, as Miss Stark had hinted, crazy to meet young men. They were open
about declaring, for any one to hear, just why they had come to
Knickerbocker University. Studying was an excuse for being in New York.
New York was crowded with young men, they had thought back home. But
once in the great city, tucked away in a woman’s hall, with the matron’s
experienced eye upon them, existence had no immediate flame. They joked
before Nora about sitting beside the good-looking men at lectures. They
asked them the time, borrowed their newspapers, appealed to them when
their fountain pens went dry. But graduate students were nuts—most of
them—and even the handsomer ones hadn’t a cent. Far more alluring the
hall just across the street, that wide, relentless barrier. The girls
had recently been experimenting with lamp signals. Two or three gathered
in one room. Then they began turning a study-lamp off and on in a new
kind of wig-wagging game. A quick response from Knickerbocker
undergraduates. Between them a code was developed. Meetings were
arranged on near-by corners. One girl returned from a Greenwich Village
dance at six in the morning. The matron was growing suspicious. The
older women in the dormitory must have told tales.

Women past their first youth have so little sympathy with the adventures
of their own sex. There was Miss Johnston on Nora’s corridor. She had
become a joke, a kind of tradition. No one knew how many years she had
been studying at Knickerbocker. She had long ago taken every course
offered by the history department. A new series of lectures roused her
