like a small boy hearing a fire-engine. Off once more. One more subject
to conquer. She was engulfed in some vast scheme in which she wandered
over anthropological wastes and historic dawns to prove her theory. Her
book, when it was done, might run into three volumes. Once she had hoped
to crowd it down into two. In all this she was supported by the earnings
of an elderly uncle, who had once dreamed of scholarship and had got
stranded in business. His niece read on year after year—miles and miles
of books—and told him all about her latest and best conclusion.

Then there was Anna Downing, waiting day by day for the arrival of her
brother’s body from France. He had been a captain, a much-loved older
brother. Her face fell into deeper grey lines, she drank more cups of
coffee, and her eyes glittered uncomfortably for those who talked to
her. Only last year she had been the wittiest of Nora’s neighbors.

Alice Conway was a poet. At least she wrote verse and studied
philosophy, a not infrequent combination. She had recently got the
notion that the dormitory women were making derogatory remarks behind
her back—saying her mind was affected. She wanted them to know she was
as sane as anybody. If she found the person who started the story about
her not being quite right——

All of the women Nora knew studied too much, with the conscientiousness
they should have outgrown with pigtails. Their chief satisfaction was in
recounting how many pages they read daily, how many consecutive hours
they toiled in the library, how late they sat up over their work. They
admitted that they were always tired—nothing seemed worth while. If they
went to a symphony concert, the music shot nervous pains all through
their bodies, like an electric shock. And the theatre—what they had
looked forward to more than anything else—probably they had been too
tired to go.

Why do women cling to the phantom—that most deceptive phantom—the joy
one finds in the play of the mind? They cling to it long after all other
hopes are gone. Happiness or intelligence in women—which is rarer? Do
any women have both? The Brontë sisters—genius and wretchedness. What
they did—walled around with the stony barriers of a limited experience.
Elizabeth Barrett—but was she really intelligent? George Eliot—getting
revenge for the stiff provincial conscience of Marian Evans by
tormenting poor little Hetty Sorrel. And the women of power.
Catharine of Russia—Queen Elizabeth in England—giants both of
them—relentless—using men for their own determined ends. But when were
they happy?

The little blonde girl from Tennessee worked harder than any one. At
least the strain bore down upon her more. She had gone to a very poor
college. She realized that now. Most of the time she didn’t know what
the professor was talking about—just chaos. She read sentences in
reference books again and again, but she couldn’t make them out. What
was the matter with her? She appealed to Nora. At night her throat ached
so. She couldn’t sleep. Maybe she ought to go home. But no, she couldn’t
do that.

One night all of Nora’s floor was roused past midnight. People rushing
back and forth. The poor little thing from Tennessee had jumped out of
her window. She was dead when they found her on the pavement below.


 IV

John Harper said women couldn’t stand the strain of too much study—at
least not many of them.

‘They go straight off,’ he said, seated across the table from Nora in an
Italian restaurant south of the Square, not unlike the one where Nora
and Mary Howe listened to the poetic raptures of Eddie Gaston that night
so long ago. ‘The reason, clearly enough, is that they don’t really want
to study. Now if you force yourself to do a thing—put on the screws
tight—there’s trouble. Like this poor girl you told me about. Collapse.’
Mr. Harper, with none of Helen’s agile psychological terms, had the
latest theories.

‘Yes,’ Nora agreed, ‘but what about the women who really prefer the
intellectual life to everything else—their professional career whatever
it may be? What of them?’

‘Let them have it,’ he declared generously. ‘They’re the minority.’ He
looked across at Nora with his disconcerting directness. ‘Is that what
you prefer?’

Nora blushed, looked down at her plate. ‘I—I don’t know. But my
friends—several of them who have already done splendid work—it’s the
first rank—competing with men——’

‘Women in business—really good ones—I met them once in a while. But they
were hard. And at Knickerbocker—well——’

‘The good ones are there. Not many, of course. But how many really good
men are there either?’

Nora took John Harper one Sunday afternoon to have tea with Helen
Spring. They got on famously together.

‘He’s a real man,’ Helen observed to Nora afterward. ‘I like him. I’m
glad you’re meeting men up there at the University.’ She sighed. ‘After
a whole week of women——But, of course, that’s my job.’

Helen had taken her first position, a good one she was told by her
superiors, in a home for fallen girls, concealed under the euphemistic
name ‘Rest Cottage.’ But its inmates never rested. They were like caged
lionesses waiting to escape back to their old life. They put on the
proper conversions. They told Helen sobbing stories of early
temptations, cruel parents, poverty. All that was true enough. But their
emotion didn’t conceal their hatred of the peace of Rest Cottage, where
they were safe, well fed, and employed in tasks designed to be
unexciting and corrective.

To a scientist convinced that the new psychology will immediately
minister to a mind diseased, Rest Cottage was discouraging. But one
found there every form of mental variety. It was a remarkable
laboratory. The inmates, properly pigeonholed by the staff of
psycho-analysts, were a most satisfying group of individuals for
scientific purposes.

Helen’s speech during the first experiments in her new job was an almost
unintelligible jargon to laity like Nora. Simple introverts and
extraverts, objective extraverts and introverts, emotional introverts
and subjective extraverts. It was as bad as old Polonius and his
classification of plays. ‘“Pastoral-comical, historical-pastoral,
tragical-historical, tragical-comical-historical-pastoral,”’ jeered
Nora. ‘“Manics, dementia præcox, paranoiacs,”’ she recited.

‘You keep quiet,’ Helen threatened, starting off next on libidos, which
were equally various—collective, unconscious, desexualized, introverted,
extraverted, excessive—many more.

But gradually the job got hold of Helen. She wanted to cure those poor
creatures. When all the analyzing was done, what next? They didn’t want
to be different. Gradually Helen suspected what they did want. The
husband of one of the psychologists knew what they wanted most. To the
pretty, lively ones, he gave it. One of Helen’s patients confided that
he felt sorry for the girls. He was such an affectionate man, she added.
All the time he encouraged his wife to go on with her remarkable cures.
It was a terrible mess. And Helen, knowing all that horror, had to keep
quiet.

Then Zoe Ryder refused to go on any more cases. She’d starve first. But
before beginning the process of starvation, described as painless by
those who have tried its early stages, Zoe borrowed money of all her
friends. The money she used for her new business venture of making
lamp-shades. Out of scraps of silk she contrived boudoir lamps of every
color and design—as delicate as flowers. Parchment and paper, cretonnes,
chintzes—she contrived an endless variety. They were all charming.
Before Christmas she had more orders than she could fill. But sitting in
a little back room in Greenwich Village making lamp-shades got tiresome
long before spring. For a change Zoe lived in luxury and ease, sat in
Washington Square, and began to write a play, with all the neighborhood
Italian children swarming around her. A friend got her the offer of
being a permanent nurse to a delicate child, but Zoe would not listen to
flattery about her magic ways with children. She made the rounds of her
friends a second time, less successfully, but still she got enough to
keep the play going. She would never make another lamp-shade. She
quarrelled with Helen, who made the suggestion. A tea-room in the
Village, where only creative artists came, was now her great ambition.
If she could get the necessary capital——In the meantime actors were
begging her for parts in her new play. She was a pathological case,
Helen stated, scolding Amelia for lending her more money.

Vicky’s Lady Needleworkers had been out on strike all winter. Vicky told
them to hold firm. They hung on, eating less and less, trying to stretch
out the union relief fund to the utmost of its capacity. Then they came
to Vicky, who had been leading their fight so valiantly. They couldn’t
pay her salary. They reminded her that last year they paid every cent.
Three thousand dollars. But Vicky couldn’t live without money. She would
have to resign. Her mother and brother, still keeping guard from the
windows of a Park Avenue apartment hotel, might have tided her over. But
that would have meant the regular life appropriate to those regions. The
needleworkers were sorry. She had lent elegance to their cause—a real
lady and all. It was too much to expect she’d keep on with them. But
they’d hoped——

Amelia was furious at Vicky’s desertion of these plucky fighters.

‘She’ll never get another job at three thousand dollars a year because
she was born a lady,’ commented Helen sagely. ‘She’s lazy—untrained.’

‘And dishonest,’ added Amelia. ‘She’s tried to conceal the fact that
she’s been living with Max Goldberg all winter. If she’d been honest
about it——’

‘Well,’ Helen excused her, ‘she knew he’d tire of her soon. He generally
demands more intelligence than she’s got to show. Don’t be too hard,
Amelia.’

When Nora told John Harper about Miss Hamilton’s treachery to the
needleworkers, he was much amused.

‘What else did you expect? There’s no one so keen about money as those
who have had it. Your friend is a good business woman. No nonsense about
her. I respect her. She’ll come out on top. And the needleworkers—when
the trades get rid of their snobbery and sentiment, they won’t need
ladies to manage their scraps for ’em.’

There was something hard and relentless about John Harper. Was it only
masculinity? Nora had known too few men to be sure. If only—something
about him—not quite. How disgusting she was! How narrow and stupid! She
revolted from her own thoughts.

While Vicky looked for a new job, Amelia used the bits of leisure left
her by the Labor College in getting Tony married off to somebody. She
was bored with Tony—not only Tony, but all Italians. They were
materialistic—at least on American soil. The Russians were her next
hope—the only people who in the twentieth century still have souls.

‘Well,’ sighed Amelia as the sixteen-year-old bride of Antonio d’Angelo
floated out of the church under a canopy of bridal veil and orange
blossoms beside her beautiful new husband, ‘I kept him out of the gang.
She can take care of him now.’

Amelia was busy soliciting funds for a new colony of rare spirits to be
started in the country, providing a combination of intellectual and
manual labor, and in connection a school for the poor, Russians
preferred.

‘Hasn’t that been tried before?’ asked Nora. ‘Some of the colony don’t
work. Others get tired of one another and start fighting.’

‘All of us will dress in grey—a neutral shade,’ Amelia persisted,
thinking only of her newest enterprise.

When the news of Garrison Smith’s marriage to an English socialist came,
Amelia had just been informed that the son of a fabulously rich father
had inherited more than a million dollars to lay at the feet of the
colony. Amelia had no time for anything else. Garrison was hardly
mentioned.

‘It’s a blessing she’s got this other thing,’ breathed Helen. ‘Don’t say
anything against her colony, Nora. You’ve got your young man caught. I
can see that.’

‘Helen!’ warned Nora, turning crimson. Nora had brought Mr. Harper down
to tea again. ‘Don’t you say that. If you do, I’ll never—never see him
again.’

‘Don’t be a fool, my child.’

The rush of events warned Nora that John Harper must soon be reckoned
with. This determined young man, who had been successful in everything
else he had undertaken, was in love with Nora. Any one could see it. It
burst out of him everywhere like a riotous flood, Helen, looking
straight at Nora with a defiant air, told her.

Nora began placing obstacles in the way of his frequent invitations, but
somehow he climbed over them. Often she was relieved when he did.
Dinners in quiet restaurants away from the noise and the smell of the
cafeteria, surrounded by people without the scars of intellectual
struggles, examining a menu card with no thrifty eyes on the price list,
spinning away from dinner to the theatre in a taxi without watching the
expensive revolutions of the metre, enjoying a play or a concert
unhampered by the cramped discomfort of balcony heights—were luxuries
Nora had come to count upon during the winter. More than that was the
stimulation of talking to a man with vigorous opinions, whose
prejudices, even if they were irritating, were at least never feminine.
There was the new sensation of not having to look out for one’s self, to
cross the street with no fear of cars darting out of unexpected corners,
to walk along a dark passage without even a shiver of trepidation, to
escape in companionship one’s own doubts.

‘But you can’t go on this way forever,’ warned Helen, who had studied
all of the uncertain ways of men and women.

Nora and John Harper, walking home briskly from the theatre, crossed the
park. Under the trees, with their lacy shadows over the path, there was
escape from the glare of the lights and the steady traffic, a thin
quietness with a lazy, pleasant hum beneath it. Nora did not see the
lovers on the benches locked in heavy, passionate embraces, nor the
homeless men slinking off to a dark corner to find a bed away from the
policeman’s beat. She paused close to the Japanese cherry trees in the
height of blossoming.

‘How sweet—sweet,’ she murmured, turning to her companion.

They stood breathing in the scented spring air. She took a step forward,
but strong arms held her back, enclosed her.

‘At last—my darling,’ he breathed close to her cheek. He was crushing
her against him with his rough, masculine strength, outraging some blind
fighting instinct in her.

‘No—no!’ She tried to free herself, trembling with rage.

‘I love you,’ he went on, not releasing her, pressing his lips against
hers. ‘My proud little girl.’

‘No,’ she repeated, pulling herself away with a quick wrench, clenching
her lips against the sobs in her throat.

‘You mean you don’t care for me?’

She began to walk rapidly, faster and faster, gathering speed, almost
running over the ground. If she could get back on the crowded pavements,
with people passing and lights, she would be safe.

‘You don’t need to be afraid of me,’ the voice beside her was saying.

They went the rest of the way in silence, she still sweeping over the
terrifying stretch of streets like a home-bound swallow, he stalking
beside her with hard, pounding strides. At the door of the women’s
dormitory, she said a quick good-night.

In her room she was eager to turn out her light, to hide herself away in
the darkness. How dared he? she kept repeating to herself. Lying in bed,
she trembled all over. A chill seized her, coming out in icy drops on
her forehead, making her lips chatter, her fingers stiffen, her feet
dead weights. Then she was burning up with fever. This man had done
something to her—battered down the outer fortifications of her woman’s
world, made her, for the first time, afraid—afraid because she was a
woman.

She knew now why she had run away—locked herself up in this room high
above the city streets. She must save herself. If he had urged her—held
out against her battling opposition—something inside might have
crumpled, and she——That was what it meant to be a woman—weak,
surrendering to brute force.

But she had saved herself. They didn’t match. She knew. They didn’t
belong to each other.

Toward morning, with the dawn making timid grey shadows in her room,
Nora was calm enough to go back over everything. There had been doubts
from the start. He was too well dressed, too sure of himself, too
self-consciously correct. And too—too hard. Almost the first time she
met him he affected her like shiny new furniture in shop windows. She
had tried to remember his finer qualities, his kindness and generosity.
But those things dear to her, which made the delicate flavor to life,
did not exist for him. No—no—they could never belong to each other.

The morning light danced over the white paper on Nora’s study table, the
cover on the bureau, the expanse of mirror, the walls. There were
flickers of dawn, then the sunshine at last in her face. Drawing up the
covers, she hid herself away from the hard brightness of the day, and
wept.


 CHAPTER III


 I

Nora was unpacking her trunk in her old room in Petersfield. Margaret
Abington, looking in at the door, hesitated shyly at the threshold.

‘I kept hoping all the time,’ sighed Margaret, ‘that something
better—something would turn up in New York.’

‘You don’t want me here, then?’ Nora looked up from the depths of her
trunk.

‘I was trying to eliminate my own choice,’ Margaret said solemnly.

Margaret looked very tired, worn, sucked dry. They had come up to
Petersfield on the same train after two busy days in New York spent in
arranging Margaret’s fall exhibition, a ceremony which generally left
her exhausted but radiant. But as Nora had watched her old friend,
silent and gloomy, superintending the hanging of her pictures, she knew
that Margaret wasn’t well. And yet, standing in the little gallery taken
over by the New Society of Women Artists before Margaret’s last
painting—the doorway of a fisherman’s cottage opening out upon a dull
sea, in the foreground the gay, old-fashioned flowers which grow best
with simple care, their bright colors standing out with a kind of
jubilation against the troubled sky and sea—one sensed the joy of
creation. Margaret, so sombre herself—all the energy driven out of her.

‘Those fleeting precious summer weeks,’ she murmured, as if she were
alone. The brilliance in her eyes burned for an instant, then changed to
dull despair. ‘My summer all gone. So little done.’ She crept out of the
room.

In returning to her profession, Nora was trying to convince herself that
she had no illusions about her future. The vows of poverty, chastity,
and obedience, without the religious exaltation—these were the rules of
the order to which she was attached. But if a woman threw herself
entirely into her work, she seemed in the end to find something which
fed her spirit.

There was Ruth Ewing. Nora had visited her at her scientific station on
the Massachusetts coast not far from Margaret’s studio. After a
laborious day hunting and studying specimens, Ruth leaped into the sea
and followed the path of the moonlight. In the water, beauty, grace, the
swiftness of a fish or a mermaid came to this plain little spinster. Her
emotions swept over the sky, over birds, butterflies, bugs of the less
ethereal kingdoms, flowers, plants, germs, with the same unspoiled joy.
Men like John Harper would say that science dried up a woman, but
nothing would ever dry up the pools of Ruth Ewing’s fresh spirit. Nora
and Margaret had left her in a New York laboratory working beside a man,
both of them trying to isolate the influenza germ. Neither one thought
of his own glorification. They were the servants of sick humanity. Their
sexless coöperation seemed to Nora very fine. Disembodied mind—that was
the goal of mankind. As she watched their generosity, each placing the
results of his former experiments at the disposal of the other, she felt
something of the heights to which science, once so lowly and despised,
has climbed. Hearing Ruth talk made one realize what an intellectual
outlet meant for women.

But Margaret Abington said Ruth was a pathetic creature. Margaret didn’t
appreciate science. Nor did she appreciate Ruth’s strenuous enjoyment of
the natural world, without contemplation or real æsthetic appreciation,
always darting after something outdoors to name and classify it, to add
it to her store of memory. Margaret’s way was so different. She let
beauty take her unaware. But the result for her as for Ruth was the
same; the same absorption, the same fusion of the individual with his
accomplishment.

The problem of women whose entire sex interest has gone into their work
belongs to the present. The old painters sentimentalized the virgin with
her rapturous gaze upon her young child. But physical creation is the
labor of only one class of women. Miss Steele had the uplifted
expression of a Raphael Madonna when she started a new book and Ruth
Ewing when she was pursuing a new germ. This emotional devotion to some
kind of work—this was what Nora must find if Petersfield was to be
endurable.

Saying good-bye to Michael and Ellen Powys on the pier, Ellen with her
first baby, sure that nothing was important but babies, Nora felt
debased—frustrated—wasted in life. Women with intellectual progeny never
flaunted their self-satisfaction that way. Nora didn’t care if she never
saw Ellen again. Michael’s father had died, leaving a generous fortune
to his son with the stipulation that he never step his foot in the
factory, reserved to the management of Michael’s conservative older
brother. Of course they were disappointed. But with the money they were
embarking to London to start a new liberal weekly. At least Michael was.
Ellen, as a mother, left the reform of the world to others. Nora waved
to her old friend and watched her sail out of her life with no regrets.
The women at Petersfield—they meant her future.

A return to the old life. But during the first days Nora felt like a
stranger. The war had changed Petersfield. During the house shortage,
commuters had discovered the quiet village, had built a row of
attractive suburban dwellings on the street leading to the campus, and
persuaded the railroad to put on express trains between New York and
Petersfield, with excellent service. The journey was now simpler than
the subway stretch between Knickerbocker University and Wall Street. For
a member of the faculty, returning from graduate study with an
indefinite stretch of years divided between teaching and mad plunges
into Knickerbocker lectures and seminars, residence within suburban
limits had special advantages.

But there were other changes. The girls had changed. Most of them had
bobbed hair, wore short skirts, and adopted a costume common over the
entire country, one heard, among college women. There were two
variations: a smart sweater over a ruffled blouse and a pleated skirt,
or the pleated skirt with a blouse in coat effect fastened below the
waist with a leather belt. A plain felt hat completed the desired
effect. Even the woollen stockings now in vogue kept close to one model.
Girls between seventeen and twenty-two all looked alike, and the
delicacy and grace which a democracy in fashion did not entirely conceal
was hidden as if the owner were ashamed of distinction.

Possibly the college world has always appropriated a homogeneous style
to characterize its own order. She may have forgotten. There was the
Peter Thompson sailor-suit just waning in Nora’s freshman year. Yet
surely there was more individuality then.

Walking alone at the end of her first day of lectures, Nora approached
the graveyard where the Suffrage League had kept its night vigils. She
laughed aloud at the memory. She wanted to see how the old place looked.
She heard voices.

Seated on the tombstones was a group of girls all having an afternoon
smoke together. When they saw one of the faculty, they rose, greeted her
cordially, and invited her to join them. No traces of embarrassment,
even though cigarettes were forbidden by college rules. Conservative
mothers from the Middle West still terrorized over the academic
authorities.

Everywhere one heard about the new frankness among girls. Frankness was
their code. They acted as they thought, Nora soon observed. A
distinguished English poet came to lecture, and the girls, expecting to
hear him read his verse, crowded the hall. But he happened to be a
critic as well, and, in a voice so low it reached only the first ten or
twelve rows, read a highly involved study of a Victorian poet. The
students in the back rows slid out. Those in the centre began trickling
away. Bolder spirits marched out of the hall. At last only those near
the platform remained.

Professor Steele was enraged by such rudeness to a guest. But the girls
proclaimed their intention of defending themselves against boredom. No
good came of it. Let lecturers speak out good and loud, and they would
stay to listen.

In the exchange of undergraduate ideas speech was full of psychological
tags. Only a friend like Helen enabled Nora to meet this new college
generation. A freshman was greeted her first day with a charge of the
Œdipus complex, against which she must defend herself. Intimacies
between two girls were watched with keen, distrustful eyes. Among one’s
classmates, one looked for the bisexual type, the masculine girl
searching for a feminine counterpart, and one ridiculed their devotions.

‘But are they really so sophisticated as they seem?’ Nora appealed to
Margaret Abington as she finished recounting a student’s avowal of
feminine independence, in which adventures of sex loomed dangerously.

‘They’re as innocent as babes,’ Margaret declared. ‘It’s pathetic. All
that talk is a protective armor. Those who don’t talk—the old-fashioned,
languishing ones—look out for them. It was like that in your day. You’ve
forgotten. When you’re young, you like to express yourself.’

A group of girls having dinner with Nora began to argue about the
difference between a flirt and a coquette.

‘A flirt,’ ventured a precocious freshman, whose older brother was of
the opinion that girls ought to learn to think and had accordingly
presented his sister with a set of Shaw, ‘a flirt is a woman who is
willing to rouse a man’s passion without being willing to satisfy it.’

The two older girls blushed beyond their rouge.

In this post-war era men flocked to Petersfield from all the colleges
for week-end visits. A man with a car speeded over the roads from Boston
to Petersfield as a relief from mental fatigue. The campus was like a
country fair. The visitors needed no special entertainment.
Roller-skating had become a fad among the girls. A youth, therefore,
roller-skated, hand in hand with his lady-love, who, if she were a
stalwart maiden, could hold him up while he was learning to balance
himself. There were bicycle races on the college walks, and Miss Steele
was almost run down. Most of the faculty went into retirement during
this frightful week-end period. Even the country roads were no longer
peacefully secluded. Youth was an athletic generation with tireless
legs. The week-enders explored the countryside. A girl, walking with her
visitor’s arm tossed carelessly over her shoulder as they trudged along,
was a frequent faculty vision of horror. There were picnics in one’s
secret haunts—droves of them. Margaret Abington gave up her walks with
Nora. But Nora could only be thankful all this hadn’t come in her day.
Then, men visited Petersfield for proms or to announce their
engagements. Other times one had peace. For the girls without swains to
exhibit, this new order necessitated advertising one’s failure to
attract men. Nora watched the female squads doing everything together,
pretending to be superior to these ruddy boys in knickerbockers, but
looking over their shoulders at them just the same.

Ruth Ewing insisted from the first that the new ways were better. Her
students told her about the violent arguments they had with their
masculine week-enders about the war, which had touched none of them, so
they could talk; about the present Administration and its shameful
attitude toward the League of Nations. World affairs were vital to the
best of these girls. They were arranging debates with the men’s
colleges. The Oxford debaters were coming to Petersfield to cross swords
with them next year. It was wonderful. In November a conference on
social questions was to be held at the college, with delegates from the
men’s colleges as well as the women’s.

Through all this revolution, however, the faculty was unchanged. The
feeble representation of men had not increased. What men there were
seemed more old-maidish than the women, with minds far inferior. Many
were there, Nora suspected, because they couldn’t compete with men in
the men’s colleges. The curriculum, too, was just the same, even though
the President announced that the colleges since the war were attracting
a new type of girl, formerly confined to the finishing schools. But
courses in household arts, by which this society type might be
benefited, had not been provided. When faculty discussions touched upon
the necessity, Miss Steele fought against such innovations. Petersfield
was a college of arts. Let these frivolous creatures who lowered
academic standards go to an institute for domestic science. The number
of girls entering teaching had also declined. But the old training for a
pedagogical career was still the backbone of the college.

Nora going down to New York twice a week, spending Saturday in the
Knickerbocker library, in the moments snatched for contemplation
pondered over what she could do. On the train travelling back and forth
she went over this huge problem. The old traditions and ideals were
gone. What was to take their place?

Her ambition for the students who crowded her courses was to give them
an understanding of the impartial mind, how one got it, what results it
gained. She tried to show them the confused, wasted world into which
their youth had been born; she put before them their special problems
and responsibilities. She talked about the international point of view,
read them the preface to the new Shaw play which had appeared that
summer; ‘The Wicked Half-Century,’ ‘War Delirium,’ ‘The Long Arm of
War,’ ‘The Sufferings of the Sane,’ ‘How the Fools Shouted the Wise Men
Down.’ But while she read, she was conscious of the daily press in the
fall of 1920, with its headlines trumpeting the hunting down of
dangerous ‘aliens and radicals,’ terms largely synonymous, the
deportation of harmless foreigners, and the madness of Americanization
propaganda. What difference did her poor feeble words make?

But she continued to remind the girls that studies in history, unless
one first tried to be impartial, were a pure waste of time. Go into
something else. The classics or a new language. Mathematics.

The descendants of the Socialist League of Nora’s day were now
concentrated upon the Russian Soviets. They looked to the radical
history instructor, Miss Deming, as a patroness for the cause of real
freedom.

‘But are your Bolshevists really giving Russia freedom?’ she demanded
the day she was asked to address a meeting. ‘Isn’t this a new form of
tyranny?’

Her audience looked at her, their courtesy fighting against hostility.
She was evidently like all the rest of the faculty, blind to the beauty
and tragedy of Russia, struggling against her old shackles. She had
deceived them with her animated talk.

Miss Steele told Nora she was becoming an excellent teacher. ‘I don’t
know what you’re doing with them,’ she said. ‘But they’re
interested—stirred up. I hear them talking. You’re waked up yourself,
Nora. Your trips to New York—the University—it won’t let you get stale.’

So the winter passed.


 II

One night Margaret Abington knocked at Nora’s door. She came in, and lay
down on her couch, utterly exhausted.

‘It’s the weather,’ Nora said. ‘The first of spring. I feel tired, too,
these days.’

Margaret said nothing.

‘I wonder,’ she murmured half to herself, ‘if it’s worth while going on.
One always feels sure the next thing will be better.’

She rose slowly, paused at Nora’s door, her fingers playing with the
knob. Her face wore its characteristic twisted, tortured smile.

‘You’re so strong, Nora,’ she said. ‘So—so firm and courageous. If I——’
She stopped, looked back at Nora, and was gone.

Late the next afternoon word got around that Miss Abington was missing.
She had not come down to breakfast, but that was not unusual. At
luncheon she was still gone. Nora went up to her room. It was empty. A
search was begun. Nora was questioned.

‘Did she seem pessimistic?’ they asked her.

‘No more than usual.’

‘You noticed nothing? What she said?’

‘She so often says queer things—when she’s tired.’

Not until the next day did they find her. A farmer three miles beyond
the college saw a hat floating down the swollen stream which ran through
his property. She had been dead some time when they took her out of the
water. Not far away was a basket weighted down with stones holding the
bodies of her two cats. She had not been willing to leave them behind
her.

In all the sad arrangements Nora and her friends had to make during the
next days, they found Margaret Abington’s life singularly solitary. She
had provided for no disposal of her possessions, even her paintings.
Some wealthy distant cousins near Boston to whom Nora wrote felt
outraged that the Abington name had been dragged into the newspapers.
They had no suggestions to make about her pictures. They did not care
for the kind of thing she did. They had been collecting Gainsboroughs
for years and were now started on Romneys. It was clear enough from
their letters that they had never given their gifted cousin a penny to
help her along with her work nor the blessed boon of a little leisure.

Miss Abington’s canvases became the possession of the college, all but
the picture of the flowers growing in the fisherman’s doorway, which
went to Nora. And to the fall exhibition, arranged in the art lecture
room, fashionable and artistic New York crowded. Miss Abington had made
Petersfield famous. The President was very much pleased. He immediately
forgot that he had never cared for Miss Abington. He thought of himself
now as the guardian of a sacred shrine. For the pictures, which the
critics tardily praised, he conceived the idea of a beautiful gallery
where the best of contemporary paintings should be gathered. He came out
in the New York papers, called on art patrons, and wrote impressive
letters to wealthy men. In a few months he announced to the college that
the art building was assured.

That the name of one so shrinking and unassuming should be tossed about
by the daily press and the college generally was unendurable to Nora.
Fame for a talent so delicate must be clean and pure. Nora saw it
besmirched by the stupidity of the rabble. In Margaret’s lifetime a
select company had appreciated her. She had had no mercy for fools. Now
she was defenceless against the enthusiasm of the loquacious dilettante.
Nora could not bear it. She kept away from the art lecture room, avoided
the sight of Margaret’s successor, a hard, confident young person who
talked about pictures as if she were trying to sell them.

Margaret—the dear companion, so tortured by her own fastidious nature,
so affectionate with her friends, so fierce against the rest of the
world—Nora would keep for herself. She no longer mentioned Margaret’s
name.

There was plenty to do. As an ambitious historian of nineteenth-century
England, she was now devoting most of her time to the eighteenth
century, and had begun her book, ‘England on the Eve of the Industrial
Revolution.’ The making of a book after long stretches of teaching, with
the frequent trips to New York on stray half-days to use the University
library, the painful adjustments of the mind from the classroom to
writing and back again, was a tortuous undertaking. Nora thought late at
night as she worked of scholars all over the world who have brought
forth their ideas with an even greater fight against unfavorable odds.
The luxuries of ease and an untroubled mind—they were unknown to the
brotherhood of poor scholars. Brotherhood of poor scholars—the phrase
running through her weary head brought cool draughts of refreshment. All
those who had something within them—like poor Margaret—how they suffered
in the pangs of creation! The chips of Nora’s labor she gave to her
students. She also took them into her confidence about her speculations,
her perplexities, tried to have them see how difficult it was to be
honest, intelligent, fair, and at the same time how beautiful were these
virtues of learning. Some of them caught her meaning.


 III

Five years of toil. There is nothing to say about it. You read for five
years, think, write, destroy everything you have done the first, perhaps
the second year; and possibly at the end, if you have kept your head and
your health, you have written a book which is worth the reading. Just
that, Nora estimated, was her history between the ages of twenty-five
and thirty.

But the book was done. Nora had spent her thirtieth birthday typing the
last of it. Miss Steele went around with a challenging air pronouncing
it the best piece of scholarship done by an American woman, or a British
woman, she often added. No one dared to contradict her. But Miss Steele
at last was beginning to show signs of age. Nora discerned a tendency in
her to emotionalize those whom she had once regarded with a chill
justice, and the praise of her old teacher had its sting of pain.
Professor Nicholson, who was urging the necessity of sending so
promising a young scholar to England to get a really big piece of work
started, regarded this first book as merely a respectable beginning. It
was all his management, of course, that the fellowship from
Knickerbocker University came to Nora. She was amused that Petersfield
should consider her a remarkable person, and that her students should
offer their congratulations with humble awe.


 IV

After a tiresome afternoon spent conferring with her publishers, Nora
went to Helen Spring’s apartment for a quiet week-end. She and Helen
were lounging in front of the fire after dinner, with no other light in
the room. A knock at the door.

‘Bother,’ said Helen.

She opened the door, standing there a minute in silence, staring at her
visitor. ‘Why, it’s Zoe Ryder!’ she exclaimed. ‘I didn’t recognize you
at first.’

Nora turned on the lights. A pale, very thin woman dropped down,
breathless, on Helen’s couch. ‘It must be five or six years since I’ve
seen you,’ Nora remarked, trying to conceal her shock at the change
which had come over Zoe. A kind of decay had settled over the former
Southern gentility, as if poor Zoe had been dragged through a miry way
in her troubles.

‘You were expecting me?’ Zoe gasped. ‘I followed the marks. I was so
relieved when I saw it was your house, Helen.’

‘The marks?’

Zoe pulled off a little black hat and patted her short brown hair,
heavily streaked with grey. ‘At first I was scared when I saw them on my
doorstep. A big letter _F_ in chalk. It’s that gang following me again,
I thought.’ Zoe’s large blue eyes regarded first Helen seated beside her
on the couch, then Nora before the fire. Nora observed their restless
glitter. ‘Another of their tricks. But something just made me follow
them. You know how it is. Something inside pushing you to do things. The
arrows at the corner—it was easy—and then following the lines—they
brought me right here.’

‘But we don’t know anything about the marks,’ Helen protested.

Zoe paid no attention. She sat, her hands clasping her knees, looking
abstractedly at something in front of her. ‘They’ve done enough. Fed on
me—ruined a good business.’ The gentle voice became shrill, then
subsided, and returned to the old girlish animation Nora remembered.
‘You never came to my tea-room, Helen. It was so cute. Of course I
didn’t have any money. I took down the doors and made them into tables,
stuck candles in the holes where the knobs had been. I called it the
“Devil’s Kitchen”——’

Nora and Helen looked at each other.

‘It was all written up in the newspapers. In two of them. I cooked
everything myself—fried the chicken—made the chocolate cake—the first
night went off wonderfully. I even had drinks—to advertise the tea-room,
you know.’

‘And you’ve made a success of it?’ Helen inquired.

‘The first week I had crowds every night.’ Zoe’s aged face had in
flashes the fire of youth. ‘The real people—writers, the theatre crowd,
artists. And that gang—they all had titles—foreigners. The Baron was
Russian. Pole maybe. I can’t remember. He brought the others. They kept
saying they’d pay and then ate off me every night. Imposing on a woman
that way! And noisy when they were drunk—it scared off the others. I
don’t blame them.’

Zoe’s narrative—broken off with this strange, inconsequential anger
against some one—the gang it was, Nora remembered. It sounded like
madness. And yet Zoe, perched lightly on the edge of Helen’s couch, was
so small and harmless, so much, Nora had always felt, like a fluttering
ground bird, with a silly ambition sometimes for the sky. Helen sat
regarding Zoe with her kind, amused eyes.

‘Why did you feed those men?’ Nora demanded indignantly.

‘They just ordered things. Nobility—all of them. Just helped themselves
to the drinks until they were all gone. And the trouble to get hootch
now——’

Zoe, with her soft voice and helpless air, in league with bootleggers
and a debauched alien crowd—the word ‘hootch’ crashing into her
old-fashioned Virginia speech—was this really Zoe sitting there?

‘My dear,’ drawled Zoe, ‘I never knew there were such people in the
world. After I’d trusted them——’ Her innocent round blue eyes with the
deep circles looked up at Nora. ‘I know now. It was a gang that feeds
off people—off women. They won’t work. Just drinking——’

Zoe wandered in her narrative. But in the end of a long description of
the troubled fortunes of the tea-room, she came at last to its sudden
end, the doors closed, no more possible credit.

‘Even then the Baron came,’ she went on. ‘Walked right in. “I’m hungry,”
he said. “You’ve eaten up all I’ve got,” I told him. “There’s nothing
left.” “I’m going to make myself some tea.” And, my dear, he walked
right out into the kitchen and got tea for himself.’

‘You ought to’ve called the police,’ Helen said.

But Zoe for some reason had no confidence in the protecting arm of the
law. She shrank away from Helen’s suggestion, as if wincing before a
blow. With a few sticks of furniture rescued from the tea-room she had
sought refuge in the attic of a feather factory as a hiding-place from
the gang, of which she lived in daily dread.

‘I didn’t even have a bed,’ Zoe continued plaintively. ‘I told my
friends——’ In all of Zoe’s troubles, when she tried to borrow money, she
spoke largely of those mysterious devoted beings always ready to come to
her aid. ‘They began sending me cots. One after another. I lost count of
them. They were just lined up——’ She laughed as she stretched out her
hands to indicate an infinity of beds. This light, untroubled laughter
as she described that terrible refuge—what—what had come to Zoe to
change her like this? Nora’s imagination leaped to a succession of
vivid, unmentionable horrors.

Zoe went on. Even in this rude shelter above the feather factory,
consisting sometimes of one room, sometimes of four in Zoe’s uncertain
recital, the Baron hunted her down. He declared he would freeze on the
streets if she didn’t take him.

‘I had such a nice fireplace,’ she boasted. ‘And I picked up wood
everywhere. He lay right down on the floor in front of my fire. He had a
terrible cough. Honestly, girls, he was very ill. I couldn’t——’

She had made up a bed for the Baron out of her superfluous store of
cots, covered him with some old clothes, nursed his cold. But as soon as
he was well, he went out and brought back the gang to occupy the rest of
the cots stacked up in the attic.

Helen accepted even this last revelation quietly, with all that one
could imagine from Zoe’s dark hints, the loft of the feather factory
with its disreputable nightly crew presided over by a worn, emaciated
Zoe. Did Zoe know what she was saying? Couldn’t Helen stop this mad
tale? Surely all this flimsy, impossible horror was the work of a sick
mind. But Helen’s calm face seemed to inspire Zoe to go on.

‘Carrying on—stamping up my stairway—there wasn’t even a lock to my
door. I’d lie there scared almost to death——’ Zoe’s skinny little
shoulders shook. ‘It was terrible—just terrible.’ Her voice had the
chirp of an insect trying to announce a tragedy.

Zoe came home one afternoon and found a crowd gathering around the back
door leading to her attic. Two policemen stood over something on the
stairs. Zoe stepped up beside them. She displayed a dainty pride as she
described her important part in the drama.

‘There was the Baron. He’d got some feathers out of one of the factory
bags. He was all rolled up in feathers. Just his head sticking out. Like
an enormous bird.’ Zoe began to laugh at the memory. Helen and Nora, not
knowing what else to do, joined her.

‘Such language. And right before a lady.’ Zoe had a slight lisp in
moments of stress. ‘He was so drunk he—they wanted to carry him off. But
I couldn’t have that. I told them he was a relative. I’d be responsible
for him.’ Again Nora observed Zoe’s unaccountable satisfaction, as if
she had performed some glorious deed.

But after Zoe had got the Baron up to the attic, she had herself taken
flight. Since then she had lived in a room not far from Helen, in daily
dread that the gang would discover her.

‘I’ve found out about them now,’ she said. ‘They collect around the
Square. They pick out their victims and then—I don’t dare go out except
after dark to get something to eat. I never go to the door. My friends
ring three times. Remember, three times. If they found where I was——’

Zoe rambled on a little more, going back over parts of her story,
exclaiming at intervals, ‘My dear, I never knew there were such people
in the world.’

Suddenly she seemed to remember a pressing engagement, jumped up, and
left, saying once more, ‘Ring three times. I’m always home.’

She was gone, and Helen and Nora were looking at each other.

‘It’s all like a hideous nightmare that you can’t shake off,’ Nora said,
her voice husky.

‘Something has happened to her. Drugs maybe,’ Helen speculated. ‘But
that old obsession of hers——’

How much of this tale of terror was true, how much built up with the aid
of Zoe’s sick mind, it was impossible to tell. They compared their
impressions, went over the details once more, but there seemed no
immediate solution.

Finally they went downstairs. Stooping they made out the letter _F_ in
chalk on the outer steps. At the nearest corner, they found a chalk
diagram.

The arrows leading from the letter _F_ pointed toward their house. They
followed the arrows for a few blocks, found them radiating in all
directions, in a perplexing maze.

‘Some new Village game,’ Nora ventured. ‘A party, perhaps—a treasure
hunt. These novelty-seekers would hit on something like this.’

Whatever it was, however, it had brought Zoe to Helen’s.

‘And I didn’t even dare offer to help her,’ Helen remembered, when they
had returned to Helen’s sitting-room. ‘She’s borrowed money from every
one she knew. It’s two years since any one I know has seen her.’

They made inquiries among Zoe’s old circle, but no one had heard of her.
At the address she gave even three rings at the door failed to bring
Zoe.

‘Well,’ Helen remarked, when Nora was starting back to Petersfield
Sunday night, ‘you’ve seen all my menagerie the years you’ve been around
New York. I guess Zoe’s the last of them, Nora. Your work’s about
finished. I’ve done my best for you—Amelia—Vicky—Zoe—I don’t believe I
can give you any more.’

‘You’ve done very well by me, Helen.’

‘Kept you from too dull a diet—the university and the normal academic
mind——Well, you’re the only one of us that’s come to anything. And yet
we’ve sneered at your sanity behind your back, Nora. I can tell you
now.’


 V

Nora stood in a long procession of men and women receiving degrees from
Knickerbocker University. She marched up to the high platform, heard a
few words mumbled over her, and was soon back in her seat. The end, so
long delayed by teaching, by her own stumbling in by-paths, by her
intellectual insecurity, was reached at last. In the crowded
amphitheatre, she sensed what a big American university, so formless and
destitute of charm or emotion, had given her. In these striving years,
shut off from much of what men experienced in the rubbing together of
the best minds, she was carrying away intellectual gains which
Petersfield could never have offered her.

After all, it was the universities like Knickerbocker which represented
the best American life could deliver, the safest places to breed the
ideas which would become the popular possession of the next age. The
universities were far from perfect—she was ready to admit, but no
institution of learning can be far above the society which supports it.
Idealists had laid heavy charges against Knickerbocker University: the
creaking machinery, the officers who placed immediate well-being before
the ultimate good, the dull professors, the dead scholarship. Many of
these evils existed in all the universities. But there was the other
side. Where did all the scornful critics who saw no virtue in American
learning get their ideals—their first spark? At a university. Some
professor. Some other student. Perhaps both. Then they grew up, went
away, and belabored the place which prepared them to walk without
leading strings.

Crossing the square in front of the library, escaping from the multitude
of Commencement Day, Nora experienced a prolonging of her elated mood.
She reviewed swiftly the books she knew written by the men of
Knickerbocker. In most of them one found honesty, patient toil, the
absence of charlatanism. And in the best there was brilliance and a safe
start for a new generation of scholars. The great need was more
time—more time to think. ‘Ripeness is all,’ wise Shakespeare had said.
More time, more leisure, peace, no worry about money—but all this is the
heaven of which the tired scholar dreams.

Ruth Ewing was waiting for Nora on the corner. ‘I’m so sorry. I couldn’t
get in to see your little show for the crowd.’ She kissed Nora. ‘What
shall we do now?’

‘I’ve meant to do it for months. I’ve never had the time. I’m going to
have my hair cut off,’ Nora said.

‘My dear!’ protested her friend.

‘Come along.’ Nora put a firm hand on Ruth’s arm. ‘My mind’s all made
up.’

‘Miss Steele will be furious,’ Ruth persisted, but Nora only laughed and
dragged her forward.

Nora gazed at a shorn head in the barber’s mirror. ‘Very nice, Miss,’ a
dark little man was saying.

Ruth Ewing was examining her not entirely with disapprobation. ‘I wish
Margaret could see you, Nora.’

Nora continued to study the reflection in the mirror. The line of her
head in back, the sweep of hair over her forehead, broad and
clean-cut—yes, it all went with her slender, boyish figure.

‘They won’t take you seriously at first over there in England,’ laughed
Ruth. ‘How you’ll fool them, my dear.’

Years had dropped from Nora, and most of her dignity. At this climax in
her academic honors, she had never seemed more lightly touched by time.
This was the way to start marching over the stretch between thirty and
forty. Thirty-one years old. A woman ready to face the world for the
first time. The best of life beginning; of that she felt sure.
Intelligence ripens slowly, gives its best only after long and weary
waiting. But she was ready, at last, to see what she could do.

‘Ruth,’ Nora called out, ‘let’s take the first train to Petersfield and
show Miss Steele. Of course she’ll be furious. I want to hear her rage.’
She slid out of the barber’s chair, her eyes seeking once more the trim,
smiling girl in the mirror.

 PART IV.
 Mount Latmos


 CHAPTER I


 I

A park, a lake, a red pillar box, a postman on a bicycle wearing a funny
flat helmet, a tall iron fence with an opening through which the taxi
passed. Nora had arrived at Saint Ethelfreda College in London. It was
exactly what an English college should be: red brick covered with ivy, a
stretch of that vivid green grass one always suspected on postcards, and
flowers, actually the snapdragons which since Newman’s ‘Apologia’ one
expected to find in English college gardens. The maid at the door, with
her crisp cap and apron and stiffly starched movements, was a perfect
specimen of the English servant, like those in the novel Nora had read
on the boat coming over.

‘Miss Talbot is expecting you, Miss. Won’t you step this way, Miss?’

Nora was ushered into a large room, very chilly and damp even the first
week of October, with all of the windows opening out into the pleasant
garden. A small, brown-haired woman, the resident tutor, Miss Talbot,
rose to shake hands with Miss Deming, the pupil of that unusual woman,
Professor Steele, who had visited Saint Ethelfreda.

Miss Talbot was all smiles, too many of them. Nora wished that the
resident tutor’s habit of cocking her head on one side like a robin and
opening her blue eyes very wide did not tempt her to laughter. On the
way to her room Nora was introduced to several severe-looking women in
college gowns who must be dons. They didn’t seem to recognize the
American’s donnishness. Evidently short hair didn’t impress the English
as a sign of a highly developed intellect.

The room set aside for the new American student was delightful, with
windows overlooking the garden and the park beyond, where through the
trees Nora could see sheep grazing. A fireplace and a scuttle of coals.
The delights of an open fire would be hers. A desk near the window, a
big armchair, a couch with an India print cover—everything most
comfortable surely. A knock at the door, and the maid she had seen
downstairs entered.

‘I trust everything’s all right, Miss. You want a bit of fire? London is
damp to the Americans, we find. We’ve had American young ladies here
before, Miss. I always take care of them.’

The maid, whose name was Clara, made Nora feel apologetic for her
careless, undistinguished American speech.

‘Now,’ Clara went on, when the coals began to burn, ‘I’m going to bring
up your tea. Miss Talbot said I might. I said you’d be tired after the
weary journey up to London.’

At the sound of the dinner bell the Saint Ethelfreda students gathered
in the chilly common room. Miss Talbot hurriedly introduced Nora to the
gaunt young woman beside her.

‘Miss South from New Zealand. Like two colonials, really.’

Nora looked at Miss South. Then her glance travelled to a girl standing
apart from the others, a girl with heavy black hair held by a large
jewelled pin, and deeply circled eyes, her wrinkled dress in startling
contrast to the English, all correctly attired for a formal eight
o’clock dinner.

‘Mademoiselle Lafleur, your next-door neighbor,’ murmured Miss Talbot.

The French girl’s tragic eyes lighted up for a moment. Miss Talbot put
out a stiff arm through which she slipped her hand, Nora was seized by
the New-Zealander, and a long procession of dons and students, two by
two, marched into the hall.

The dining-hall soared into heights of new oak imitating some
Elizabethan pattern, Nora suspected, and the girls seated at the rows of
tables appeared insignificant in the vastness of space over them.

Nora’s neighbors ate steadily and swiftly, talking very little. All of
them had red cheeks. Like Peggotty’s in ‘David Copperfield,’ as
brilliant as a red velvet pincushion. Nora had heard about English
complexions. It was the dampness, which at the moment hung like a grey
cloud around the chandeliers suspended from the lofty ceiling, now
obscured. These girls were so bony, too. Their arms, their necks, their
cheeks. Not one morsel of superfluous flesh.

From across the table she caught the word hockey—the hockey matches.
Hockey, not yet introduced at Petersfield, had already invaded some of
the American colleges, Nora told them.

‘It’s a topping game,’ ventured a quiet little girl with a shy smile.
‘Topping.’

Miss Talbot rose, Mademoiselle Lafleur took her arm, and the procession
marched back to the common room. The solemnity of the ceremony was
really delicious. The English girls began passing coffee. It was vile.
One sip almost choked Nora. She saw the French girl slide her cup under
the sofa. The dons, raw-boned and talking hockey, took two cups.

Nora, back in her room, closed the windows to shut out the clammy chill
of English air, and shook some coal over the fire. The common room had
kept the shivers chasing up and down her backbone.

A knock on her door. A girl who looked like Fuzzy Leland at Petersfield,
but much taller and thinner, entered.

‘Won’t you come to cocoa at ten o’clock?’ she said. ‘In my room at the
end of the corridor.’

Petersfield all over again. Even to night feeds. But something hot to
drink would be grateful before getting into bed. The sheets were always
damp. Some one on the boat had warned her never to sleep in an English
bed without slipping a watch between the sheets to see if the glass got
damp. But if it did, what next? Nora decided it was more comfortable not
to know too much about the sheets.

In the tall girl’s room—Sheldon her name was, and English girls called
one another by last names just like boys—five girls sat on the floor in
front of a bright fire. They were very friendly to the strange American,
but they didn’t talk much. Long—long pauses. The cocoa made a comforting
warm trickle down Nora’s clammy interior.

In bed that night Nora prayed that she wouldn’t have to live her
freshman year over again at thirty-one. It would be worse than
reincarnation. Those cocoa parties! Professor Steele was responsible for
her coming to Saint Ethelfreda. She had selected Miss Talbot as a
reliable caretaker for her dear pupil. Nora had counted on cheap
lodgings near the British Museum and the School of Economics before Miss
Steele got started on the lurid horrors of an unheated room, or at best
one with a reeking gas-fire, presided over by a slatternly landlady. She
also preached to Nora the duty of studying English university women at
first hand. Her pupil, with her deceptive youth, would be accepted as
one of the girls and could find out if they, in spite of their
seriousness, were any cleverer than the frivolous American girl who had
brains, even while she concealed them.

So here Nora was. Her room was very still. She could hear the ducks on
the lake and the striking of a church bell. Saint Ethelfreda College,
after a cocoa party, had betaken itself to slumber or to study, instead
of conversing loudly in the corridors, romping and squealing in the
Petersfield fashion. An American had to admit that the English respected
quiet.

At breakfast Annette Lafleur slipped into the vacant chair beside Nora.
All around her sat the English girls fresh from a morning tub, their
hair brushed back into a tight bun in a straight line with their shiny
noses. The French girl wore the tumbled dress of the night before. The
jewelled pin, which still upheld her sable coils, had an uncertain,
trembling tilt. Beside the snappy alertness of her neighbors, she looked
as if she had passed a troubled, sleepless night. She stirred a cup of
tea languidly, and looked around her. The English were quietly consuming
frightful quantities of porridge.

Opposite Nora sat the same quiet little hockey enthusiast to whom Nora
had spoken at dinner. She came from Dorsetshire, and her name by some
strange twist was Dorset, Lucy Dorset.

‘Going to play in the match Saturday, Dorset?’ called out the
cocoa-party hostess of the night before.

‘Hockey—always that hockey,’ muttered the French girl. She turned to
Nora. ‘I want to talk to you about America. Some day soon? This
afternoon maybe? I know many Americans.’

Nora and the New-Zealander walked through the park after breakfast, the
feeble sun lighting up the brown of the London trees and the red buses
lumbering past them. Miss South leaped on to the proper bus, Nora
clumsily catching on behind her.

At the British Museum they passed the guardian policemen at the outer
gate, the sight-seers already assembling, and the guards within. The
signatures of the Saint Ethelfreda officers gave Nora immediate access
to the reading-room sanctities.

‘That much done,’ sighed Miss South, leading the way to University
College and then to the School of Economics to arrange about lectures.

On her return, Nora knocked on the door next to hers. Mademoiselle
Lafleur, wrapped in a big white shawl, was leaning over a cold grate
rubbing her hands together.

‘Haven’t you lit your fire yet?’ Nora asked.

The French girl shook her head. In the centre of the mantel shelf above
her was a little shrine covered with gaily colored silk for a picture of
Napoleon as a young man, draped with the tricolor. Beside him nodded two
white carnations in a tipsy little vase. Mademoiselle Lafleur observed
the direction of Nora’s gaze.

‘The Savior of France,’ she said. ‘I pray for the coming of his
successor to save my poor country. A strong man——’

‘I think a descendant of the Napoleon family lives in America.’ Nora was
conscious of the inappropriateness of her matter-of-fact tone. ‘At least
I have a vague memory——’

‘If it could be a ruler with Napoleon’s blood,’ sighed Annette Lafleur.

When Nora suggested tea in her room in front of a blazing fire, the
great melancholy eyes lit up with a faint ray of response. The sight of
Clara bringing in a tray with an abundance of bread and butter, damson
preserves, plum cake, and tea for two hungry people, changed her into an
eager and normal girl.

‘Beautiful—beautiful.’

‘I’m going to start calling you Annette immediately,’ announced Nora,
pouring out fragrant hot tea from a squatty brown earthenware teapot.
‘And please call me Nora.’

‘With pleasure—Nora.’ Annette laughed over her clumsy fumbling with the
name. ‘To escape “Mademoiselle.” Mademoiselle—Mademoiselle with these
English from morning until night. Like a servant. It is horrible.
Miss—Miss—how would they suffer it?’ Annette began to drink her tea, her
chair up close to the comforting flames.

Annette began to question her new friend eagerly about the United
States. She had met American boys during the war. When the big trucks
stopped in front of her country home and the soldiers tried to buy
gasoline, she, the only member of her family speaking English, had to
talk to them. She had begun filling a note-book with her observations of
American men. To draw them out, she made a list of questions.

‘“Do you believe in Platonic love?” That was one of my questions,’
Annette said, looking at Nora with a triumphant and superior air. ‘And
always the young American man said yes, he believed in Platonic love.’

‘Of course,’ Nora agreed.

‘Then I decided the American young man is remarkable. Now the
Frenchman—of course I love my countrymen best—but no Frenchman believes
in Platonic love. And the Englishman—but they are too cold—too frozen to
believe in anything about love. The Englishman—I hate that kind of man.’

Nora had hoped there wouldn’t be another cocoa party that night. But
there was. This time Annette was invited. Cocoa parties were absolutely
new in her experience. She sat perched on a stool before her hostess’s
fire staring around her, a quizzical wonder in her eloquent eyes.

In Nora’s room afterward she went off into spasms of amusement. ‘I shall
write home,’ she snorted. ‘Cocoa—cocoa party.’ In her mocking tones the
words sounded very ridiculous. ‘Oh, my God!’ Annette threw herself face
downward on Nora’s couch.

The French girl interspersed all her remarks with free translations of
her _mon Dieu_. They flavored her speech like red pepper. Of course the
English girls were shocked by her ‘My God,’ which snapped out like a
firecracker.

But even more startling was the American student’s announcement that she
was taking lectures at the School of Economics. None of them went down
there. Somehow it seemed outside the pale of respectability, an
un-English place, they had been told. It also brought back too vivid
memories of that recent shameful but mercifully brief period when a
Labor Government had ruled England, and a coal-miner had donned satin
breeches to bow before the King. There seemed to be no radicals at Saint
Ethelfreda.


 II

Down in the visitors’ room, located with the assistance of Clara, Nora
found Ellen Powys. Ellen, very fresh and lovely, with the pink bloom of
her new home, looked very English in her broad-brimmed hat, tweed
costume, homespun scarf, and chamois gloves.

‘I’ve come to carry you home with me for tea,’ she said, enfolding Nora
in a warm embrace. She looked around the bare little room with its
hissing gas-fire. ‘Isn’t this the quaintest place? Like an inquisitorial
chamber. And I walked through miles and miles of halls to find it.’

Ellen was the mother of four substantial British subjects, waiting to be
displayed to Mummy’s American friend in a big cheerful nursery, presided
over by a Scotch nurse; two boys and two girls, the youngest a babe of
three months. They were beautiful and blond like their parents, with
firm hard legs like pillars. Even the small baby promised a stalwart
robustness.

‘You see,’ explained Ellen, as they drove away in her two-seater, ‘I’m
not clever like Michael. So I took to the baby business. All we can hope
is that the children will inherit their father’s brains.’

On Sunday Nora went a second time to the Powys’s Kensington flat, where
Michael’s friends working with him on the paper and a pleasant mixture
of other people dropped in for supper.

Michael had the same beautiful radiance, but he looked more substantial,
and his enthusiasms were tempered by a sobriety and humor which he had
not yet developed in his Petersfield days.

‘I expected you’d get over,’ Michael said to Nora. ‘I hope we can be
some help to you, Ellen and I. We’ll do what we can.’

The guests began to arrive rapidly. They were gayer than Nora had
anticipated, with a careless and informal kindness to the American
stranger unlike the studied benevolence of Saint Ethelfreda. In their
talk, glancing over various employments and avocations, there was none
of that veneration for the arts which characterizes the outsider. Most
of them, it appeared, had a recognized place in a chosen craft.

‘Jolly piece of acting you put on last week, Jerry.’ A stout man
expanding beyond the confines of his dinner jacket nodded toward an
Irish boy stretched out on the floor before the fire.

‘And a jolly bit of writing you spun out for me in “The Londoner,”’ the
actor rejoined. ‘It was kind of you, Norris.’

‘The honest overflow of a disciplined heart,’ laughed the other.

Supper was handed out informally to the guests, who stood around in
groups balancing plates or sought quiet corners. Nora found herself in a
window-seat between an elderly woman whose studio overlooked Hammersmith
Bridge and a young journalist who was trying between assignments to
write a great novel on the war. Others joined them, talked to Nora,
drifted away. Ellen brought up a few people to be introduced to Nora,
but she left her guests largely to themselves.

It was the most delightful evening Nora had yet spent in England. She
leaped on a bus at a near-by corner, with a new affection for London and
for old friends. At the edge of the park near the college gates, she
passed two policemen.

‘Good evening, Miss,’ they said with a warming politeness.

Yes, London was a charming place. She sped along, the church bell
striking, striking. It must be midnight. She reached the college gates.
They were closed. She tried to pull them open. She strained against
them. No response. They were locked. The porter’s lodge on the other
side was in darkness. She looked around. No one was in sight. She
examined the gate. Yes, she could climb over it. She lifted her skirt,
placed one foot on the first cross-piece, up and up, now over, and
quickly climbed down the other side. Walking up the avenue of beech
trees, casting long shadows over the driveway, she remembered that this
was Sunday night, Sunday in England, church-going, propriety, a quiet
evening at home. The college entrance was locked. Of course it would be.
But a light in one of the first-floor windows saved her. She crept over
to it, called softly, and Willis, a very serious second-year girl,
appeared at the window.

‘I’m locked out,’ Nora said.

‘Of course. It’s past midnight,’ announced Willis.

‘May I climb into your window?’ The window-sill was close to the ground.
The only difficulty was in leaping over the flower borders which
stretched between.

‘I oughtn’t to let you,’ scolded Willis. ‘If you trample the flowers——’

But Willis was so absorbed in higher mathematics that she had no desire
to inquire into the American girl’s irregularity. She said good-night
chillily and went on with her work.

The next morning a note in Nora’s post-office box summoned her to Miss
Talbot’s room. She knocked on what she thought was the proper door.

‘Come in,’ called a faint voice.

Miss Talbot was seated at the opposite end of her very long room in
front of an official table.

‘Will you please go around to the other door,’ suggested Miss Talbot,
‘and not tramp through my sitting-room?’ Nora looked around her, backed
out of the room, and found a door marked ‘Office.’ Evidently, Miss
Talbot’s room possessed a public and a private entrance.

Nora stood in front of the official table, Miss Talbot not asking her to
be seated.

‘I am informed that you were not in the college at closing time last
night, Miss Deming. Your entrance later needs some explanation.’

‘I—I didn’t know—that the gates were locked. When I returned——’ Nora
felt the black guilt of a small child unable to make the proper
explanation to his elders.

‘You did not consult me about any evening engagement, Miss Deming.’

‘You mean that I—if I go out evenings——’

‘Every resident in this college makes no engagement without consulting
me,’ Miss Talbot declared.

‘I—I didn’t know,’ Nora faltered.

‘Oh.’ Into Miss Talbot’s exclamation one could read almost anything.

Outside the inquisitorial chamber, Nora realized that Miss Talbot was
ignorant of her academic standing in her own country, her age, her years
of independence. She must go back and explain. But something held her
outside Miss Talbot’s door. Why should she live in this English college
without the restrictions of the other students, free of the shackles
which held them? The girls had accepted her as one of them. She would
not spoil the naturalness of her position.

Her heart still beat with enraged thumps. Her cheeks burned. She
remembered now the rancor the young suffer against restraint. So this
was what the English girls had to endure.

Annette was delighted at Nora’s experience in bumping her head against
the unyielding stone wall of a British Sabbath. She, too, had suffered.
But she had her peculiar kind of revenge. Whenever Miss Talbot appeared
within her orbit, she muttered under her breath two phrases which she
had found in Gaston Leroux: ‘Smirking with the careful charm of a woman
a little along in years who relies still on infantine graces,’ and ‘fat
smile of importance,’ which was even better.

The English girls called Miss Talbot ‘Tabby,’ and they resented her
tyranny as much as did the two foreigners. None of them, however, dared
to rebel. Poor Jonesie, an honors student in chemistry, always studying
long after midnight, had asked for permission to go to a Bach concert.

‘You will never distinguish yourself by running out at night like a
servant girl,’ pronounced Tabby icily.

Jones, on a scholarship and not far enough removed from the servant
class to escape the sting in Tabby’s words, had submitted to the
decision.

‘She’s a tyrant,’ one of Jonesie’s champions said. ‘It’s power she
loves. Poor Jonesie who gets dull working so hard——’

‘If one felt Tabby did it for our good. The way she grins when she says
something nasty——’

But Fairfield, a stubby girl from Cheshire, always defended Tabby.
‘She’s clever—awfully clever. You all know that.’ No one disputed
Fairfield. All of them had too many sore memories of Tabby’s
relentlessly powerful tongue. ‘You’ve got to let Tabby have a little
temperament,’ she concluded.

Nora soon discovered that Fairfield was right. Tabby was so much more
than a tyrant. At the International Dinner given at the Trafalgar Club,
she overheard Miss Talbot talking to a distinguished Frenchman partly in
French and partly in English in a brilliant fashion which she realized
she at Tabby’s age could never equal. In the candlelight, Tabby, with
her firm white neck and shoulders, the brilliant green of her eyes, the
delicately shifting color of her face intensified by the rose of her
gown, was very handsome, one of the most notable women there. Only as
the shades of Saint Ethelfreda drew around her did she become sharp and
hard, ruling with a force which none dared question. She had two lives
which never crossed each other.


 III

Lucy wanted to be Nora’s friend. She gave a light little knock on Nora’s
door, lingered over her fire, talked to her about Hardy, the Dorsetshire
poet, whose verse stirred her, another Dorsetshire person, as nothing
else had. Gradually she told more about herself. She came from a family
whose ambition for her was success at the county balls. But she wasn’t a
success. She couldn’t be. At dances her hair got wet and stringy. She
knew she was awkward. And dancing made her tired—so very tired. She came
to college to escape the county balls. Her father had been angry, her
mother sad, at their daughter’s failure. But she loved Saint Ethelfreda.
And she loved hockey. Hardy and hockey—strange little Lucy.

One night she looked up at Nora with a bird-like glance from her
footstool in front of the fire. ‘Do you like Mademoiselle?’ she asked.
‘I’ve seen you talking to her.’

‘I find her very interesting—very different,’ Nora told her.

‘I’m afraid I don’t care for the French. We always have a French girl
here at college. They seem very strange. Not like the English. I don’t
like people who are different. That’s English, I suppose. We’re narrow,
you think?’

‘But I’m different.’

‘In such a nice way,’ interrupted Lucy. ‘You seem so friendly—easy to
talk to. Really,’ she added, the courage rising in the depths of scared,
blue-green eyes.

There seemed no poetry in the English girls—except Lucy—no imagination.
When Annette insisted upon reading aloud her latest discovery in English
verse at the cocoa parties, reading it as it should be read—tenderly,
with moments of fire, of passion, their red cheeks flushed carmine and
they looked down toward their boots. They appeared proud of their lack
of emotion, at least their concealment of it, of their regular and
studious life at college. They expected to secure posts in a school,
good ones if they got ‘firsts’ in the examinations, those eventful
examinations, which, at the end of three years, decided their
professional standing in life. To be successful students, they
eliminated all vanity. Neatness, an almost oppressive soapy cleanliness,
they insisted upon, but scraped-back hair and dull woollen dresses under
academic gowns were the marks of the serious university woman. They, the
daughters of doctors, barristers, schoolmasters, and clergymen in
provincial towns, had no curiosity about London. They laughed at the
time Nora wasted with sight-seeing. They had been to the Tower and the
Zoo. A few of the more spirited had visited Madame Tussaud’s. When
necessity took them to East London, they avoided buses which carried one
relentlessly through miserable thoroughfares. They used the Underground,
bolted out as quickly as they could, and didn’t look around them. They
felt a sense of good form in not staring at the misery of others. Out of
kindness several of the older girls went with Nora to the City churches
like Saint Bartholomew’s, but they disapproved of her choosing a
different one each Sunday. They hinted that it seemed too much like
church sight-seeing. But Nora told them with a laugh that she was not
above such ecclesiastical junketings.

All the political sense the female novelists gave to Englishwomen Nora
never discovered at Saint Ethelfreda. Not one of them had attended a
session of Parliament. There wasn’t time. They couldn’t understand
politics. Conservative, Liberal—they left all that to their fathers. A
government election the year before had started the college, so Maggs
said, into staging a mock election, in which, even in play, most of
Saint Ethelfreda had kept away from Labor. The few votes given all came
from day students, London girls, who sported with a mild radicalism. But
never the residents from the provinces.

‘Too stodgy and respectable,’ declared Maggs, a new acquaintance of
Nora’s, who was Cockney to the marrow.

There were no Socialist Leagues, no arguments about the duty of bearing
fatherless children, or of becoming sisters to the unwashed Bolshevists.

When Nora dropped in to visit the history seminars, she kept comparing
the English and the American way. Petersfield played more lightly with
the mind. It wasted time with college activities, but there was an
intensity, an enlivening force in its best moments which she felt Saint
Ethelfreda lacked. Here was the daily faithful application, the mature
self-direction which flitting American girls seldom arrived at, the
solid, honest accomplishment. Balance, restraint—these were the
intellectual virtues of Saint Ethelfreda. But the ignorance of
contemporary events, the indifference held the students in a sluggish
theoretical backwater. They could not translate what they read into
terms of the present. Yet was Petersfield any better? She was probably
nursing romantic memories of her own college. All college women,
possibly, were equally out of the world.

The brilliant students at University College and the School of Economics
were generally men. Not all. The short-haired women with masculine dress
and the prim Quaker ladies with strong opinions she had met at Professor
MacPherson’s Sunday afternoon teas argued boldly about political
philosophy with the Hindoos, the eager Jews, the Boer, the Canadians, a
Siamese, the New-Zealanders, the Egyptian, the shabby Irish and English.
Although they were solidly Labor, they represented delicate shades of
distinction hard for an outsider like Nora to catch. Some of them were
Fabians, much interested in the Fabian Kindergarten, instituted for the
young and faltering in radical thought. Others were Guild Socialists
ready to watch the spectacular downfall of the present capitalistic
society almost any moment. When Nora carried back these prophecies to
her own college, the chemistry don had laughed like a horse, throwing
back her head, and screaming, ‘What nonsense!’ Others among Professor
MacPherson’s guests, merging Fabianism and Guild Socialism, represented
the most subtle and tantalizing of thinkers. But none of them had the
middle-class taboos of Saint Ethelfreda, with the belief in the
necessity of respectability. How they despised Saint Ethelfreda! Those
women!

Michael Powys’s crowd, which Nora met almost every Sunday, were less
statistical, less grubby. What brought all of them to Ellen’s little
drawing-room was their common belief that a man could be a painter or a
journalist and at the same time hope that England could be made a less
hopeless hole to live in. They wanted to reconstruct a society shattered
by war and still, after eight years, sadly in pieces, so that one could
