be spiritually comfortable while one fiddled in Queen’s Hall or hurried
off the last pages of proof. But all of this seriousness they concealed.
To Nora the well-set-up and carefully finished young men were the most
graceful product of British civilization. Their perfection of ease, the
best American men never succeeded in giving the effect of such
undesigned art. The women, young matrons like Ellen, with serene eyes
and delicate flower-like bloom, lacked the nervous intensity which wore
one out with American women.

But at ten o’clock the porter was beginning to lock the college gates,
and Nora had to leave these delightful people warming up to their best
conversation to return to another week of porridge, Tabby, and the dons,
and table talk of hockey.


 IV

London was now held fast in the grip of November fogs. The light October
mists were only happy memories. Day after day, when Nora looked out of
her window, even the nearest trees were hidden by greyish vapor, and the
thick air made her eyes burn and her throat dry. Going to the Museum,
she felt as if she were walking in her sleep as she passed from one
choking area to another. The water on the pavements, the slimy streets,
the sooty air made her spirits sink. She felt as if something were
pushing her down to the pavement, and she would never be able to get up
again.

In the reading-room the grey clouds floated up to the lofty dome,
dropping down upon the men and women huddled in their stalls, and giving
to the benevolent attendants in skull caps imprisoned in a sacred inner
circle the dark, forbidding obscurity of mischief-loving genii.

When she and Annette went to the theatre in Hammersmith, the fog, like
pea-soup the British described it, was so dense that the policemen after
the play escorted parties to the Underground. Along the edge of the park
they felt their way, touching the iron palings of the fence for
guidance. They heard voices near by, but could see no one.

When the fog lifted after five days and a convalescent sun glimmered
weakly over the streets, Nora went to the Wallace gallery to see ‘The
Laughing Cavalier.’ She found it an amusing pastime to move slowly away
from the compelling smile of the merry gentleman in the painting to see
how far it could follow her. Farther and farther away she went, the eyes
pursuing her with their tantalizing gaiety. Suddenly the laughter was
gone. She looked around her. The same pall had fallen over all the
neighboring canvases—Reynolds, Gainsborough, Rembrandt, Titian, and the
rest. The fog had returned and was rapidly filling the gallery. As she
passed through the room full of old French clocks, all ticking away with
different voices and rhythms in the dimness, they seemed to be closing
in upon her, ticking, ticking, in her ears, all around her head, at her
back, over her feet. She ran down the staircase to get away, and out
into the black chasms of Marylebone High Street.

The next day she was back in the B.M. She had been reading aimlessly for
almost two months and had got nowhere. Nineteenth-century England
stretched out greater and greater, vast unexplored spaces. One plunge
seemed as sensible as another. It really made no difference. Sometimes
she stayed away from the reading-room and from her lectures, but the
weeks were slipping by, and she had done nothing. If she sat in that
grey, airless tomb, an inspiration might suddenly come to her. She had a
cold, and her hacking cough made the dried-up old woman in the chair
next look up sharply from her copying. She reached in her bag, and
brought out a box of lozenges.

‘Here, take this,’ she said crossly. Nora obediently swallowed one, and
thanked her.

Another day Nora, writing hard on a new project for a book, toiled with
a scratching pen. Screech—screech—it was annoying. Once more her
neighbor regarded her with hostility. She handed Nora a thick blotter.

‘I can’t stand that pen of yours,’ she scolded. ‘You write on this.’

Four o’clock. Nora was accomplishing nothing. She would go back to
college. She reached her room exhausted, her head aching, her neck
stiff. As she sipped her tea in front of the fire, the pounding of heels
in the hallway making throbs of pain shoot through her head, she knew
that she hated everything at Saint Ethelfreda, everything except Annette
and Lucy and Maggs—and dear old Clara who made her fire and brought her
beautiful tea every day. Tabby and the other dons—yes, she hated them.
They had no feeling. They were so hard—hard. Was it being shut up in a
college nunnery which made them like this? Would she—yes, she was afraid
that some day she, too——‘Consign me to the watery depths if I get that
way,’ she confided to her diary. ‘Make me love the human race, O ye
great powers of the universe.’

‘I don’t see how you can live this way,’ protested Annette as she
watched her friend start off after breakfast every morning. ‘A system so
unbending—I should die, Nora.’

She clapped her hands together for joy when Nora, after dinner,
stretched out on her couch, with Annette and Lucy for an audience,
before the fire, cursed the B.M. and all the diabolical realms of
scholarship.

‘You Americans are delightful!’ cried Annette. ‘So—so complicated. Going
off to the B.M. with that bag after breakfast. That Nora, I
say—she cares only for books and for working. But now you are
delightful—delightful, Nora.’ She rushed over and kissed her friend on
both cheeks.

But Lucy was shaking her head. Lucy’s main regret was that she had
wasted most of her youth with the county balls. ‘You don’t know, Nora.
You’ve done so much. And I—I must work every minute now to make up for
all the time I lost. Books—books. I can never get enough. There’s
nothing in life equal to good books. They are everything.’

Annette snorted. ‘It is the soul—the heart. An empty heart——’ Annette’s
great eyes were lifted toward the ceiling. ‘A heart not loving——In this
college there is nothing for the heart—nothing.’

Lucy looked arch. ‘Is it the young men you miss, Annette?’

‘English young men? I do not care for English young men.’ Annette
marched out of the room.

‘You know the French,’ hinted Lucy when Annette had closed the door
majestically behind her. Lucy had read Balzac the preceding summer and
had even peeped into Maupassant’s ‘Une Vie.’ ‘They’re always thinking
about young men.’

Poor Lucy! Did she never have shy memories of the young men in Dorset
with whom she had danced year after year?

When Nora emerged from the reading-room at noon and found the sun
shining, a low pale sun scarcely rising above the grey Georgian houses
with yellow blinds in Bedford Square, she often decided not to go back
to her work, but to spend the afternoon walking the streets. She was
almost happy then. She loved the winding lanes of the City, the mazes of
Southwark, the forest of church spires she saw from London Bridge.

But too often at the end of these footsore adventures, there was the old
pressure of pain at her neck, the tight ache in her throat. She must be
homesick. It was being out of her own country. She wanted Petersfield,
Miss Steele, Ruth and Helen, dear Cordelia and the quiet Maronac house
near the lake. With her international conception, she had once scoffed
at an irrational love of one’s own country. But there was something—you
just couldn’t explain it away. She felt it even in the friendliness of
Michael’s and Ellen’s drawing-room. The most charming, appealing people,
and yet between them and her such fearful gulfs yawned. From the women
she felt less separated, but it was the men she wanted to know. The
Irish actor—there were several actors; the violinist who played for his
friends as he never played in Queen’s Hall; the painter in the Royal
College of Art hating his classes the way Margaret Abington had
hated hers; the intense man with the streaks of grey in his
chrysanthemum-yellow hair, who was on Michael’s paper—it was they who
followed her as she ran toward the college gates. But perhaps even more
she thought of the men who were nothing. What they did wasn’t what
mattered. It was their grace, their careless talk with her, their mere
existence she wanted for herself. But they were all English, and removed
from her by this silly and accidental fact. She always the stranger. She
vowed she would keep away from the supper parties, but the next week she
suffered all over again the blend of pleasure and pain.

No one—nothing was to blame. She was tired. She worked too steadily in
the B.M. The bad air, and then getting nowhere with her work. She must
stay away for a few days and rest. But in the morning she started
reading a new book. She was afraid of the silence of her room, broken
only by the hard heels of the English girls marching back and forth to
and from lectures. She admitted to herself sitting late at night over
her dying fire that when she had come to England she had counted on
life’s being different for her. New scenes. A fresh existence.
Something—changing all the past. The men at Ellen’s—each week the
feeling that she would meet some one who——But always the same thing. The
Englishmen standing in groups against the wall. ‘Clever American woman,’
they might say. ‘Attractive in an odd way. Almost beautiful, you know.’
These composed Britishers whom Annette hated and Nora found mysteriously
different. But never did they and she quite meet on a common
understanding. Perhaps if she had not been forced to hurry away so soon
after supper——

‘It’s ridiculous—you running back to that old college like a
schoolgirl,’ scolded Ellen. ‘Michael could write a letter to that
woman—his connection with the paper would impress her. That kind always
are impressed that way.’

‘If you like,’ Michael added, smiling at Nora.

But Nora remained firm in asking for no concessions, and each week she
had the same sensation of just missing what she most wanted.

Work—doing something. She must keep very busy.


 V

On the last Saturday before the Christmas vacation, Maggs, Nora,
Annette, and Lucy went to visit the Docks. From the Underground they
walked through Shadwell, the district where Michael Powys, as a
Cambridge undergraduate, had lived in his vacations. When they started,
the sun had shone upon their undertaking, but now the gloom of winter
intensified the dreariness of the rows and rows of wretched houses and
the dirt of the children playing in the narrow streets.

A guide at the entrance to the Docks conferred with Maggs. He handed
over the young women to the foreman of the ivory warehouse. Thousands of
elephants’ tusks from East and West Africa, from Asia, from South
Africa, lay piled upon the floor. The foreman pointed to one beautiful
polished tusk, a creamy, shiny white.

‘Worth two hundred and fifty pounds, that one,’ he informed them.

There were walrus and rhinoceros tusks in the great warehouse, and
twisted tusks, at least five feet long, from an Arctic fish, out of
which ivory beads were made.

The spice warehouse had the delicious odor which Nora associated with
Eastern tales. Nutmegs, cloves, cinnamon, all sent up great columns of
pungent sweetness. Wool—rubber—in all stages. Australia, South Africa,
Siam, New Zealand, South America. The tropics—frozen seas. These far-off
treasures nursed the imagination, freed one from the confining walls of
Saint Ethelfreda. Oceans—typhoons—sailors—deserts—caravans—the whole
world.

The wine cellars the guide saved for the end, dim, cavernous spaces, the
roofs white with the fungus growth from evaporating wine, through which
both the guide and his party carried torches which lighted up the rows
of fat casks.

But the heavy fog had shut off the river from the Docks, and no boats
could land, so the wharves stretched out into nothing.

On the way back to the Underground station, they passed the Shadwell
Market. Nora thought herself hardened to misery after almost three
months of London. She had learned to hurry past the men without legs
sprawled on the pavement who drew pictures with colored chalk and held
out ragged caps for pennies. She had seen a wild-eyed man feeding out of
a garbage can in Tottenham Court Road. From the steps of the National
Gallery she had watched the army of unemployed mass themselves at
Trafalgar Square, distribute their handbills, with unutterable woes upon
them, and make their terrible speeches. She knew that if she stood in a
theatre queue to get a cheap seat in the pit, she must endure the lively
business contrived by those who gathered in a few pennies where they
could. There were the three blind men led past the queue by a fat boy.
They seemed to follow her everywhere. And the organ wheeled up by the
red-faced man, who played and sang, while his friend, with iron plates
on his heels, did a clog dance. There were whole families trooping past,
avoiding the penalties of begging by some pathetic display of feeble
wits. Card tricksters, melodramatic monologuists with appeals to heaven,
fiddlers, pipers—all of them concluding with the same speech before they
passed the hat. They were the London unemployed. The world owed them a
living. ‘Only a penny or two, Miss.’

But Nora was not prepared for Shadwell Market filled with Christmas
toys. Dolls hung suspended over the crowd of children, beautiful smiling
dolls with pink and blue dresses, perambulators for dolls, beds,
bureaus, little stoves, boxes of dishes with tiny teapots, Noah’s arks,
games, drums, pistols, trumpets, a toy telephone, pails of candy,
barrels of nuts, apples, oranges. Around them crowded dirty ragged
little girls of eight or ten, carrying pale babies under their shawls.
Even little boys carried babies. Women, tugging small children by the
hand, showed that there would soon be new babies. Babies—more babies. So
the world goes on.

Nora had never seen so many children before. And children in a great
flock often bring tears to the eyes, even if they are happy children.

The others stopped to buy some toys for the college Christmas tea. Nora
bought dolls, and, when she came to the dirtiest little girls carrying
the dirtiest babies, she slipped a doll into their hands.

The next morning in the British Museum, Nora picked up a book Annette
had wanted her to read. It was about the emotion of beauty. She read a
few pages. Then the book dropped from her hand. She had decided that
instant to write a history of women in the textile industries.


 VI

Nora was sitting in the drawing-room of Michael Powys’s brother Tom in
Felton, two hours out of Manchester. Mrs. Powys, in a setting of heavily
stuffed furniture and large oil paintings, was pouring tea. Her fat
blonde face had an amusing lack of harmony with her elaborate Paris
clothes.

‘I can’t understand why you want to see our mills, Miss Deming. You
won’t understand anything about it. I never can. Just fearful noise and
queer hot smells. I insisted that we live as far away as possible. All
that dirt, you know. Even here the atmosphere is always smoky. Please
don’t talk about the mill to Tom when he comes in. He’s so bored by any
reference to it. Of course, it’s profitable. But if he hadn’t inherited
the business, we could live in London or near by like Michael and
Ellen.’

As a preparation for her visit the next morning, Mrs.
Powys felt impelled to contribute a few facts. ‘They’re so
ungrateful—working-people. The more you do for them, the worse they
are. In bad times, my husband has kept his factory going at a loss to
himself just to help out his hands. But they never appreciate such
things.’

‘Isn’t it also an advantage to an employer to keep his industry from
getting disorganized?’ Nora inquired.

‘Not at all. And you’ll be shocked at the way those mill women neglect
their children. Off to the mills before their babies are two weeks old.
The children grow up wild—hardly know their mothers. If they’d stay
home, take care of their houses, and cook proper meals——’

It was useless to bring a ray of light into Mrs. Powys’s dark views of
the human beings upon whom her husband’s industry rested.

In the morning Nora motored through hilly country dark with smoke to Mr.
Powys’s mill, where the manager conducted her over the various
departments, shouting out explanations of the mysteries of cotton
manufacture above the din of machinery. He had worked for the Powys
family, father and son, for forty years. Splendid men both of them. If
their workers did the right thing, they couldn’t have better employers.
But with these trade-unions putting wrong ideas in people’s heads and
the Labor Party in Parliament shouting nonsense, there was no telling
what England was coming to.

On the second day Nora was conducted through another mill by Miss
Chapin, a slow-moving, spiritless woman in the Welfare Department, and a
friend of Maggs and the girls at the Working-Women’s College near
London. They went from one hot, noisy department to another. In the
spinning-room Nora found women employed, ring-spinning it was instead of
the mules, which required men. Many of the workers were far advanced in
pregnancy. And the little ‘doffers’ who took off the full bobbins and
put on empty ones appeared very small for the legal fourteen years.
Neglect and undernourishment had not made giants of North England
people.

Miss Chapin was discouraged about her mill. It belonged to an extensive
limited company, with no concern in the industry except its profits. She
disapproved of the policy carried out of taking back the mothers of
illegitimate children, a bad example for the rest of the girls.

‘The only difference they make is that the unmarried girls have to stop
work a month sooner than the others. But they can come back as quick.
They bring their babies in here for me to see. It’s seldom they try to
get rid of them. I can’t understand it.’

There was much Miss Chapin would never understand. But she did the best
she could.

Nora mingled in the noon crowd of mill people hurrying home to their
dinner. The women, with their shawls thrown over their heads shuffling
along in clogs, were a picturesque novelty to a stranger. Small brothers
and sisters came bringing lunches wrapped up in red bandanna
handkerchiefs for members of the family who lived too far away from the
mills to go home. The babies in the perambulators, potential spinners
and weavers, in a community where few children dreamed of a future
outside the mills, looked sad to Nora. The rows of stone houses buzzed
with the excitement of home-coming workers. A little doffer whom Nora
had noticed in the spinning-room smiled at her from a group of children.

‘I seen you in our place,’ she said shyly.

Nora ate a cheerless bun and a hard banana on a chilly bench near the
trolley line. The spring sun had been obscured by the dense clouds of
smoke which gave the manufacturing community a uniformly drab
countenance. Raw winds blew from the sea.

The trolley entered a beautiful valley, with tall chimneys rising above
the pale green foliage of early spring. The next mill, unlike the
efficiently constructed new building Nora had seen in the morning, was a
rambling structure close to a stream, which had evidently in an early
day furnished water-power. Miss Gretton, the Welfare Worker, in a light
but littered office, was a brown-eyed, enthusiastic woman close to
forty. She was attached to her employer, John Alexander, by a lifetime
of the finest kind of association. He was a grand old man with four sons
just like him. All of them lived within sight of the factory. Four
generations of workers had toiled in the mills established by the
grandfather of the present owner. His employees were recognized in all
the mill towns clustered around Manchester as steady, honest men and
women. Old John Alexander got the best of them. He tolerated no
looseness. No children born out of wedlock in his mills. One case in
fifty years, Miss Gretton said.

The workers stopped to show Nora what they were doing. At piece-rates,
this meant the gift of valuable time. But a pleasant spirit of
friendliness was evident everywhere. One woman explained her warping;
another, handsome and pink-cheeked, her weaving. In a corner a very old
man, following an obsolete method of warping, worked alone.

‘He was too old to learn the new ways,’ Miss Gretton explained. ‘He
never looks up from his work. I don’t believe he’s seen me all the years
I’ve been here.’

In the Welfare Room a young girl brought tea for the visitor. All the
mill was taking a few minutes off for afternoon ‘drinking.’ Over their
tea Miss Gretton admitted that family mills like hers were rapidly being
replaced by the limited companies operating scores of mills, with
impersonal, disinterested stockholders. The paternalistic system must
give way to some organization where the workers looked out for their own
well-being. But the old way, at least their mill, had been so pleasant.
Like Miss Chapin she had her discouragements. The evening clubs designed
for the improvement of the work-people were not well attended.

‘The cinema and boxing matches—it’s all some of them want. And now the
radio. We’ve tried reviving some of the old village customs—the games,
the May pole and the May dances. There’s one old man who’s tried to keep
them up. But they don’t care. And the reading-clubs and the
lectures—what was my father’s life and mine—only a few want them.’

When they parted at the trolley station, Nora, waving from the car until
she could see Miss Gretton’s sturdy figure no longer, knew that her book
was already begun.


 VII

In the British Museum reading-room Nora began over again. All her life
she had been preparing herself for the new book. Women—women—their
lives—their frustrations—their work. What she had done before was like
the background of a tapestry—sky, earth, trees. She was now to weave in
the main figures. These she had already started in the dark winter weeks
when she thought she was accomplishing nothing.

She wanted to be fair, to observe all sides, to keep her historic
perspective. She was pleading no cause, making no case. Perhaps when the
facts were gathered, her readers could go farther. But that was not for
her. She no longer felt fatigue. Every day showed some trifle finished.
More and more hours—stretching out the labor of the day.

At night the immensity of her task lay before her when she tried to
sleep, like the vast spaces of the heavens which she had observed with
Ruth Ewing back in Petersfield. All those whirling worlds and millions
of suns. Numbers, space, time, they went beyond all human comprehension.
Hardy’s ‘Two in a Tower,’ which Ruth had sent her at Christmas time. The
poetry of the sky. Immeasurable infinity. A big undertaking. It was what
one tried to do. All those women—all countries—all ages. The little she
could do for England was less than the smallest star. Generations of
women spinning—spinning—spinning. In Greece, in Palestine, all over
Europe. China, India, Japan—perhaps she would never know much about
them. Even in England the Wyf of Bath, the ladies Piers Plowman had
chided, Margaret of Shrewsbury—but so many more. Then women the
weavers—carders—winders. In her dreams one pale figure after
another—stretching on and on—an endless procession—the same unending
toil. Spiders—they had become spiders. Long silken threads everywhere.
Had she read of a woman changed into a spider? A Greek tale. She was
awake.

 * * * * *

‘Are you made so, Nora, that in the spring you can shut yourself up in
that miserable B.M.?’ inquired Annette sarcastically. ‘Live on books in
the spring? The sun so beautiful. The sunshine on my face, my hands—it
is as if I, too, were growing like the flowers. The daffodils springing
up in the grass. By moonlight I wander down to the lake. Like
butterflies fluttering—the daffodils on the edge of the water. Another
week, and they will be gone. My poor little Nora. Is it the little old
men in the skull caps in that B.M. reading-room she dreams about at
night?’ Annette, laughing, bobbed forward and kissed Nora with her
double-cheeked French kiss.

‘You don’t think you are working too hard?’ inquired Lucy gently.

At first Nora fought against the pain in her neck which climbed to the
top of her head and lay there like a heavy pressing stone and then went
down, down her back, until each aching nerve cried out, and she could
not sleep. Through a course of spring lectures at the School of
Economics given by the most famous historian of industrial England, a
man who emerged seldom from his country library, Nora hung on. Each day
a little harder than the one before. One morning in May she had to stay
in bed.

Matron, the college nurse, said all she needed was rest. She shut
Annette and Lucy out of the room. She pushed back the curtains so that
the sunshine streamed through wide-open windows, and one could hear the
birds singing in the garden, and smell the tang of the earth and the
sweetness of the flowers. Nothing but rest. Sleep—sleep once more. One
hour after another. The church bells—the clicking heels in the hallway
between lectures, and the voices and the strike of balls in the near-by
tennis courts.

At last Nora got restless. She wanted to see Annette and Lucy. She must
thank Miss Talbot for the irises she had sent. Matron gave her a
reluctant smile. ‘Well,’ she said, ‘perhaps you may have a visitor now
and then.’

From Clara, Nora first learned that London was not so peaceful as usual.
Clara had come in from her walk through her favorite streets, and was
making a little fire, now that the chill of evening was coming on.

When she faced around, Nora detected an unnatural excitement in her
usually calm eyes. ‘I don’t know what we’re coming to,’ she began. ‘Not
even the paper this morning to tell us the news.’ Clara reached for the
scuttle and shook it violently over the blazing kindlings. ‘They’ve gone
mad—don’t know what they’re doing. Everything’s stopped—the trains they
say. And all those dock-men down where you young ladies was——’

‘You mean the miners—that it’s a sympathetic strike——’ Nora in her sick
retreat had not seen a paper for days. She sat up in bed and stared at
Clara.

‘There’s none o’ my sympathy in it.’ Clara turned with a snap toward
Nora. ‘What do they think they’re doin’—London with no milk, butter,
nothing. It’s starvation. Like the war. I know how it’ll go.’

Tabby during her frequent short visits had never mentioned the strike.
To one suffering from nervous fatigue she was a delightful visitor. She
had been ill during the war—a breakdown it was—and she had a skill in
darting lightly over inconsequential topics which was both restful and
entertaining.

What had prostrated Tabby? Had the war stricken her? One would never
know.

To-night, having heard Clara’s excited tones she divined at once what
they had been discussing.

‘I hoped you wouldn’t know,’ she said, when she and Nora were alone.
‘But of course that would be impossible.’ Tabby stretched out in the big
chair near the fire. She looked very weary, her color gone, her eyes
protruding and heavily circled. ‘Things will be patched up,’ she
continued. ‘They always are in England, you know. We make no desperate
experiments—tearing down the old and beginning all over again. The Labor
leaders are already scared. The strike has been too successful—the
suddenness and completeness. They could do almost anything if they
dared. But they only talk. Every one talks. In a few days we’ll go on
the same old way.’

Nora began to tell Tabby about her interrupted book, her visits to
industrial England, her months of reading.

‘You are older than most of the students here, Miss Deming?’

‘Yes,’ Nora answered.

‘I thought so.’ Tabby was gazing into the fire. ‘I wonder if our
universities will ever be anything but the playing-fields of infants.’

‘I’d forgotten—that we, too, are like that—until I came here. I thought
at first we were better—less intellectually provincial—more interested
in the broader aspects of learning. But lying here—so many things——’

Tabby had no illusions, then. But why couldn’t she do something—treat
the girls like equals—show them her own mind brilliant, clear,
relentlessly logical?

Even Annette had caught glimpses of the real Tabby. ‘She talks French
like an angel,’ Annette often said. ‘I love her for that. For making my
language beautiful—not like the rest of the English.’

Annette was beginning to droop. She longed for Paris. May in Paris. In
London, the daffodils had become shrivelled husks on green stems. Before
Nora was well enough to go back to the B.M., the general strike was
over. The suddenness of the end no one could understand. The strikers
assumed an apologetic attitude toward their recent insurrection. Only
the miners held out, deserted by the rest, they thought, with no relief
in sight. The vague promises of gentlemen—not for them.


 VIII

Nora, seated in the taxi beside Annette, was seeing her off to Paris.
That morning she had said good-bye to Lucy, fighting back her tears. The
year was over. Annette, waving toward the red-brick buildings visible
through the trees until they were hidden from her sight, threw herself
against Nora and sobbed.

‘Dear Saint Ethelfreda, dear Saint Ethelfreda. Will I ever see the
beloved college again?’

‘But Annette,’ Nora reminded her, ‘you know you really didn’t like it
here.’

‘A whole year of my life—a whole year. Part of myself I leave behind me.
Never again will those happy days return to me.’

At the station she wept afresh. ‘We are sisters,’ she sobbed against
Nora’s shoulder. ‘Sisters forever. Although the great ocean is between
us, never will I forget my dear Nora. Never until I die.’ The train
sighed and began to move, Annette mopped her eyes, waved her
handkerchief, and threw kisses to her dear friend. At last Nora was
alone on the platform, friendless in London.

In the deserted college, she began to pack her trunk hurriedly. She was
leaving London in the morning to go down to Cornwall for a few weeks.
After that, she planned to take lodgings near the B.M. and read through
the summer.

Late that night, she stood looking out of the window. There was a
shimmer of moonlight over the garden, and she could smell the roses in
the border. Like Annette, she felt a stirring of the heart at leaving
Saint Ethelfreda. When she had said good-bye to the girls, she had got a
glimpse into their locked lives. She pitied them as she pitied herself.

England, bowed down by taxes, by debt, by its huge pauper population,
fed just enough by the dole to save it from utter starvation, held out
no rosy promises to girls of the impoverished middle class. They would
never marry the young men who in normal times would have become their
husbands. Young men were too poor. If they married, they must seek
brides with a dowry.

Consciously or unconsciously, after observing the spinsterhood of their
native villages which went for a walk in the afternoon, poured out tea
in a fussy, chilly drawing-room, or looked over the ‘Times’ before
dinner, these girls had worked out escape from such a life by going to
the universities. The hope of a profession—teaching or medicine—that was
life for them. And in fighting for this kind of existence, they crushed
out weakness, softness. They seemed hard. Struggling for scholarships,
for ‘firsts,’ for posts with a living stipend—one had to be hard. And
Miss Talbot, who knew so much better than the girls what was ahead of
them, held them to their work. She had appeared tyrannical, cruel, but——

Nora, so much older, with so much more of the struggle behind her, fewer
years of helpless longing for the overpowering emotion that clutched at
the throat, stung the eyes, made stabs of pain at the breast—for her now
there was the salty flavor of work. But for them——

A tap on the door. Clara held something in her hand. ‘A letter, Miss, by
the late post.’

The letter was from Judge Andrews. Cordelia was dead. She had been dead
almost three weeks. The end had come quickly. Two weeks before her death
she had been living alone, no one but her family doctor suspecting there
was little of life left to her. The malady had been of long standing,
ever since the year Nora went away to college, Judge Andrews believed.
During the last two years, when Nora had not been back to Maronac,
Cordelia had been much worse. The previous summer Doctor Elliot had
wanted to send for Nora before she started abroad, but Cordelia had
refused. She had been a resolute, valiant sufferer.

Cordelia’s last letter was in Nora’s bag to be answered in the quiet of
Cornwall. She brought it out now. The date over a month back. Close to
the end. The crabbed, almost undecipherable writing was unchanged, the
news the usual parish happenings. Della had a baby of her own now. She
and Nora would go together to see it next summer. Cordelia wrote, made
plans for the future when she knew she was dying.

A resolute woman. Resolute and self-denying always. That terrible
abnegation of Cordelia’s. Regret was useless. It was too late. Cordelia
with gnawing pain for two years, and no one near her. That lonely
house—the succession of empty rooms—Cordelia waiting to die.

The rest of Judge Andrews’s letter was business. Cordelia left
everything to Nora, more than most people thought she had, the house
with all of its furnishings, and property and investments which brought
a comfortable income.

Nora’s eyes were dry as she stared at the facts of Judge Andrews’s
letter. If she had loved Cordelia more—been kinder—remembered her
oftener.

Nora realized for the first time what it meant to be entirely alone. She
had no one. The tears came. She sobbed in the stillness of her
dismantled room in Saint Ethelfreda College.


 CHAPTER II


 I

All the afternoon the scene from the train window had been the dreary
wastes of Cornwall moors, trees tortured and dwarfed by twisting sea
gales, stretches of furze, gorse, and rock, occasionally enlivened by
sprightly clusters of purple flowers which tossed in the summer wind.
Perhaps Nora had made a mistake to come. The Cornwall of which she had
dreamed in London had been that of rocky coasts, with brilliant
contrasts of sea, cliffs, and sky, and a midsummer sun which shone
daily. Such beauty, she remembered now, had its foundation only in
Margaret Abington’s raptures over the summer she had spent at the
farmhouse near Trebavas, where Nora was going; a memory to which
Margaret returned again and again. Usually her friend had been right
about places.

Nora’s travelling companions had done nothing to relieve the monotony of
the journey. Opposite her sat a pale, dark man whose spiritless
expression and well-kept, bloodless hands suggested illness. For some
time he had looked as if he wanted to talk to some one, but she had
gazed steadily out of the window. She was afraid he would begin to tell
her about his ruined health. Beside him was a young mother with a very
active child, a smeary-faced little girl with thin, molasses-tinted hair
which her mother had tried to curl. A querulous voice followed her
succession of bobbings up and down, ‘Now, Annie, be good; sit still,
Annie. No, you can’t have more sweeties, dearie.’

The child was now plunging toward the fourth traveller in the carriage,
whose face, hidden behind his London paper, Nora had not seen since he
entered. When the porter had arranged his luggage in the rack, very
good, but long used, the dull sheen of it, its substantial quality, had
made her curious about its owner, a tall man in shaggy tweeds and golf
stockings who had slipped quietly into the seat beside her a moment
before the train moved. He had the careless, travelled ease of the bags
over his head, an aloofness to his companions in the carriage.

Annie’s waving, sticky hands now clutched at the defences of newspaper.

‘Annie,’ shouted her mother, ‘don’t trouble the gentleman. I’m sorry,
sir,’ she apologized, giving the child’s hands a quick smack.

‘Not at all. No trouble,’ he murmured, once more resuming his reading.

Nora, roused from the dreariness of the landscape, had looked quickly
toward the disturbed traveller. He was, as she had suspected, like those
young men she had met at Ellen’s, who were nothing in particular, with
the baffling charm of nonchalant, cheerful acceptance of that importance
which is superior to any mere profession or slavery to the arts. They
had achieved significance at birth, with no trouble to themselves. If it
had been trouble——But this stranger, at second glance, was not entirely
of their brotherhood. He was lean like them, but with more iron about
him. His face was bronzed, his very dark hair burned by the sun. His
prominent cheek-bones and his nose and jaw had the angularity which
might be Scotch. Pictures of Highland chieftains showed the same hawk
profile. His general bearing, his erectness as he sat in the swaying
carriage, suggested military discipline—the army—a man who gave orders.

The pale man, encouraged by the confusion, looked up once more at Nora.
He pointed to the purple flowers clustered against the rocks.

‘Foxgloves. That’s Cornwall. They’re everywhere.’

‘But it’s desolate country,’ she said.

‘You wait.’ A bland smile lighted up the drooping features. ‘Wait till
you get near the sea.’ His gaze wandered to the man next to her, but the
newspaper was too much of a barrier. He went on talking to Nora. ‘You’ve
never been in these parts before?’

‘No.’

‘You’re travelling toward the coast?’

‘Yes.’ Then, not wishing to seem rude to a person so obviously ill, she
added, ‘I’m going to Trebavas. You know it? I get off at Pelreath.’

There was a rustle of newspaper beside her. Her neighbor’s eyes were
turned in her direction. They were deep blue, with a direct glance that
did not avoid hers, as he slowly measured her with a detached, swift
interest before he returned to his paper.

‘Very fine coast,’ the pale man said. ‘I know the south coast better.
Around Penzance. I go there for the climate.’

‘Oh, yes.’ He was ill, then. She was conscious of the difference between
the two men in the carriage, the tanned vigor of one, the anæmic
transparency of the other. He wouldn’t live long. Perhaps he knew.

A deep voice interrupted Nora’s speculations. ‘Would you care to look at
this?’ The man in tweeds was holding out his newspaper.

Although she didn’t care to read, she thanked him, and made a pretence
of examining the headings on the first page.

‘The cricket matches at Lord’s—they’re on page 12,’ he suggested.

She smiled. ‘I’m afraid I don’t care much about cricket.’

‘Oh.’ The rising and falling of his interjection was like Tabby’s.

‘I don’t understand cricket.’

‘It’s very simple, really.’ He was now smiling too, his smile making him
unexpectedly boyish and a little less assured. His blue eyes had a gleam
which attracted the smeary little girl a second time. She turned from
the pictures in her mother’s gaudy magazine and looked at him. Then,
dropping down from her seat, she planted a sticky fist upon his knee.

‘Annie, don’t you bother the gentleman.’

‘Please don’t be troubled on my account.’ Again that aloof expression
which covered his face like a mask. ‘I, too, am going to Trebavas,’ he
said, turning once more toward Nora to escape the mother’s embarrassed
apologies. ‘It’s an unusual place to go. Very isolated and dull,
really.’

‘I had a friend who spent a summer there and loved it. It’s because of
her I’m going.’

He crossed one leg over the other, and shifted his position in his
corner seat so that they faced each other. ‘Yes—the quiet. I understand.
And very fine cliffs.’

‘She has told me about them. The colors—the blackness of them and then
the deep green sea.’

‘My old school friend is rector there. I’ve been coming to him for short
visits a long time now. Rather a nice old Norman church. He’s very keen
about it. He’ll be glad to show it to you. It’s away from the village,
close to the cliffs. One hears the sea all through the prayers.’

‘How lovely!’

‘Yes, but——’ He looked smilingly toward Nora’s childlike face under her
severe blue travelling hat. ‘It may be too unexciting for a young
person.’

He evidently took her for an eccentric American schoolgirl, wandering
about in queer places. She smiled to herself, and to hide the quiver of
her lips looked out of the window again.

With late afternoon, the sun, falling lower, made long shadows on the
moor and deepened the mauve of the massed foxgloves to purple. One would
not care to walk alone in this threatening, waste country. The sick man
and the troublesome child first went to sleep, then the tired mother.
Nora and her neighbor were left to themselves. They conversed from time
to time in low tones. A shyness swept over her. She took up the paper
lying between them.

‘I wonder if they’ll take Germany into the League this summer.’

‘You believe in the League? I thought Americans didn’t, you know.’

‘Yes—many of us. We place our hopes in anything that——’

‘You have no interest in the glories of our late disturbance, then?’ he
inquired.

He was clearly enough a military man. No need to be silly or get
excited. One’s real opinions—they are saved for the rare moments—not for
a railway carriage.

‘No,’ she answered, ‘not very much.’

There was something queer about his eyes, behind the deep blue of them
and the directness of their glance, which she could not understand. He
was hiding something—not from her only, a stranger, but from every one.
Some hurt—some painful secret.

Looking out, Nora saw Pelreath painted on the station benches. Some
ponies with two-wheeled carts were waiting near the platform. As she
began to bestir herself, the sleepers awoke. The tall stranger was
lifting her bags from the rack and carrying them out to the platform. He
raised his hat, and turned to get his own luggage. The pale man smiled
at her, the mother and child waved. In front of her a slight Englishman
in clerical dress was examining the passengers. He looked a little like
Father Stokesbury—the same brilliant smile.

‘Neville, old boy! I couldn’t see you at first.’

The wind-swept wastes, the rocks, the furze and gorse. A pang of
desolation swept Nora. She had come all this distance to throw herself
among strangers.

‘Miss Deming? The American lady? I got the pony an’ jingle over this
way, Miss.’ A mild little man had approached her, his stiff hat held
like a salver in front of him.

As Nora climbed into the cart, she saw her travelling companion driving
off in a grey two-seater with his old friend. He lifted his hat and
smiled before they disappeared behind the tall hedges of the road toward
Trebavas.

‘The rector’s got his friend down again,’ observed Nora’s driver. ‘Army
man. Major West.’ As they passed between the hedges which shut out the
landscape, he introduced himself as Thomas Pentillie, Mrs. Bodmin’s new
husband. She was Bodmin now only for business. They had been married two
weeks. ‘In time for the summer visitors,’ he explained. ‘I’m a big help
to her that way.’

The road turned suddenly and ascended above the valley. There was a rush
of air, and then Nora saw the sea. It was booming against a deep purple
cliff lost in green water and pale green spray.

‘Oh,’ she breathed.

Mrs. Pentillie, who as Mrs. Bodmin had known Miss Abington, a very
pleasant American lady always out on the rocks painting, ushered Nora up
to a little room on the third floor with a view of the sea.

‘It was hers—your friend’s. I remember her wantin’ the top o’ the
house,’ Mrs. Pentillie said.

Nora stood looking out of the open window. A chill filled the room now
that the sun lay low over the water. It was almost time for supper, Mrs.
Pentillie had announced. Only a moment or two before she had to join the
strange Britishers below, sit down at a table crowded with holiday
visitors. She could hear their voices. Somehow she had not thought of
them before—only seacoast and great spaces. She was afraid. She wasn’t
very hungry. If she could walk out toward the sea first—not have any
supper. Walk toward the church—a bell tinkled, tinkled. Doors slammed.
Some one laughed. Again the bell. Nora washed hastily, brushed back her
hair, and went downstairs.

The other boarders—paying guests they said in England, even at Mrs.
Pentillie’s possibly—were already seated. Nora slipped into a vacant
chair. A strange man, not Mr. Pentillie, was at the head of the table,
carving a guinea fowl. Nora sniffed at it hungrily. An angular woman
next to her smiled.

‘Miss Deming, let me make you acquainted with every one.’ She began to
call out the names of the people gathered around the table.

‘Oh, the American lady,’ commented a stout woman down near the guinea
fowls. She paused in a monologue she had begun upon the Second Coming.
‘We been hearing about you. Travelling all alone, too. You Americans are
so adventurous.’

Opposite Nora sat a husband and wife talking together. There was
something so united, so indivisible about them, that Nora sensed their
relationship at once. Next to Miss Wallis, who had conducted the
introductions, were two women who looked exactly like her, the same dun
coloring which was neither brunette nor blonde, the same bloodless faces
and bony figures. They were sisters, Miss Wallis told her, all taking
the holiday they looked forward to the whole year.

‘After a winter in London,’ she concluded.

The other two bobbed behind or in front of their obstructing relative,
and said a word or two to Nora. This was a beautiful place. She would
love it. Miss Wallis explained about the others in a low tone during the
passing of plates. The man serving, Mr. Downey, was on furlough from
Central Africa. Some Government job. His wife hadn’t seen him in three
years. She couldn’t stand the climate. The child between them, a bright
little thing, was almost four years old. Sad—living that way. And the
two on the other side, aunt and niece, both post-office clerks in
London. Nora looked in the direction of a stout black-haired woman with
a face like a tomato and a girl much like her, slightly less red, with a
shaggy mane of coarse, dark bobbed hair.

‘They can’t talk without swearing,’ Miss Wallis said sadly.

On Nora’s end of the table were two middle-aged women dressed in heavy
black. They were discussing the church services of the preceding Sunday.
The church was, then, not very far away.

After supper, when the rest of the procession trooped into the
drawing-room, the Wallis sisters offered to show Nora the path to the
cliffs. In a week they had learned all the pleasant walks in the
vicinity. As the four passed back of the house, two of Mrs. Pentillie’s
Leghorn hens trailed after them. Cows in a near-by farmyard looked up,
and then came slowly toward the fence. As they walked along the narrow
hedge-bordered path, the youngest of the three sisters joined Nora. She
was probably not much over thirty, less neutral than the other two, with
a sturdy spring to her step unlike the drag of theirs.

‘Two men among us,’ she began. ‘One a Quaker—the other from Africa. And
both married. Rare, isn’t it? Is this what I’ve come down from London
for?’

Her sisters behind laughed heartily. They regarded Dora as a terrible
wag, Nora guessed almost immediately.

‘And the Quaker lady hangs on to her husband as faithfully as to her
pocket-book,’ Dora went on.

All three regarded the middle-aged femininity of their fellow guests as
a tremendous joke on Adeline Pinks, the post-office niece, probably as
much on her aunt, one of those maiden ladies who never knows that her
day is over.

‘They keep an eye out for what Pentillie will bring from the station.
“Another woman,” they said, when you drove up before supper.’

‘I’m sorry,’ Nora said, laughing.

They climbed over one slate hedge. A light mist lay over the cliffs
above the sea, which resounded from below. The high grass between the
hedge-rows was very wet, the foxgloves against the stone walls drooping
with their load of dew. As she was mounting the stairway made of flat
stones over the second hedge, she heard her first lark. The bird had
swooped up, and was singing, singing as he climbed higher and higher.
She sat on the flat top of the stile, looking up toward the sky and
listening to the warblings, fainter and fainter. Just as the poets had
told her, singing as all of her life she had dreamed they would sing.
The three sisters stood waiting for her to climb over the hedge. While
she had been sitting on the stile, the larks had slowly gathered in the
sky, one after another, hundreds of them, it seemed, singing together,
close to the last yellow light.

They went on toward the cliffs. Far below sounded the sea, covered with
a lacy haze, like thick cobwebs. They stood, looking down, not speaking
a word.

They followed the path close to the edge of the cliffs, as it twisted in
and out. Another hedge. Standing on the last tone, Nora saw a square
tower rising above a dip in the flat stretches of green. The church. A
queer feeling, like pain, made her turn.

‘Shouldn’t we be going back? It’s getting dark.’

Between the last hedge-rows, in sight of Mrs. Pentillie’s lights, night
had already fallen. They crept cautiously forward. In the drawing-room
the household was gathered around the Government official from Africa
telling tales. Nora paused at the doorway, listened a moment, and then
went upstairs.


 II

In the morning she hurried through breakfast and took the path which led
to the sea. The sky above her was deep sapphire, the sun strong and
clear. She could hear the lambs bleating from the other side of the
hedges, the tinkle of bells, the ecstasy of the morning sea. She
followed the general direction of the preceding night, and sat close to
the edge of the cliff, looking down. Some sheep were grazing in the
grassy crevices between the black rocks. The gulls swept close over her
head, and dipped down to the water, with hoarse shrieks. A sweetness—a
familiar sweetness was all around her. Wee purple flowers close to the
ground everywhere. Thyme—wild thyme. She knew from the smell. She
pressed a purple cluster between her fingers.

A crunching of rocks, and footsteps approaching. Possibly some one from
Mrs. Pentillie’s house. Nora gazed steadily out upon the sea.

‘Good-morning.’ The tweeds—the familiar voice. ‘I felt sure I should see
you again.’

Major West seated himself on the rock beside her. ‘Jolly day, isn’t it?
So you’re a near neighbor of Reggie’s? Over there? How very good! And do
you think you’ll like this place?’

She assured him that she liked it, that already she loved it. Trebavas
was what she had wanted. She went on to describe the Pentillie
gathering, its middle-aged quality. They would not bother her. She liked
them that way best, she had decided. They wouldn’t wander far away from
Mrs. Pentillie’s summer-house, with the British flag floating over it, a
border of geraniums, and a comfortable view of the sea.

They sat looking over the shining blue water. He brought out a pipe,
knocked it against the rock. The sheep bounded up the trail and tinkled
past them. Once more the larks were beginning to sing, a faint, cheerful
squeak only, high in the sky.

‘Reggie’s in the church. Should you care to see it? Oh—I forgot to
introduce myself yesterday. Neville West——’

She murmured her name in return, and he helped her up from the rock.
They climbed over the stiles, passed rude heaps of stone, like the ruins
of a castle wall, and entered the churchyard. Reginald Enderby stood in
the doorway. As she saw him in the sunshine, holding a funny
broad-brimmed hat like Father Stokesbury’s, he looked enough like him to
be an older brother—older than Father Stokesbury, so long ago. When he
smiled, he was like an old friend.

‘Miss Deming would like to see the church, Reggie.’

‘Delighted, dear boy. It’s really a snug little place, Miss Deming.’

The church was like a twilight chamber. Its thick walls were cut by
Norman windows with dim glass; there was a Norman font near the door,
with four fat legs and a funny face cut into the stone.

‘Major West says this old chap looks like me.’ The rector twinkled.

It was a beautiful little church in its dusky way, with its timbered
roof, its black-oak rood screen, its dull seats, and grey stone walls.
Looking out through the open doorway, one felt the peace of its shade, a
retreat from the burning brightness of the sky and sea. The rector
pointed out the tombstones in the churchyard propped up with buttresses.

‘To keep the winter winds from blowing them away,’ he explained. ‘We
have some jolly blows here then.’

She glanced back for another look at the interior. ‘You like our old
places?’ he asked her.

‘Very much.’

‘Perhaps you’d be kind enough to have tea with us in the garden this
afternoon. The widow of the former rector is coming. It would be a
pleasure to her, I know. There are some ruins of the thirteenth-century
monastery there I’d like to show you.’

Major West walked back toward Mrs. Pentillie’s with Nora. She was late
for dinner. Mr. Downey had finished serving.

‘Well, where you been?’ inquired the large woman, who was still arguing
the tenets of Seventh Day Adventism. ‘I thought you’d jumped over the
cliff.’

 * * * * *

In the rectory garden Nora was seated beside Mrs. Cecil, the widow of
Father Enderby’s predecessor, white-haired, her large hands covered with
diamonds. As Mrs. Cecil talked, a cigarette dangled from her mouth. When
the others took the burden of the conversation, she smoked with quick
puffs one cigarette after another. She was like a chimney, with good
drawing powers.

Major West talked very little. He sat holding his pipe, looking across
first at one and then the other of Reggie’s guests. When they were
exploring the monastery after tea, Father Enderby took possession of
Nora, Major West coming up slowly behind with Mrs. Cecil, who had little
interest in ecclesiastical remains, she said.

‘I’m one myself,’ she informed Nora. ‘Not in very good condition. Can’t
you restore me—like the church—Reggie?’

The monastery ruins were a hoary background for the garden. Foxgloves
grew close to the lichen-covered walls, and there were clematis vines
over one sunny end.

‘Charming—charming,’ Mrs. Cecil murmured at appropriate intervals, in
spite of the fact that her best days had been spent here. Reggie really
had improved the garden. Then she confided to Nora: ‘These men know
nothing about my poor legs. Handsome man—Major West. Reggie’s always
talking about him. How violent he looks sometimes! Don’t fall in love
with him, my dear. Young girls always do with that kind of menacing
desperation when it smiles at them.’ Mrs. Cecil laughed at her blush.
‘Take Reggie instead. He’s one of the sweetest creatures. A saint with a
sense of humor.’


 III

Nora had been in Trebavas a week. Since the day of the rectory tea,
Reggie—she thought of him as Reggie always—had assumed the rôle of guide
to the young American who had strayed into his quiet settlement. He
offered himself and his two-seater in her service, Major West with
comical dignity perched behind in the dicky, for a pilgrimage in search
of such neighborhood antiquities as Cornish crosses and churches with
Saxon walls, for a visit to the slate quarry, and a sight of the spot
where Reggie had seen the very rare Cornish chough. Major West laughed
at these excursions, but he did not refuse to join them.

In the primitive Cornwall scene of tumbling sea and rock, Major West,
even in his rough country clothes, remained the sophisticate for whom a
London club window in Saint James’s Street seemed the appropriate
setting. The face, with the arrogant curve of nose and the jaw held with
unconscious pride, was a powerful contrast to Reggie’s mild rusticity.
But between the two men existed an understanding which Nora, as an
outsider, envied. They shared innumerable ancient jokes, had been raw
schoolboys together, made frequent references to the war when they, no
matter how separated, had depended on each other. ‘Good old Reggie,’
Major West said of his friend. ‘Poor sinner,’ Reggie called the Major.

When they stretched out on the grassy heights of the cliffs in the
golden hour before dinner, Reggie, as an amateur botanist, explained the
native flowers blossoming luxuriously all around them. To an American
they would be entirely strange—the gay, yellow Tom Thumb; the sea pinks
which they called thrift, almost like American red clover in color, Nora
told Reggie; the snowy bell-shaped campion, and the pink stone-crop
covering the rocks.

In the midst of Reggie’s talk, Major West walked over to the edge of the
cliff, and stood there tossing slate chips over the steep side. Nora
joined him. Down—down—the stones rattled, until they could no longer be
heard. The rocky walls, unrelieved by lichens, were blue-black
zigzagging ravines against which the waves beat with a muttering,
menacing thunder.

‘And to think that all these flowers grow close up to the edge of such
horror,’ Nora whispered, as the spray struck the slate columns far
below.

Major West nodded, turning his blue eyes, half-closed in a smile, toward
her. ‘Jolly old show—the sea with the tide rushing in,’ he said, as they
confronted the limitless expanse of convulsive green waste, upon which
not even a sail was visible.

Above Doscastle Strand early in the afternoon when the tide was low, the
three of them looked down upon the groups of men, women, and children
seated on the rocks, lying out on the smooth white sand, or plunging
into the sea. The children, as they threw themselves with shouts against
the crests of waves, were like sporting elves, mere midgets of humanity.
Even the men and women had a comically insignificant aspect, funny
little wiggles of arms and legs, puny struts of importance up and down.
Upon the height one felt Olympic grandeur and serenity, detachment from
the pigmy concerns of earth.

‘Don’t you ever go down to swim?’ Nora asked Major West one day.

‘Not of late. I haven’t been swimming this summer.’

The day Reggie had church duties which carried him off to Exeter, Major
West and Nora visited the caves, reached by a steep trail cut into the
cliff. They entered the murky chamber and looked out upon the tossing,
watery plain which could not reach them. But as the tide rose higher,
the waves sent laps of water into the cave. One drew farther back. The
water next time came closer. Soon the cave was filled with a great
clamor. The sea shouted, boomed like a cannon. Nora scrambled hastily up
the steep path to escape the crashing terror of the ocean-filled caves.

‘You make me think of a Himalaya goat,’ he said, coming on more slowly
behind her.

While they rested on the grassy slopes at the top, he talked carelessly
about India, his expeditions in the hills, the sunsets over the
Mediterranean when he was going to Egypt. One gathered that he had been
everywhere.

She, in turn, launched out on her favorite political topics.

‘I didn’t believe women really cared about that sort of thing,’ he
commented. ‘But you Americans are different, I see.’

‘No, I don’t think we are.’

‘Then you’ve been with men more—talked with them—got their point of
view.’

‘Oh!’ Nora gasped, looking at him with wide-open eyes. Then she laughed.
As she reflected on his magnificent conclusion, she laughed harder and
harder. The delight—the wonder—the ridiculousness of fooling people that
way. That she—from men—she could not stop laughing.

‘Do—do help me,’ she begged. ‘I——’ Again ripples of helpless mirth swept
over her, waves, torrents. She could never stop.

‘I know what will make you stop laughing,’ Major West said, looking down
at her with uplifted brows.

‘Tell me,’ she entreated between her seizures. ‘Anything.’

‘You wouldn’t like it.’

‘Yes—yes.’

‘You are very lovely when you laugh that way.’ He pronounced the words
with triumphant calm.

She looked solemn in an instant.

‘See—I knew I could make you stop.’

‘But I—I must tell you why—why I laughed.’ She hesitated. ‘Some other
time.’

The next morning she found him pacing back and forth at the end of Mrs.
Pentillie’s lane.

‘I thought I’d catch you here,’ he began carelessly. ‘Shall we walk
toward Doscastle Strand?’

He sprawled lazily upon a mound of thyme, sniffing at it, then with his
head raised against his clenched hands, regarded Nora seated near him
with his disturbing, direct gaze. His eyes were like bottomless
wells—depths she could not measure—his face a handsome, unintelligible
defence. He had none of Reggie’s comforting simplicity. She felt as if
he were highly amused at her American gawkishness.

‘Do you know, I do like you, Miss Deming,’ he said. ‘Really.’ He gave a
short laugh when he saw a flush spreading to her hair, flaming in the
sunshine. ‘Usually I detest young girls. Their chatter bores me—their
chatter about themselves. But you—you converse so wisely, you know.’

‘I—I’m not so young,’ she protested.

‘Oh, come.’

‘I’m—I’m thirty-two,’ she confessed.

‘Magnificent. Now I understand why we get on so well together. Poor
Reggie! He’s shocked at the way American parents let their young charge
about unguarded. He’s been trying to take care of you during your
Cornwall sojourn. And you—why you’re almost my generation. I’m close to
forty.’

She noticed for the first time the threads of grey in his hair, the deep
lines around his eyes and mouth. ‘I’m going to tell you why I laughed so
hard yesterday, when you——’

‘I haven’t an idea what I said or did.’

‘You said I’d talked to men a lot—had their point of view.’

‘Well, you have, you know.’

‘But not from talking to men. Only to women.’

‘Come, now. That’s a bit thick. Even American women——’

‘Yes, I’m serious. I’ve lived all my life in hives of women.’

‘Good God! Not really?’

‘It’s true. I have. It’s a man’s world I’ve wanted to know—tried to
know. What ought to be everybody’s world. But women all the time—caught
in petty squabbles and backstair talk. Nothing big—nothing——’

‘But isn’t that what the dear creatures want?’

‘No,’ Nora contradicted, beating her fist against a rock. ‘No—no. So
many of us—what we want—I can’t explain. It’s endless. I can’t make you
see. What we have, it’s pitiful—stifling. I can’t stand it any more!’
She burst into unexpected tears, and threw herself face downward on the
ground.

She was so ashamed—women and their tears—how she hated them! And she had
degraded herself even more by pretending it was intellectual
participation in the lives of men she wanted. He would know. He couldn’t
help guessing. How he would despise her—weak, ridiculous creature. But
she was helpless—no control before this man. No one before—this
way——What—what had changed her?

She rose quickly, tried to plan escape. If she could run away, not see
the kindness—the pity in his eyes.

‘I’m—ashamed—ashamed,’ she murmured, her eyes still brimming over with
tears.

He seized her hand so that she could not escape. ‘No—no,’ he protested.
‘I understand. But a man’s world isn’t what you think. It’s a rotten
bore—most of it.’

He was like her. He had missed something. The more she saw of this dark,
grim friend of Reggie’s, the surer she felt that some reason lay behind
his reticence, his consistent distaste for talking about the adventurous
life she envied. She had thought first that he was a cynic, a man of the
world who tossed everything serious away from him. But when he told her
of his childhood, especially of his mother who died when he was nine, a
wistful boyishness replaced his usual mocking tone.

‘I come of an army family,’ he confided one day when the two of them
were searching out new cliff heights, new views far away from the
rectory. ‘Sandhurst was as inevitable for me as Oxford for Reggie. When
I was a youngster, my mother used to talk about Parliament. She was keen
about one’s social responsibilities. Perhaps if she had lived——But
Parliament’s an awful bore, they tell me. All talk and muddling along.
The army didn’t give me time to get bored—constant excitement—first one
part of the world and then another. But after a time——’ He broke off
suddenly and began to talk of other things.

His references to the army were always that way, apologetic,
unenthusiastic. He hated the army. She was sure of it. The conviction
gave her an unaccountable upliftment.

After dinner one night Major West came upon Nora perched on the top of
the stile listening to the skylarks.

‘Reggie’s driven me off. He’s in solemn session with the vestry.’ He sat
down beside her.

The sun was glorious fire close to the sea, which turned pink, lavender,
grey-green as the sun dipped lower and lower. Above them larks, faint
specks in the darkening sky, continued to sing.

‘Let’s walk,’ Nora suggested, drawing her coat around her. ‘It’s cold
the moment the sun is gone.’

As they skirted the edge of the cliff, the shadows imperceptibly crept
toward them, but they walked on. The stunted trees, the hedges, the
hollows were changed into black shapes which rose up suddenly all
around. Nora stumbled in a tangle of gorse.

‘We’ve lost it,’ she said, as Major West took her arm and tried to guide
her back to the path.

Below them the sea moaned, the circling gulls cried. Another step, and
the sea made a nearer clamor. The edge of the cliff, they now realized
for the first time, was lost in darkness. Major West struck a match. The
feeble little circle of light intensified the thick night surrounding
them.

‘Don’t move,’ he said, exploring the ground around them with his cane.
‘We may be close to the edge.’

The undulating folds of grey, dark blue, black, shut out the trees, the
hedges. The tinkle of a sheep bell, the twitter of a sleepy bird broke
the silence between the regular beat of the waves far below.

He took a cautious step backward, drawing her by the hand after him.
Another step backward. He lit a second match.

‘This seems to be a path.’

Slowly they made their way, stumbling over rocks, over roots, over deep,
moist furze, the ground rising up against their feet unexpectedly.

Major West bent over Nora. ‘You’re all right?’ he asked. ‘You aren’t
afraid?’ He laughed at his question.

‘No, no, not afraid,’ she insisted in an unnatural, weak voice. The
nearness of his face to hers, brushing it almost, his shoulder against
hers, the pressure of his arm overpowered her with the sense of their
intimacy, the night bringing them together, enclosing them in a small,
dark room. No, she wasn’t afraid, hovering, maybe, on the edge of the
precipice. They stood motionless, then once more advanced one step at a
time.

At last Mrs. Pentillie’s lights winked in the distance.

‘We were close to the edge,’ Nora said.

‘Yes, I didn’t tell you. Back there, when I reached out with my stick,
there was nothing.’

‘Oh,’ she shuddered.

Then they plunged through the caverns of the hedge toward the lighted
windows and the sound of voices in the drawing-room. They crossed the
lawn unobserved, and Nora guided him to the main road on the other side.

‘Good-night,’ she said hastily, drawing her hand away from his and
hurrying back to the house.

In her room she sank down on the floor in front of the low window. She
was trembling all over. She clutched the sill, and leaned her head over
her tightly clasped hands. No, she hadn’t been afraid out there lost in
the night. But now——In the darkness beyond, the waves crashed toward her
nearer and nearer. Like the rattle of the sea in the caves. Something
swept over her, chilled her, burned her, leaped over her head, and
engulfed her. When she emerged, she was breathless, gasping, as if she
had been fighting against the waves.


 IV

Major West was waiting for Nora outside the rectory gate.

‘Ah,’ he exclaimed, as she came up to him in her moss-green hat and
dress, ‘you’re the color of the sea this morning.’ Then with a sigh, he
added, ‘Poor old Reggie—old Reggie in Exeter,’ and both of them laughed
together.

The larks were swooping up from the ground with flashes of song and
soaring toward the clouds. Timid lambs peered out from behind the
shelter of their mothers at the intruders in their pleasant green
pasture. There were foot-bridges to cross over little streams, with
forget-me-nots clustering around them, their tender, unforgettable blue
eyes blinking up toward the sun. Around interrupting cliffs, following
steep trails up and down, sometimes leaving the sea and coming back to
it gladly again, Nora and Major West tramped toward Roswithiel. From
time to time they flopped down upon a soft bank of flowers to rest.
Flowers were everywhere. One stepped upon them, lay upon them in this
profuse summer season. They lost the trail, floundered in streams and
swampy ground, leaped over pools, found the trail again. The whole
expedition was a gigantic game of hide-and-seek against a prankish
Nature with her tests of endurance.

On a hillside high above the sea, they sat down to eat their luncheon.
Sticking up over the trees behind them was the steeple of Roswithiel
church.

‘It’s a silent little church. There aren’t any bells,’ Major West
explained. ‘They were bringing them by ship from Plymouth, so the story
goes, and there was a wreck in sight of the town. The natives say that
on calm nights they can hear the bells ringing from the depths of the
sea. Reggie can tell you all about it.’

‘It’s a pretty story,’ Nora commented, looking across the meadow where
men were haying. From the fragrance of the new-mown hay and the peace of
the church tower, one turned back to the sea. She peered down—down the
scarred black cliffs—at the sea, rising and falling, high ridges, low
ridges, then a great mountain of heaving, destructive ocean.

A middle-aged woman panted up toward their hilltop. She was staying in
Roswithiel, she said, a very pretty place, but lonely.

‘I heard you laughing together,’ she added, apologizing for her
intrusion, ‘and I felt I must come up. Just the sound of voices
sometimes——My husband and I, when we were young like you, came to
Cornwall for our holidays. But it was a mistake for me to come back
here.’

They begged her to share their luncheon, but she refused. If they didn’t
mind, she’d like to chat with them a bit before she went back to her
lodgings. They had looked so happy sitting there together on the
hillside that she had envied them. Every one ought to be happy. That was
what life was for.

Beyond a twist in the trail, they dropped down into Roswithiel, with its
whitewashed cottages lined up along the narrow stream trickling in from
the sea, where the ducks paddled up and down, the granite quay and
lighthouse mere toys beside the solid wall of rock behind the village.
Hedges of valerian bent their fragrant rosy blossoms over the water.
Children waded in the shallow pools. Old men sat idle in the sunshine.
Scarcely a human sound. Nora and Major West, perched on a hillock
commanding the diminutive settlement, looked down upon the quiet scene.

‘How sweet—how very sweet it all is,’ breathed Nora.

Neville West sat holding one knee between his hands, his eyes blinking
in the brilliant sunshine.

At tea-time they found a retreat from the sun, which had grown steadily
hotter, a cool, dim room, with walls tinted yellow, and yellow curtains
and yellow poppies on the tables painted a cool green.

‘All your color scheme,’ laughed Major West, as they entered.

‘Tea—tea—oceans of tea,’ begged Nora, pouring a cup of tea for him out
of a green-and-yellow teapot.

Beyond the window where they sat, an old mill-wheel turned and splashed
up cool columns of water. Drip—drip. There was green moss on the
silver-grey wood of the wheel, mossy depths in the water. As it turned,
Nora felt the streams flowing gently over her, refreshing her, making
her over. Perhaps she had been happy before. But never like this—knowing
she was happy—sure that this exquisite, beautiful moment was all for
her.

They finished their lettuce sandwiches, and began on scones, with honey
and Cornish cream, and cup after cup of tea, steaming hot, smelling like
pressed rose leaves.

A deep sigh from her companion. She looked up. There was a queer twist
to his face, as if he were in pain.

‘What is it?’

‘I want to stay here forever,’ he said. ‘Here—just like this.’

‘And I—I too,’ she added.

The waitress had left them. They sat regarding each other, neither one
remembering to look away. He was pulling at the petals of the yellow
poppies between them. Those long brown fingers——She dropped her eyes.

‘Sitting here—you and I—I suddenly remembered—never before—the quiet.
After so many years. In the army they were always packing me off
somewhere. After Sandhurst it was China. Next Egypt.’ He held a poppy
petal between his fingers, crushing it, letting it fall. ‘I was married
the night before we started for Cairo. Three years of it. My children
were born there. I remember the trials of getting milk for them. Goat’s
milk it was. And they are great girls now. With their mother in
Brighton. Molly, the oldest, going to dancing parties.’ He rubbed his
hand over his eyes. ‘I’m glad they’re girls—she can manage them—see
that——’

Nora poured out a cup of cold tea and drank it in great gulps, the
sickening stuff nearly choking her. Of course, she ought to have known
that he was married. A man with his charm, his handsome face. As the
clergyman’s widow had said, all women——

‘Until the war I was a soldier who had never seen a battle. Just dress
parade. Always in some show or other. My wife liked the excitement—the
social responsibilities. She managed it all superbly. And then the war.
I was twenty-eight, a spoiled boy who had danced at Embassy balls, army
balls. At first I couldn’t take it all in. I went around in a funk, a
daze. I—a professional soldier. I was no good. And then—we all got
spinning around. Orders from on top. At last the front lines. A sporting
chance—facing danger every moment—your men such sporting chaps. Every
day we thought they’d get us. We didn’t care. Soon we wanted them to end
it for us. It’s like a nightmare. You keep thinking you’ll wake up. The
blood—quivering bodies—reeking—reeking—the shrieks—squeals. I never wake
up in the morning without seeing and hearing it all over
again—smelling—the horror of it.’

Nora swallowed a second nauseous gulp of tea. A few moments before, she
had been happy.

‘Nothing touched me. Not a scratch. On leave, constant excitement. It’s
the way of our family. They fancied it was what I needed. My wife and
I—those few days—she with colonels, generals—I carrying on with young
girls—bold, pretty things. I hated them back at the front.’

Nora’s tea-cup rattled against the saucer. She sat bent over the table,
her face shaded by her hands.

‘Men beside me—dying like pigs in a slaughtering pen. But I—up to the
end—I thought if I lived to see it over—I’d kill myself. Never another
war then. But they sent me to Mesopotamia afterward. We were quartered
in an old Turkish fort. Nothing to do. Just thinking. The men spent
their time with field glasses ready for a glimpse at a Turkish woman.
Any woman—when they saw her, they called to the others. All
that—thinking—it came to me—I knew I hated the army—hated it. And when I
remembered that young boys like me—brought up on family traditions
of the obligation to serve one’s country, to preserve the
Empire—killing—killing other men, I wanted to save them. All those days
with nothing to do—the idea tortured me that there was no one—no one but
me to tell them. Your serene years with time to give to books—I’ve
envied you that. I read when I could. Adventure tales—then history.
Thucydides over and over. But a soldier trying to write a book——’ He
shook his head. ‘He’s like a plough boy with a pen. But I began to write
for the cadets at Sandhurst. Four, six, eight hours a day. Sometimes
nothing would come. The men lolling around me—their interminable
stories—of women mostly. To escape I began writing again. Maybe I stuck
at it too close. At last they sent me home—home for a rest. I wanted to
finish my book. Only rest—complete rest they said. But how does one
rest?’

Nora lifted herself out of her chair. They must go back. They had a long
way to go. How could she drag herself over all those weary miles—up and
down—hour after hour? She was so tired. Every bone cried out against the
pull of that endless journey before they saw the tower of Reggie’s
church. But now that they knew the trail, they plodded along more
quickly than they had in the morning, though with all the elasticity,
the keen sense of discovery gone.

In the meadows, which a few hours before had been so cool and moist, the
hot sun beat down upon the little streams, which sent up steaming fumes.
In the shadow of the rocks the ewes lay, indifferent to the poor lambs
bleating and crying for the mothers they had lost. This way and that
they scampered, seeking here, there, behind a bush, a rock, stupidly
missing the place where their hot, bad-tempered mothers lay concealed.

Nora tried to help them. ‘Here, poor thing, here is your mother.’ But
they plunged away from her in fear. Like people—so stupid—just missing
what they most wanted. ‘They will never find their mothers,’ Nora cried
out to the silent man beside her. But he didn’t seem to hear her, as he
stamped along over the spongy earth. A deep flush had spread over his
face. Then he was very pale.

He fell on the ground close to the scanty shade of some stunted
evergreen trees.

‘Let’s rest a moment,’ he said in a queer, gasping voice.

She had a vivid memory of her mother during her last sickness. ‘You are
ill!’ she exclaimed in terror. She got a cup out of the picnic bag and
ran for some water from the stream they had passed. She spilled it over
his face, his hands. Small beads of perspiration stood out on his
forehead. Bending down over him, she rubbed his hands. A faint color
came back to his face, the sickly pallor retreated.

‘I—I’m sorry,’ he breathed, turning toward her. ‘I haven’t been well.
I——’

‘We walked too far.’

‘No—I’m all right now. Quite all right, I assure you.’

Her tears splashed down upon his hands, which she was rubbing between
hers.

‘I’m sorry—I—I couldn’t spare you—seeing—me. It’s my heart. I—I want to
tell you. I can’t last long, they say. I came down to Reggie. He knows.
A month—six months——’

She covered her face with her hands.

‘Oh, please,’ he begged. ‘It doesn’t matter, really. It’s been so
delightful to-day. If—a minute ago——’

‘I—I care,’ she wept, shaken with sobs.

‘Until a few days ago—living or dying—it didn’t matter,’ he confessed.
‘But now I—I want to live. Now when it’s too late. Living—it can be
sweet, enduring. I’ve found out. I want it——Darling, I——’

From his broken words she pieced together what he meant, what both of
them meant, as they clung to each other in the shade of the scrubby
pines around which the lost lambs still straggled with their
heart-breaking cries.

But it was no use. They must go back, he to Reggie, she to Adeline Pinks
and the rest of Mrs. Pentillie’s guests waiting in their best clothes in
the summer-house for the tinkle of the dinner bell. The tide was coming
up, the sun dipping lower and lower. They rose, and, after one last
embrace, went on slowly, heavily down the trail.


 V

Nora had one day more in Cornwall. A lady from Manchester was coming to
take her room as soon as she was gone. Nora and Neville met after dinner
on the black cliff glowering down upon Doscastle Strand.

‘Nora, can’t we, your last day, go back to Roswithiel?’

‘If you wish,’ she agreed feebly. In the depths of her present misery
what they did no longer mattered.

On the second expedition, the sun shone with the same beautiful yellow
gleam as the first time, but sun, blue sea, floating gulls, the flowers
under their feet—none of it existed for them. Nothing was real but their
wretchedness, their parting in the morning. As they ate their luncheon
in the cool tea-room, the water dripped off the mossy mill-wheel, the
little waitress hovered over them with the same flattering attentions as
before, but the food they tried to eat was tasteless.

As they followed the single street of the village joining a highway
